\title{Dahlen's fluid closure and its generalisation:\\
       hydrostatic fluid regions in a static\\
       elasto-gravitational body of any shape}
\author{AdGIA method notes}
\date{}

\begin{document}
\maketitle
\sloppy

\paragraph{The problem.} This note records one method for the static,
or secular, response of a self-gravitating elastic body that contains
hydrostatic fluid regions, the liquid outer core being the
geophysical case, together with the evidence that the method works.
Under a slowly applied load the fluid rearranges itself and settles,
after however long dissipation takes, into a new hydrostatic
equilibrium in the perturbed gravity field and inside the deformed
container: the \emph{secular state}. If the fluid is described by a
displacement field, as the solid is, the static problem has no
solution whenever the fluid is stratified: the stratification pins
each parcel to an equipotential, and the resulting normal
displacement generically disagrees with the wall's wherever the wall
is itself an equipotential. The starting point is Dahlen's (1974)
treatment of a fluid core. In it the fluid's displacement is
eliminated, and the fluid enters the solid's problem only through its
density and through terms on its boundary. On a spherical body the
elimination is exact at every spherical-harmonic degree $\ell \ge 1$.
At degree $0$ it leaves one function of radius undetermined. Off the
sphere it leaves one function of the background potential
undetermined at every degree. Spectral codes repair the first defect
by solving degree $0$ separately, with the fluid's displacement kept.
A three-dimensional finite-element model separates no degrees, and on
an aspherical body has no substitute for that repair.

\paragraph{The method.} The generalisation recorded here determines
the missing function on a fluid region of arbitrary shape. It changes
variables to the cumulative volume change $V(c)$ of the body of fluid
lying below each level surface $\{\Phi_0 = c\}$ of the background
potential. In that variable the missing function satisfies a regular
second-order ordinary differential equation, the Euler--Lagrange
equation of the fluid's own energy, with one condition at each end.
The resulting one-dimensional unknown couples to the
three-dimensional problem as a symmetric \emph{border}: a few rows
and columns appended to the finite-element system. One such unknown
is added per connected fluid region. On a spherical body the method
reduces to Dahlen's at every degree $\ell \ge 1$ and to the
compressible degree-0 problem at $\ell = 0$. Off the sphere it is, as
far as we know, the only static method for a fluid region whose
stratification is not neutral. The construction extends to solids
with arbitrary pre-stress, to rotating bodies, and to finite
amplitude.

\paragraph{Road map.} Part~I (\S\S\ref{sec:setting}--\ref{sec:background})
fixes the setting and the assumptions (A1)--(A5) and develops the
exact theory: the state of a fluid and its inventory, the energy and
hydrostatic balance, the level structure of the potential, the
equilibrium as ordinary differential equations on the Reeb graph in
the mass coordinate, the arrangement and the quantile function, the
variational form and its second variation, the fluid at the wall, and
the background's stratification parameter. Part~II
(\S\S\ref{sec:lin}--\ref{sec:var}) linearises: the lift defined
without a displacement, the three laws, the closure and its end
conditions for any wall, the closure on the tree of level components,
what the solid feels, the closure as the Euler--Lagrange equation of
the fluid's energy, the null-Lagrangian identity, the bordered weak
form, and existence and uniqueness. Part~III
(\S\S\ref{sec:disp}--\ref{sec:why}) treats the wall: which
displacements realise the state, transverse walls, the level-wall
sheet as a tangential transport, and why displacement formulations
fail. Part~IV (\S\S\ref{sec:dahlen}--\ref{sec:rotation}) specialises
and extends: the sphere and Dahlen's reduction, the fluid shell, the
referential solid and its wall seam, rotation. Part~V
(\S\S\ref{sec:implementation}--\ref{sec:standing}) records the
implementation as built, the measured verification and the predicted
gates, the older literature, and the standing assumptions and open
items.

\paragraph{Status tags.} Every section opens with a status line, and
individual claims carry tags. \emph{Derived} means a calculation
written out here. \emph{Argued} means a claim whose proof is sketched
but not completed. \emph{Cited} means a result taken from the
literature. \emph{Measured} means a numerical fact from this library.
\emph{Programme} means work outlined but not done. Statements about
the code say \emph{built} (in the library and tested), \emph{in
progress}, or \emph{specified} (designed, not built). Predictions of
the theory that no computation has yet tested are marked
\emph{untested}.

\paragraph{Notation.} $\varpi := \rho\,\vol$ is the
\emph{mass form}, and $m$
is the \emph{mass coordinate}, the mass of fluid below a level; $\mu$
is the inventory and $\Mcdf$ its cumulative form. The in-situ density
gradient along the levels is
$\rho' = \mathrm d\rho/\mathrm dc$, \code{density\_gradient} in the
code. Background and
perturbation fields carry subscripts, $p_0$, $p_1$, $\rho_1$, $S_1$.
Vectors are bold. $\Vol(c)$ is
the volume of fluid below a level and $V(c)$ the cumulative volume
change, which are different objects; $\bar h$ is called the level
\emph{integral} of $h$.

\paragraph{Other notes.} This note is self-contained: every
definition, weak form and measured number it relies on is stated or
displayed within it. The file references kept throughout are
provenance and onward reading, not prerequisites. Four other notes
remain the fuller treatments of their own subjects:
\code{doc/static\_fluid\_core.tex} develops the ill-posedness at $N^2
\ne 0$, the dissipative selection and the formulation hierarchy, with
the complete measurement record; \code{doc/quasi\_static\_models.tex}
the mixed class in general; \code{doc/gravitating\_elasticity.md} the
referential formulation; and \code{doc/generalised\_rotation.md} the
rotational theory.

%=====================================================================
\part{Setting and the exact theory}

\section{Setting, notation and assumptions}
\label{sec:setting}

\status{definitions.}

\paragraph{The body.} A body $B \subset \R^d$, with $d = 2$ or $3$,
consists of solid regions and fluid regions separated by interfaces.
The two-dimensional case, a gravitating disc, is the library's
testing geometry, and every statement below holds in both dimensions.
We describe one fluid region and its wall. Several fluid regions are
coupled to one another through the solid and the potential, but the
construction is made separately for each, one border per region. In
the exact theory (\S\ref{sec:nonlinear}) the fluid occupies
$\Omega_F$, the region enclosed by the deformed wall. In the linear
theory $F$ is the background fluid region and $\Sigma = \partial F$
its wall, the interface it shares with the solid. On $\Sigma$ the
unit normal $\bn$ points out of $F$, and the solid's outward normal is
$\nuhs = -\bn$. On a level wall $\nuhs = \mp\nuh$, with $\nuh$ the
level normal defined below: minus at a top wall such as a CMB, where
the solid lies above the fluid, and plus at an inner wall such as an
ICB, where it lies below. $\rho_F$ and $\rho_S$ are the fluid-side
and solid-side densities on $\Sigma$, and $G$ is the gravitational
constant.

\paragraph{The potential and the levels.} $\Phi$ is the total
geopotential, gravitational plus centrifugal for a rotating body
(\S\ref{sec:rotation}), and $\Phi_0$ its background value. The body is
in static equilibrium in $\Phi_0$, and in the fluid the equilibrium is
hydrostatic,
\begin{equation}
  \nabla p_0 = -\rho\,\nabla\Phi_0 \quad\text{in } F ,
  \label{eq:hydro}
\end{equation}
which is \eqref{eq:hydro1} of \S\ref{sec:firstvar} at the background.
We write $g := |\nabla\Phi_0|$ for the magnitude of the background
gravity and $\nuh := \nabla\Phi_0/g$ for the unit normal to the level
surfaces, pointing towards increasing potential, which is outwards on
a body near spherical. $\nabla_{\!\parallel}$ is the part of the
gradient tangent to the level surfaces. For a level value $c$,
\begin{equation*}
  L_c := \{\Phi_0 = c\}\cap F,
  \qquad
  R_c := \{\Phi_0 < c\}\cap F,
  \qquad
  \Sigma_c := \Sigma\cap\{\Phi_0 < c\}
\end{equation*}
are the level set, the region below it and the part of the wall below
it (Figure~\ref{fig:geometry}). $c_{\min}$ is the lowest level in $F$,
at a centre or on an inner wall, and $c_{\mathrm{top}}$ the highest. A
\emph{centre} is an interior minimum of $\Phi_0$ in $F$, where $g =
0$: a full core has one, a fluid shell none. The solid may be
anything: aspherical, pre-stressed, non-hydrostatic. What it must
satisfy at the wall is stated in \S\ref{sec:solid}.

\emph{Derivative convention.} A prime on a level function ($f$, $V$,
$U$, $A$, $\kappa$, $\rho$, \dots) denotes $\mathrm d/\mathrm dc$ along
the levels, equivalently $\mathrm d/\mathrm d\Phi_0$. The two
exceptions are stated where they occur: on the volume-of-mass map
$v(m)$ of \S\ref{sec:NL-variational} and the level-of-mass map $c(m)$
of \S\ref{sec:energy1d} a prime denotes $\mathrm d/\mathrm dm$. No
prime ever denotes $\mathrm d/\mathrm dr$; radial derivatives are
written $\partial_r$. The primes on the load Love numbers $h'_0$,
$k'_0$ of \S\ref{sec:dahlen} are the standard notation for load Love
numbers, not derivatives.

\caption{The level geometry, drawn in two dimensions. The fluid region
$F$ lies inside its wall $\Sigma$ (solid ellipse) and is foliated by
the level curves of $\Phi_0$ (dashed circles about the centre, a
minimum of $\Phi_0$). For one level value $c$ the figure shows the
level set $L_c$ (blue: the part of the circle $\{\Phi_0 = c\}$ inside
$F$), the region below it $R_c$ (shaded) and the part of the wall
below it $\Sigma_c$ (thick). The dots mark where the level set meets
the wall; in three dimensions these are curves. This wall is
\emph{transverse}. A \emph{level} wall would itself be one of the
level curves, and $\Sigma_c$ would then be empty for every $c$ below
the wall's level. $\nuh$ is the unit normal to the level curves, and
$\bn$ the wall's unit normal out of $F$.}
\label{fig:geometry}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Level and transverse walls.} A wall component is
\emph{level} where it is a level surface of $\Phi_0$. There $\bn =
\pm\nuh$: plus on the top wall of a region, minus on the inner wall of
a shell; $c_w$ denotes its level. The wall is \emph{transverse} where
it cuts the level surfaces, and a point where it touches a level
surface tangentially is a \emph{level point}. The two cases are not
symmetric. A closed wall can be level everywhere --- on a hydrostatic
equilibrium figure every density interface is, and such a wall has
no transverse point at all --- but it cannot be transverse
everywhere: $\Phi_0$ restricted to a closed wall attains its extrema,
the tangential gradient vanishes there, and each extremum is a level
point. An enclosed fluid is in equilibrium against a wall of any
shape, the solid supplying the traction; only a free surface must be
level. On a spherical body every density interface is level, and the
Earth's core--mantle boundary is level to about a part in a thousand.

\paragraph{The fluid.} The fluid is inviscid and supports no shear
stress. Each parcel carries a conserved scalar label $S$ (specific
entropy or composition), the equation of state is $\rho = \rho(p,
S)$, and $\kappa := \rho\,(\partial p/\partial\rho)_S$ is the adiabatic
bulk modulus. Its background profiles of density, pressure and bulk
modulus are $\rho(c)$, $p_0(c)$ and $\kappa(c)$, functions of the
level by \S\ref{sec:profiles}; $\rho'$ is the in-situ density gradient
along the levels, and $\rho_F$ the fluid density at a level wall. The
stratification parameter $D = \rho' + \rho^2/\kappa$ and the squared
buoyancy frequency $N^2 = -g^2D/\rho$ are introduced in
\S\ref{sec:background}; the closure below never divides by either,
and this is one of its main properties.

\paragraph{Perturbations.} The solid's displacement is $\bu_s$. $\phi$
is the Eulerian perturbation of the geopotential (the change at a
fixed spatial point), including both the applied potential and the
self-gravity of the perturbed body. $p_1$, $\rho_1$ and $S_1$ are the
Eulerian perturbations of pressure, density and label in the fluid. A
fluid displacement $\bu$ appears only in Part~III and in the
comparisons, as a device for asking which displacements reproduce the
state; it is never computed. For a background field $q^0$ with
Eulerian perturbation $q^1$,
\begin{equation*}
  \Delta q := q^1 + \bu\cdot\nabla q^0
\end{equation*}
is the first-order \emph{Lagrangian}, or material, perturbation: the
change following the particle. This is the convention of normal-mode
theory (Dahlen and Tromp 1998). The symbol collides with the
Laplacian, so we state the resolution: $\Delta$ never denotes the
Laplacian, which is always written $\nabla^2$. The two principal
instances are the Lagrangian pressure perturbation $\Delta p = p_1 +
\bu\cdot\nabla p_0$, which the constitutive law makes $-\kappa\Div\bu$,
and $\Delta\Phi = \phi + \bu\cdot\nabla\Phi_0$, the material increment
of the total potential at a particle. Mass conservation is $\rho_1 =
-\Div(\rho\bu)$. The linearised static momentum balance of the fluid
says that the Eulerian pressure gradient balances the perturbed
gravity acting on the background density and the background gravity
acting on the density perturbation:
\begin{equation}
  -\nabla p_1 - \rho\,\nabla\phi - \rho_1\,\nabla\Phi_0 = \mathbf 0 .
  \label{eq:momentum}
\end{equation}

\paragraph{The solid.} Two descriptions of the solid are used, and
both are implemented in the library.
\begin{itemize}
\item The \emph{mixed} description
  (\code{LinearQuasiStaticMixedSelfGravitatingProblem}) has a
  displacement on the equilibrium configuration and the Eulerian
  potential perturbation $\phi$. It assumes a reference state that is
  hydrostatic (the pre-stress is a pressure) and natural (the
  reference configuration is the equilibrium one).
\item The \emph{referential} description
  (\code{LinearQuasiStaticReferentialSelfGravitatingProblem}) has a
  referential displacement and a referential potential perturbation
  $\zeta$, the potential perturbation pulled back to a fixed reference
  body. It takes an arbitrary constitutive state $(\hat C, \Se,
  \varphi_e)$: $\hat C$ is the elastic tensor, $\Se$ the equilibrium
  stress, and $\varphi_e$ the equilibrium mapping from the reference
  body to the equilibrium configuration
  (\code{doc/gravitating\_elasticity.md} \S1 and \S3.1).
\end{itemize}
The mixed description is the special value $\Se = -p_0 I$, $\varphi_e
= \mathrm{id}$. Parts~I--III are written in the mixed description, and
\S\ref{sec:solid} lifts the restriction. (The potential perturbation
$\phi$ is written $\phi^1$ in the referential notes; $\varphi_e$, a
mapping, is unrelated to it.)

\paragraph{The formulations compared.} The library has other
treatments of the fluid, which serve as comparisons in
\S\S\ref{sec:why}, \ref{sec:dahlen} and \ref{sec:verification}. They
are described in \code{doc/static\_fluid\_core.tex}; in one line each:
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{welded}: the fluid is kept as an elastic material with
  vanishing shear modulus, and the displacement is continuous across
  $\Sigma$;
\item \emph{slipping}: the fluid's displacement is an independent
  field, and only its normal component is matched at $\Sigma$;
\item \emph{gauged}: the welded or slipping fluid, with a small penalty
  that selects one representative of the fluid's relabelling freedom;
\item \emph{Maxwell relaxation}: the fluid is given a Maxwell viscosity
  and relaxed in time to its long-time limit.
\end{itemize}
The first three are elastic-static. The last is a time-dependent
route to the secular state. The \emph{relabelling freedom} consists of
the rearrangements of the fluid's material labels that change no
physical field (\S\ref{sec:A4}). When $N^2 \ne 0$ ($D \ne 0$) they are
tangent to the level surfaces and mass-divergence-free (the
\emph{strict class} of that note). When $N^2 = 0$ ($D = 0$) they need
only preserve the mass distribution (\emph{class K}).

\paragraph{Level integrals.} For a function $h$ on the fluid region,
\begin{equation}
  A(c) = \oint_{L_c}\frac{\dS}{g},
  \qquad
  \bar h(c) = \oint_{L_c}\frac{h\dS}{g},
  \qquad
  \frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm dc}\int_{R_c}h\dV = \bar h(c)
  \quad\text{(coarea)} .
  \label{eq:coarea}
\end{equation}
$A$ is the volume per unit potential, $A = \Vol'$ with $\Vol(c) =
|R_c|$, and $\bar h$ is the \emph{level integral} of $h$; $\bar h/A$ is
the $1/g$-weighted mean of $h$ over $L_c$, and $\bar h$ vanishes
exactly when that mean does. Capital letters denote the level
integrals of the potential-like fields: $\bar\Phi := \bar\phi$, $\bar
X := \bar\chi$ for a potential test function $\chi$, and, in
\S\ref{sec:rotation}, $\bar\Psi_k := \bar\psi_k$. No level surface is
ever constructed in the implementation. Every level integral is
converted back to a volume integral over $F$ by \eqref{eq:coarea}
(\S\ref{sec:implementation}).

\paragraph{Geometric notation.} Part~I is written with differential
forms, and the rest of the note uses them where they shorten a
statement. $\vol$ is the Euclidean volume form and $\varpi := \rho\,\vol$
the mass form; integrals of top forms over a region are written
$\int_\Omega\alpha$, and a measure of finite mass on a line is
identified with its density when it has one. For a vector field
$\bw$, $\iota_{\bw}$ is the interior product and $\mathcal L_{\bw} =
\mathrm d\iota_{\bw} + \iota_{\bw}\mathrm d$ the Lie derivative
(Cartan's formula). On functions $\mathcal L_{\bw}f = \mathrm
df(\bw) = \bw\cdot\nabla f$, and on the volume form $\mathcal
L_{\bw}\vol = (\Div\bw)\,\vol$, so $\mathcal L_{\bw}\varpi =
\Div(\rho\bw)\,\vol$. Two identities recur: for a 1-form $\beta$ and a
top form $\alpha$, $\beta\wedge\iota_{\bw}\alpha = \beta(\bw)\,\alpha$;
and $\mathrm d$ kills top forms, so $\mathcal L_{\bw}\alpha = \mathrm
d(\iota_{\bw}\alpha)$ is exact, with $\int_\Omega\mathcal
L_{\bw}\alpha = \oint_{\partial\Omega}\iota_{\bw}\alpha$ and
$\iota_{\bw}\vol|_{\partial\Omega} = (\bw\cdot\bn)\dS$. A map $F:
\Omega \to Y$ \emph{pushes forward} a top form $\alpha$ of finite
mass to a measure $F_*\alpha$ on $Y$, defined by $\int_Y
h\,\mathrm d(F_*\alpha) = \int_\Omega(h\circ F)\,\alpha$ for every
test function $h$. Push-forward is functorial, $(G\circ F)_* =
G_*F_*$, and a diffeomorphism $\varphi$ acts by $\varphi_*\alpha =
(\varphi^{-1})^*\alpha$. Where $F: \Omega \to \R$ has $\mathrm dF \ne
0$, the push-forward has a density given by the \emph{Gelfand--Leray
residue} $\alpha/\mathrm dF$, the $(d-1)$-form with $\mathrm dF\wedge
(\alpha/\mathrm dF) = \alpha$, unique once restricted to a level set,
so that
\begin{equation*}
  F_*\alpha = \Bigl(\int_{\{F = c\}}\frac{\alpha}{\mathrm dF}\Bigr)\dc ,
  \qquad
  \frac{\vol}{\mathrm dF}\Big|_{\{F = c\}} = \frac{\dS}{|\nabla F|} .
\end{equation*}
The second formula is the coarea formula, and it is the only place the
Euclidean metric enters the level structure. The level integrals of
\eqref{eq:coarea} are exactly these residue integrals, $\bar h(c)\dc =
\Phi_{0*}(h\,\vol)$.

\paragraph{Assumptions.} These hold throughout unless a section says
otherwise.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(A\arabic*)]
\item \emph{Constitution.} One conserved label; $\rho = \rho(p, S)$
  with $\partial_S\rho \ne 0$, hence of one sign, over the range of
  $(p, S)$ occurring in the fluid; $\kappa > 0$.
\item \emph{Levels.}
  \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
  \item In the closed background fluid region $\Phi_0$ has no
    critical points, except possibly a centre.
  \item In the background, each level set in the fluid is connected.
    This holds for a level wall and for a transverse wall without
    pockets; where it fails, the closure is stated on the tree of level
    components (\S\ref{sec:tree}) and nothing else changes. After
    perturbation it is required only below the band of top levels that
    a deformed level wall spans (\S\ref{sec:fluidatwall}); above it the
    levels are generically disconnected, and harmlessly so.
  \end{enumerate}
\item \emph{Walls.} Each wall component is smooth and either level or
  transverse, with isolated, nondegenerate level points in the
  transverse case.
\item \emph{Arrangement.} The perturbed equilibrium keeps the
  background's ordering of the fluid along the mass coordinate
  (\S\ref{sec:A4}).
\item \emph{Regularity.} All data are smooth enough for the expansions
  used.
\end{enumerate}
On (A2): inside matter $\Phi_0$ is subharmonic, $\nabla^2\Phi_0 = 4\pi
G\rho > 0$ (or $4\pi G\rho - 2\Omega^2$ with rotation), so it has no
interior maximum, and the foliation can degenerate only through
centres and the saddles between them. What is known about the level
structure --- Sard's theorem, Morse theory, the Reeb graph, and the
theory of convex level sets --- is collected in \S\ref{sec:foliation};
(A2a) is the assumption (L1) there and (A3) its companion (L2). If
(A2b) fails in the background, the formalism lives on the Reeb graph
of $\Phi_0$, with one profile per branch, the pressure continuous
where branches merge and masses adding there: \S\ref{sec:exactgraph}
for the exact theory, \S\ref{sec:tree} for the closure. Walls with
level patches, and two independent labels, are discussed where they
arise but are outside the assumptions.

$\mu = S_*\varpi$, $\Mcdf$ & inventory (mass--label measure); its cumulative form & \ref{sec:inventory} \\
$\mathcal R$, $\pi$, $e$, $v$, $T_e$ & Reeb graph, quotient map, an edge, a vertex, the tube of an edge & \ref{sec:foliation} \\
$\Vol(c)$, $M(c)$; $\hat p$, $\hat\rho$, $\hat S$ & volume and mass below level $c$; pressure, density, label profiles & \ref{sec:exactgraph} \\
$m$, $\Lambda(m)$, $m_e$ & mass coordinate; label as a function of it (the arrangement); pocket mass & \ref{sec:exactgraph} \\
$D$, $N^2$ & stratification parameter $\rho' + \rho^2/\kappa$; buoyancy frequency, $N^2 = -g^2D/\rho$ & \ref{sec:background} \\
$f$, $\delta M$, $s$ & perturbations of $\hat p$ and $M$ at fixed level; the lift $s = \delta M/(\rho A)$ & \ref{sec:lift} \\
$\delta\Vol$, $S_c[\bu_s]$, $S[\bu_s]$ & change of $\Vol$; volume swept by the wall below $c$; by the whole wall & \ref{sec:levelgeom} \\
$V(c)$, $U(c)$ & volume change of the fluid below level $c$, $V = \delta\Vol - sA$; flux through $L_c$, $U = V - S_c$ & \ref{sec:threelaws} \\
$\Vt_e$, $\sigma_e$ & the closure's unknown on an edge of the tree; material lift & \ref{sec:tree} \\
$J[V]$, $\Ltot$, $\lambda$ & the fluid's quadratic functional; the bordered functional; wall multiplier & \ref{sec:var} \\
$\hat s$, $\bu^0$, $a$, $\sigma$, $\bq$, $\Gamma$ & pointwise lift of a displacement; realising field; mismatch; sheet; transport; reference equipotential & \ref{sec:disp} \\
$\bu_s$; $\bu$, $u_\nu$ & solid displacement; fluid displacement (a device, never computed), $u_\nu = \bu\cdot\nuh$ & \ref{sec:setting} \\
$\phi$, $p_1$, $\rho_1$, $S_1$ & Eulerian perturbations of potential, pressure, density, label & \ref{sec:setting} \\
$\zeta$; $(\hat C, \Se, \varphi_e)$ & referential potential perturbation; referential constitutive state & \ref{sec:setting}, \ref{sec:solid} \\

\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

%=====================================================================
\section{The exact secular state}
\label{sec:nonlinear}
\label{sec:exact}

\status{derived from (A1)--(A5), except where marked: the smooth
converse (Proposition~\ref{prop:moser}) and the energy-minimisation
statement of \S\ref{sec:arrangement} are argued, the level-structure
theorems of \S\ref{sec:foliation} are cited, and the fluid at the wall
(\S\ref{sec:fluidatwall}) and the nonlinear computation
(\S\ref{sec:NL-compute}) are argued and programme respectively.}

This section treats the finite-amplitude equilibrium of a stratified,
compressible fluid region inside a self-gravitating body, the secular
state, with no linearisation. Nothing here depends on the solid's
constitution or on the linear theory; the solid enters only through
the shape of the container and the potential. The closure of Part~II
is the linearisation of this theory, in which the fluid has no
displacement at any order. The exact theory is the cleaner statement,
and it explains the features of the closure that look accidental in
the linear setting: why the sign of $D$ never enters the equation,
and why a level wall needs more care than a transverse one. In this
section $\Phi$ is the current geopotential and $\Omega_F$ the current
fluid region; $L_c$, $R_c$, $\Vol$, $M$ and $A$ refer to them.

\paragraph{The claims, in one paragraph.} A fluid has no reference
configuration, so its state is a mass form and a label field, and
what its history fixes is the push-forward of the one by the other,
the inventory. Stationarity of the energy at fixed inventory is
hydrostatic balance. Hydrostatic balance makes pressure, density and
label constant on each connected piece of each level set of the
geopotential, so the whole state factors through the Reeb graph of
the geopotential, which for a near-spherical body is a tree, and is an
interval when the levels are connected. Pushing the volume and mass
forms down to the graph reduces the equilibrium to a pair of
first-order ordinary differential equations on each edge, coupled at
the vertices by continuity of pressure and additivity of mass, with
the fluid filling its container as the end condition. The one datum
these equations need beyond the geometry is the arrangement: which
adiabat each mass shell lies on. On an interval, stability and the
inventory fix it uniquely up to interfaces; on a tree, they fix it up
to the division of the inventory among the pockets.

\subsection{The state of a fluid}
\label{sec:state}

A solid has a reference configuration, and its displacement from it
is physically meaningful. A fluid has no such reference: exchange two
parcels carrying the same label, and nothing observable changes. The
physical state of the fluid is therefore the pair
\begin{equation*}
  (\varpi, S),\qquad \varpi := \rho\,\vol ,
\end{equation*}
a positive top form and a function on the region $\Omega_F$ the fluid
occupies. It is not a map from some earlier configuration, and no
displacement field appears in this section. Two things restrict which
pairs are possible. The first is geometric: the fluid fills its
container, so $\Omega_F$ is the region enclosed by the deformed wall.
This is the finite-amplitude form of no-penetration, and the only
place the wall enters the description of the fluid. The second is
material: the fluid must be made of the right stuff, in the right
amounts. It is the subject of the next four subsections.

\subsection{The inventory}
\label{sec:inventory}
\label{sec:NL-state}

Suppose we know nothing about where the fluid is, only what it is made
of. Since each parcel keeps its label and its mass, the most we can
know is, for each range of labels, how much mass carries labels in
that range. This is the \emph{inventory}, the push-forward of the mass
form by the label:
\begin{equation}
  \mu := S_*\varpi,\qquad\text{that is}\qquad
  \int_{\R}h\,\mathrm d\mu = \int_{\Omega_F}h(S)\,\varpi
  \quad\text{for every test function $h$ of the label.}
  \label{eq:mu}
\end{equation}
It is a histogram of the labels weighted by mass. Its value on a set
$E$ of labels is $\mu(E) = \int_{\{S\in E\}}\rho\dV$; its cumulative
form is $\Mcdf(S) := \mu((-\infty, S])$, the mass carrying a label at
most $S$; and $h \equiv 1$ gives the total mass $M_F$.

\paragraph{What ``prescribed'' means.} To say that $\mu$ is prescribed
is to impose \eqref{eq:mu}, for every $h$, as a constraint on the
state. The constraint is infinite-dimensional, one scalar condition
per test function, but it is entirely about amounts. It does not say
where any label sits, and it does not fix the density: the fluid is
compressible, and a given mass of a given label occupies more or less
volume as its pressure changes. The admissible states are all pairs
$(\varpi, S)$ with $\varpi$ a positive top form on the region enclosed
by the wall and $S_*\varpi = \mu$. Three examples show the range.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{A well-mixed fluid.} One label $S_*$: $\mu = M_F\,
  \delta_{S_*}$, and the only constraint is the total mass. This is
  the neutral case of \S\ref{sec:background}.
\item \emph{Two immiscible fluids.} Labels $S_a < S_b$ with masses
  $M_a$, $M_b$: $\mu = M_a\delta_{S_a} + M_b\delta_{S_b}$. The
  constraint fixes the mass of each fluid; any partition of the
  container, at any densities giving those masses, is admissible.
\item \emph{Continuous stratification.} $\Mcdf$ smooth and strictly
  increasing: $\mu$ has density $\Mcdf'(S)\,\mathrm dS$, the mass per
  unit label.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Labels are a gauge.} Replacing the label by any strictly
increasing function of it, $S \mapsto \gamma(S)$, changes $\mu$ to
$\gamma_*\mu$ and changes nothing physical, provided the equation of
state is rewritten accordingly. What is physical is the family of
\emph{adiabats}: for each parcel, the function $p \mapsto \rho(p, S)$
at its own label. This remark matters in \S\ref{sec:arrangement}.

\paragraph{The rearrangement class.} Two states in the same container
with the same inventory are rearrangements of one another
(\S\ref{sec:allconserved}), so the physical state space of the fluid,
the quotient of its configurations by the relabelling symmetry, is the
\emph{rearrangement class} of $\mu$. This is the setting of Burton
(1987), and of Benjamin (1976), for steady flows as energy extremisers
over rearrangements, with the mass measure in place of the volume
measure because the fluid is compressible. In the Lagrangian picture a
motion $\gamma$ of a reference fluid body, with reference labels $S_0$
and density $\rho_0$, enters the energy only through
$(\rho_0/J_\gamma)\circ\gamma^{-1}$ and $S_0\circ\gamma^{-1}$, so two
motions that differ by a mass- and label-preserving map of the
reference body are the same state.

\subsection{Why the inventory is conserved}

A motion of the fluid is a flow $\varphi_t$ of diffeomorphisms of the
container (or of the fluid's own region, if the container moves). Mass
and labels are carried:
\begin{equation*}
  \varpi_t = (\varphi_t)_*\varpi_0,\qquad S_t = S_0\circ\varphi_t^{-1} .
\end{equation*}
By functoriality,
\begin{equation}
  \mu_t = (S_t)_*\varpi_t
  = (S_0\circ\varphi_t^{-1})_*(\varphi_t)_*\varpi_0
  = (S_0)_*(\varphi_t^{-1})_*(\varphi_t)_*\varpi_0
  = (S_0)_*\varpi_0 = \mu_0 .
  \label{eq:functorial}
\end{equation}
Any motion, however large or violent, leaves the inventory unchanged,
provided each parcel keeps its label. In coordinates this is the
change of variables $\int h(S)\rho\dV = \int h(S_0)\rho_0\,\mathrm
dV_0$. The infinitesimal version is worth having. With $\bw$ the
velocity of the flow, the transport laws are
\begin{equation}
  \partial_t\varpi + \mathcal L_{\bw}\varpi = 0,
  \qquad
  \partial_tS + \mathcal L_{\bw}S = 0 ,
  \label{eq:transport}
\end{equation}
the continuity equation and label conservation. Since $\mathcal
L_{\bw}$ is a derivation, every form $h(S)\varpi$ is transported,
\begin{equation*}
  \partial_t\bigl(h(S)\varpi\bigr)
  = -h'(S)(\mathcal L_{\bw}S)\,\varpi - h(S)\,\mathcal L_{\bw}\varpi
  = -\mathcal L_{\bw}\bigl(h(S)\varpi\bigr)
  = -\mathrm d\bigl(\iota_{\bw}h(S)\varpi\bigr),
\end{equation*}
the last step by Cartan's formula. Over a fixed region the change of
$\int h(S)\varpi$ is the boundary flux $-\oint h(S)\rho\,\bw\cdot\bn\dS$
of mass carrying labels, and that cancels against the motion of the
region in the transport theorem $\frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm
dt}\int_{\Omega_t}\alpha = \int_{\Omega_t}(\partial_t\alpha +
\mathcal L_{\bw}\alpha)$. In the Hamiltonian description of the
compressible fluid the functionals $\int h(S)\varpi$ are the Casimirs
of the Lie--Poisson bracket; that the equilibria below are critical
points of the energy at fixed inventory is the energy--Casimir method.

\subsection{It is all that is conserved}
\label{sec:allconserved}

Conversely, two states in the same container with the same inventory
can be carried into each other by a map that preserves mass and
labels. This holds in the measure-theoretic sense: the map may have to
cut and reassemble the fluid, for instance to exchange two distant
parcels. A slow dissipative evolution can do that over long times; a
smooth displacement field of small amplitude cannot, which is already
a hint of \S\ref{sec:levelwalls}. The smooth version is Moser's
theorem and its fibred form, and it is worth seeing exactly what it
needs.

\begin{proposition}[fibred Moser; argued]
\label{prop:moser}
Let $(\varpi_0, S_0)$ and $(\varpi_1, S_1)$ be smooth states on
$\bar\Omega_F$ with the same inventory, with $\mathrm dS_0, \mathrm
dS_1 \ne 0$, and suppose that for each label value $s$ the surfaces
$\{S_0 = s\}$ and $\{S_1 = s\}$ are diffeomorphic, compatibly in $s$.
Then there is a diffeomorphism $\psi$ of $\bar\Omega_F$ with
$S_1\circ\psi = S_0$ and $\psi^*\varpi_1 = \varpi_0$.
\end{proposition}

\emph{Sketch.} Moser's theorem: two positive top forms on a compact
connected manifold with equal total integral are related by a
diffeomorphism isotopic to the identity, constructed by flowing along
a vector field that solves a cohomological equation. Here, first
choose $\psi_0$ matching the label surfaces, $S_1\circ\psi_0 = S_0$;
then $\varpi_0$ and $\psi_0^*\varpi_1$ have the same push-forward
$\mu$ under $S_0$, so their residues $\varpi/\mathrm dS_0$ have equal
integrals on each label surface. Apply Moser's theorem on each
surface, with $s$ as a parameter; the flow construction depends
smoothly on parameters, which gives a fibre-preserving correction
$\chi$, and $\psi = \psi_0\circ\chi$. \hfill$\square$

What equal inventories supply is the fibre masses. What they do not
supply is the topology of the label surfaces: whether, at a given
label, the fluid carrying it sits in one piece or in several. That is
precisely why the converse is only measure-theoretic in general, and
it is the geometric form of the arrangement datum met in
\S\ref{sec:arrangement} and of the pockets of \S\ref{sec:tree}, where
surfaces of equal mass sit in different components.

\subsection{Which processes preserve it}

Advection by any flow, including slow viscous creep, preserves $\mu$.
Diffusion of the label replaces the second of \eqref{eq:transport} by
$\partial_tS + \mathcal L_{\bw}S = \kappa_S\nabla^2S$ and does not.
The secular state as defined here takes the inventory as fixed over
the loading time. Whether a relaxation driven by label diffusion
reaches the same state is a separate question.
Whether label diffusion changes the inventory at first order near the
wall, on which the equivalence of the diffusive completion with the
advective one depends, is open (\S\ref{sec:standing}).

\subsection{One label is a restriction}

With two conserved labels, $\rho = \rho(p, S, C)$, hydrostatic balance
(\S\ref{sec:NL-energy}) still makes $\rho$ and $p$ level functions, but
only the combination $\rho(\hat p(c), S, C)$ is constrained: on each
level, $(S, C)$ may wander along an iso-density curve in label space.
Moving parcels along a level, preserving their volume, preserves
$\rho$, $p$ and the specific energy pointwise, so these motions are
exact symmetries and the equilibria carry an infinite-dimensional
degeneracy beyond the one-label case. The inventory, now a measure on
a plane of labels, fixes amounts but not the arrangement on each
level. For the response this matters because $\kappa$ then varies
along the levels, so the perturbed density is no longer a level
function and the reduction to profiles fails. The physically relevant
instance is a density-compensated thermal and compositional
structure. Assumption (A1) excludes it, and nothing in this note
applies to it.

With one label, by contrast, $\kappa$ is constant on the levels: with
$\partial_S\rho \ne 0$, level-constant $\rho$ and $p$ force
level-constant $S$, and hence level-constant $\kappa$.

\subsection{Energy, and hydrostatic balance}
\label{sec:NL-energy}

\paragraph{The energy.} The fluid's thermodynamics enters through its
specific internal energy $e(\rho, S)$. Write
\begin{equation}
  p := \rho^2\,\partial_\rho e,\qquad
  T := \partial_S e,\qquad
  h := e + \frac p\rho,\qquad
  \kappa := \rho\Bigl(\frac{\partial p}{\partial\rho}\Bigr)_S ,
  \label{eq:thermo}
\end{equation}
the pressure, the variable conjugate to the label (the temperature
when $S$ is specific entropy), the specific enthalpy and the bulk
modulus. In terms of the specific volume $\tau = 1/\rho$,
$\mathrm de = -p\,\mathrm d\tau + T\,\mathrm dS$: the pressure is
minus the derivative of the energy with respect to volume at fixed
label, which is how it will appear below. Adding $\mathrm d(p/\rho)$
gives
\begin{equation}
  \mathrm dh = T\,\mathrm dS + \frac{\mathrm dp}{\rho} .
  \label{eq:dh}
\end{equation}
Inverting $p(\rho, S)$ at fixed $S$ gives the equation of state $\rho
= \rho(p, S)$ of (A1), with $(\partial\rho/\partial p)_S =
\rho/\kappa$. The energy of the whole body is
\begin{equation}
  E = E_{\mathrm{solid}} + \int_{\Omega_F}e(\rho, S)\,\varpi
  - \frac{1}{8\pi G}\int_{\R^d}\mathrm d\Phi_g\wedge\star\,\mathrm d\Phi_g
  + \int\Phi_c\,\varpi_{\mathrm{tot}} :
  \label{eq:energy}
\end{equation}
the solid's stored energy, which depends only on the solid's motion;
the fluid's internal energy, $\int\rho e\dV$; the gravitational
self-energy of the whole body, $-\tfrac{1}{8\pi G}\int|\nabla\Phi_g|^2
\dV$; and, for a body held at fixed angular velocity $\bOmega$, the
centrifugal term with $\Phi_c := -\tfrac12|\bOmega\times\bx|^2$. Here
$\varpi_{\mathrm{tot}}$ is the mass form of solid and fluid together,
$\Phi_g$ solves $\nabla^2\Phi_g = 4\pi G\rho_{\mathrm{tot}}$ and decays
at infinity, and $\Phi = \Phi_g + \Phi_c$. (Convention: $\Phi_g \le 0$,
gravity is $-\nabla\Phi$, and $\nabla^2\Phi_g = +4\pi G\rho$. Some
geophysical texts use the opposite sign for the potential.) With
$\bOmega$ dynamical, the last term is replaced by the rotational
Routhian of Maitra and Al-Attar (2024); nothing in the fluid changes.

\paragraph{Admissible variations.} Move each parcel from $\bx$ to $\bx
+ \epsilon\bxi(\bx)$, keeping its mass and label, and write $\delta$
for the first-order change at a fixed point. This is
\eqref{eq:transport} with $\bw = \bxi$:
\begin{equation}
  \delta\varpi = -\mathcal L_{\bxi}\varpi = -\mathrm d(\iota_{\bxi}\varpi),
  \qquad
  \delta S = -\mathcal L_{\bxi}S = -\bxi\cdot\nabla S ,
  \label{eq:admissible}
\end{equation}
in components $\delta\rho = -\Div(\rho\bxi)$. Every such variation
preserves the inventory. Geometrically, the admissible states form the
orbit of the state under the diffeomorphisms of the container acting
by push-forward on $\varpi$ and by composition on $S$, together with
the variations of the container itself, and \eqref{eq:admissible} is
the tangent space of that orbit. The container constrains $\bxi$ only
at the wall: $\bxi\cdot\bn$ must equal $\bv\cdot\bn$, where $\bv$ is
the variation of the solid. Two kinds of variation are used: the fluid
alone, $\bv = 0$; and fluid and solid together. The field $\bxi$
generates variations; it is not a state variable.

\paragraph{The first variation.}
\label{sec:firstvar}
\emph{Internal energy.} With $\delta e = \partial_\rho e\,\delta\rho +
\partial_S e\,\delta S$, $\delta\rho = -\mathcal L_{\bxi}\rho -
\rho\Div\bxi$, $\rho^2\partial_\rho e = p$ and $\mathcal
L_{\bxi}\vol = (\Div\bxi)\,\vol$, a short rearrangement gives
\begin{equation}
  \delta(e\,\varpi) = -\mathcal L_{\bxi}(e\,\varpi) - p\,\mathcal L_{\bxi}\vol .
  \label{eq:deltaem}
\end{equation}
The energy form is transported with the parcels, except for the work
the pressure does against the change of the volume form: this is
$\mathrm de = -p\,\mathrm d\tau$ read on forms, and it is the only
place the thermodynamics enters. Integrate over the fluid, whose
boundary moves by $\bxi\cdot\bn$, so that the transport theorem adds
$\oint e\,\iota_{\bxi}\varpi$; the first term of \eqref{eq:deltaem} is
exact and cancels that addition. The second gives, with Cartan's
formula and $\mathrm dp\wedge\iota_{\bxi}\vol = \mathrm
dp(\bxi)\,\vol$,
\begin{equation}
  \delta\!\int_{\Omega_F}\!e\,\varpi
  = -\int_{\Omega_F}p\,\mathrm d(\iota_{\bxi}\vol)
  = \int_{\Omega_F}\mathrm dp(\bxi)\,\vol - \oint_\Sigma p\,\iota_{\bxi}\vol
  = \int_{\Omega_F}\bxi\cdot\nabla p\dV - \oint_\Sigma p\;\bxi\cdot\bn\dS .
  \label{eq:dEint}
\end{equation}
(The vector-calculus route uses $\partial_\rho(\rho e) = h$ and
\eqref{eq:dh}: $\int(-h\Div(\rho\bxi) - \rho T\bxi\cdot\nabla S) =
\int\bxi\cdot(\rho\nabla h - \rho T\nabla S) - \oint\rho h\,\bxi\cdot
\bn$, with $\rho\nabla h - \rho T\nabla S = \nabla p$ and $\rho h -
\rho e = p$.) The thermodynamics has collapsed into the pressure.

\emph{Gravity.} Varying the self-energy, integrating by parts over all
space and using Poisson's equation gives $\int\Phi_g\,\delta
\varpi_{\mathrm{tot}}$; the centrifugal term gives $\int\Phi_c\,\delta
\varpi_{\mathrm{tot}}$. Extend $\varpi$ by zero outside the fluid and
read \eqref{eq:admissible} distributionally, so that $\delta\varpi$
includes the surface mass of the fluid occupying the region the wall
has left; then
\begin{equation*}
  \int\Phi\,\delta\varpi = -\int\Phi\,\mathrm d(\iota_{\bxi}\varpi)
  = \int_{\Omega_F}\mathrm d\Phi\wedge\iota_{\bxi}\varpi
  = \int_{\Omega_F}\mathrm d\Phi(\bxi)\,\varpi ,
\end{equation*}
with no boundary term. The solid's part depends on $\bv$ alone.

\emph{Total.} With $\bxi\cdot\bn = \bv\cdot\bn$ on the wall,
\begin{equation}
  \delta E = \int_{\Omega_F}\bigl(\mathrm dp + \rho\,\mathrm d\Phi\bigr)(\bxi)\,\vol
  - \oint_\Sigma p\,\bv\cdot\bn\dS + (\text{terms in } \bv \text{ alone}) .
  \label{eq:firstvar}
\end{equation}
Varying the fluid alone, $\bxi$ is any smooth field tangent to the
wall, and stationarity is the 1-form equation
\begin{equation}
  \mathrm dp + \rho\,\mathrm d\Phi = 0 \quad\text{in } \Omega_F ,
  \label{eq:hydro1}
\end{equation}
hydrostatic balance $\nabla p = -\rho\nabla\Phi$. Varying fluid and
solid together, the fluid then enters the solid's virtual work only
through $-\oint p\,\bv\cdot\bn\dS$, besides its gravity: it pushes on
the wall with force $p\,\bn$ per unit area, that is, it exerts the
traction $-p\,\nuhs$ on the solid. Nothing of $\bxi$ survives,
consistent with $\bxi$ not being a state variable.

The equilibrium is a critical point of $E$ on the admissible set. The
admissible set contains the overturning motions --- exchanging two
parcels of different label is a rearrangement --- so the critical point
is a saddle wherever the stratification is unstable ($N^2 < 0$,
$D > 0$, \S\ref{sec:background}). For $D \le 0$ the fluid's own
quadratic form is non-negative (\S\ref{sec:uniqueness}); whether the
critical point is then a minimum of the whole is the gravitational
stability of the body, which the fluid alone does not settle.

\subsection{The level structure of the potential}
\label{sec:foliation}

\status{cited for the theorems, derived for their application.}

Hydrostatic balance is a statement about the level sets of $\Phi$, so
before using it we need to know what those level sets look like. This
subsection collects what three classical results say --- Sard's
theorem, Morse theory, and the Reeb graph construction --- and what is
known about convexity of the levels. Everything here is about the
single function $\Phi$ on the closed region $\bar\Omega_F$ with
boundary $\Sigma$.

\paragraph{Regular and critical levels.} A point is \emph{critical}
for $\Phi$ if $\mathrm d\Phi = 0$ there; a level $c$ is a critical
value if $L_c$ contains an interior critical point. On the wall there
is a second notion: a point of $\Sigma$ is critical for $\Phi|_\Sigma$
if the tangential gradient $\gradS\Phi$ vanishes there, that is if the
level surface is tangent to the wall; its value is a
\emph{wall-critical value}. A level $c$ is \emph{regular} if it is
neither. At a regular level, $L_c$ is a smooth hypersurface in the
interior and meets the wall transversally, so it is a smooth manifold
with boundary $L_c\cap\Sigma$, and the residue $\vol/\mathrm d\Phi$ is
smooth on it.

\begin{proposition}[Sard; cited]
\label{prop:sard}
The critical values of a smooth function on a manifold, with or
without boundary, form a set of Lebesgue measure zero in $\R$.
\end{proposition}

Applied to $\Phi$ on $\Omega_F$ and to $\Phi|_\Sigma$ on $\Sigma$,
this says that \emph{almost every level is regular}. That is exactly
what the push-forwards of \S\ref{sec:exactgraph} need: $\Phi_*\vol$ and
$\Phi_*\varpi$ have smooth densities $A(c)$ and $\hat\rho A$ on an
open set of full measure, and the one-dimensional equations below hold
almost everywhere in $c$. It is also all that Sard says: a statement
about the regularity of level sets, not about their topology. A
regular level set may have any number of components.

\paragraph{Where the topology changes.} What controls the topology is
Morse theory. Call $\Phi$ \emph{Morse on} $\bar\Omega_F$ if its
interior critical points are nondegenerate (non-singular Hessian), the
critical points of $\Phi|_\Sigma$ are nondegenerate as critical points
of a function on $\Sigma$, no interior critical point lies on
$\Sigma$, and the critical values are distinct.

\begin{proposition}[isotopy of level sets; cited]
\label{prop:isotopy}
Let $\Phi$ be smooth on the compact $\bar\Omega_F$, and let $[a, b]$
contain no critical value of $\Phi$ and no wall-critical value. Then
$\Phi^{-1}([a, b])$ is diffeomorphic to $L_a\times[a, b]$ by a
diffeomorphism carrying levels to levels. In particular all the level
sets $L_c$, $a \le c \le b$, are diffeomorphic manifolds with
boundary, and the number of their connected components is constant on
$[a, b]$.
\end{proposition}

\emph{Sketch.} The absence of wall-critical values means $\gradS\Phi
\ne 0$ on $\Sigma\cap\Phi^{-1}[a, b]$, so there is a smooth vector
field $X$ on $\Phi^{-1}[a, b]$ with $\mathrm d\Phi(X) = 1$ that is
tangent to the wall: take $\nabla\Phi/|\nabla\Phi|^2$ in the interior
and $\gradS\Phi/|\gradS\Phi|^2$ on the wall, blended by a partition of
unity. Its flow for time $c - a$ carries $L_a$ to $L_c$ and stays in
$\bar\Omega_F$. \hfill$\square$

So the topology of the level sets, and with it their number of
components, can change only at the critical values of $\Phi$ and of
$\Phi|_\Sigma$. If $\Phi$ is Morse on $\bar\Omega_F$, these values are
finitely many, and at each one the change is a standard local
surgery: at an interior critical point of index $k$ the sublevel set
gains a $k$-cell; at a wall-critical point the change depends on the
index of $\Phi|_\Sigma$ there and on whether $\nabla\Phi$ points out of
or into the fluid. (For the closed case see Milnor's \emph{Morse
Theory}; for manifolds with boundary, for example, Borodzik, N\'emethi
and Ranicki; both to check.) Whether a given surgery changes the
\emph{number} of components is not a local question; it is read off
the Reeb graph.

\begin{remark}[genericity]
\label{rem:genericity}
Morse functions are open and dense among smooth functions, so a
``generic'' potential is Morse on $\bar\Omega_F$ and the structure just
described is the rule. Genericity cuts both ways here. The physically
central case of a hydrostatic figure, in which the wall is itself a
level surface, is as far from Morse as possible: $\Phi|_\Sigma$ is
constant, the whole wall is critical, and the level set at $c_w$ is
$\Sigma$ itself. It is a Morse--Bott situation, nondegenerate in the
normal direction because $\partial_n\Phi \ne 0$. Any small deformation
of the wall or of the potential breaks it into a Morse configuration
with many wall-critical points in a thin band of levels, and that is
the band of \S\ref{sec:fluidatwall}.
\end{remark}

\paragraph{What the physics says about critical points.} Inside
matter, $\nabla^2\Phi = 4\pi G\rho - 2\Omega^2$, which is positive for
any planet ($4\pi G\rho \gg \Omega^2$); this is the only place rotation
enters the level structure. So $\Phi$ is strictly subharmonic in the
fluid and has no interior local maximum, by the maximum principle; its
interior minima and saddles are not excluded by this alone. For a
near-spherical body the levels of $\Phi$ inside the fluid are nested,
near-spherical surfaces, with a single minimum at the centre if the
fluid reaches it and none otherwise, and no saddles. This note
assumes:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(L\arabic*)]
\item $\Phi$ has no interior critical points in $\bar\Omega_F$ except
  possibly one minimum, a centre;
\item $\Phi|_\Sigma$ is either constant on a component of the wall (a
  \emph{level wall}) or Morse on it (a \emph{transverse wall}).
\end{enumerate}
(L1) is (A2a) and (L2) is (A3). Under (L1) every change of level-set
topology, other than at a centre, happens at the wall. This is the key
simplification: the Reeb graph of the fluid is determined by the
wall's shape relative to the levels, not by anything in the interior.

\paragraph{What is known about convexity of the levels.} For the
background region $F$ and the background potential $\Phi_0$, the
theory of convex level sets adds the following. The implementation
checks (L1) rather than assuming it (\S\ref{sec:implementation}).
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{The hydrostatic condition upgrades the maximum principle
  (derived).} With $\rho = \rho(\Phi_0)$, the restriction $\Phi_0|_F$
  solves the semilinear equation $\nabla^2\Phi_0 = 4\pi
  G\rho(\Phi_0)$ in $F$. On a level wall the boundary datum is the
  constant $c_w$, so $\Phi_0|_F$ solves a Dirichlet problem on $F$
  alone. With the torsion-type function $\tau = c_w - \Phi_0 > 0$ this
  reads $\nabla^2\tau + 4\pi G\rho = 0$, $\tau = 0$ on $\Sigma$, a
  torsion-type problem, to which the theory of convexity of level sets
  applies (Kawohl 1985).
\item \emph{A full core in a convex region (cited).} For uniform
  density this is the torsion problem, and $\sqrt\tau$ is concave
  (Makar-Limanov 1971 in two dimensions, Kennington 1985 in any). The
  level sets of $\Phi_0$ are then nested convex surfaces around the
  single critical point. For stratified density, Kennington's
  Theorem~4.2 (as restated by Ishige and Salani 2014) applies to
  $\nabla^2\tau + \beta(\bx) = 0$ with $\beta = 4\pi G\rho(\bx)$ read as
  a function of position. If the density field is concave on $F$, or
  more generally $q$-concave for some $q \ge 1$, then
  $\tau^{q/(1+2q)}$ is concave. Hence $\tau$ is quasi-concave and the
  conclusion is the same. A density profile that is a concave,
  non-increasing function of radius on a ball satisfies this:
  examples are a uniform profile, one linear in $r$, and the stable
  cores of the library's benchmark models. A profile convex in $r$ is
  not covered. On a ball the Gidas--Ni--Nirenberg theorem makes
  $\Phi_0$ radial and monotone outright. Lions's conjecture, that every
  non-negative nonlinearity on a convex domain gives convex level sets,
  is false (Hamel, Nadirashvili and Sire 2016), so no blanket statement
  exists.
\item \emph{A shell (cited; no theorem).} An outer core between two
  level walls is a ring problem, with constant data on each wall. The
  ring theorem of Caffarelli and Spruck (1982), in the convention
  $\nabla^2\tau = \beta(\tau)$, requires $\beta$ non-decreasing with
  $\beta(0) = 0$. For the gravitational shell that would mean density
  increasing outward and vanishing at the outer wall, which is not the
  physical case. And Hamel, Nadirashvili and Sire (2016, Theorem 1.4)
  show that with $\beta$ a positive constant (uniform density) there are
  convex rings on which the level sets are not convex. So for the shell
  the assurance is near-sphericity, not a theorem; the counterexamples
  need rings far from concentric spheres (\S\ref{sec:shell}).
\item \emph{Transverse walls.} The boundary datum is no longer
  constant, and none of the above applies; the Reeb graph below is the
  general tool.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{The Reeb graph.}

\begin{definition}
\label{def:reeb}
Let $f$ be a continuous function on a space $X$. Two points are
equivalent if they have the same value of $f$ and lie in the same
connected component of that level set. The \emph{Reeb graph}
$\mathcal R(f)$ is the quotient $X/\!\sim$, with the quotient map $\pi:
X \to \mathcal R(f)$ and the induced height $\bar f$, $f = \bar
f\circ\pi$.
\end{definition}

In words: collapse each connected piece of each level set to a single
point. For a Morse function on a compact space the quotient is a
finite graph. Its \emph{edges} are families of components varying
continuously with the level, parametrised by the level, and its
\emph{vertices} lie over the critical values, where components are
born, die, split or merge. The construction goes back to Reeb (1946);
in scientific visualisation, where it is computed for scalar fields on
meshes, the tree case is called the \emph{contour tree}, and there are
standard algorithms for it (Carr, Snoeyink and Axen; to check). Two
examples fix the idea. The height function on a round sphere has
circles for level sets, born at the bottom and dying at the top:
$\mathcal R$ is an interval. The height function on an upright torus
has one circle near the bottom, two between the saddles, one again
near the top: $\mathcal R$ is a loop. In general the number of
independent loops of $\mathcal R(f)$ is at most the first Betti number
of $X$, so for a simply connected region, a ball or a
three-dimensional shell, $\mathcal R(\Phi)$ is always a tree. A
two-dimensional annulus is not simply connected and admits a loop in
principle; a loop cannot occur when the inner and outer walls' bands
of levels do not overlap (the largest value of $\Phi$ on the inner
wall lies below the smallest on the outer), which covers any
near-circular model.

\paragraph{The fluid's Reeb graph.} Under (L1) and (L2) the picture
is concrete. Below the lowest wall-critical value the level sets are
closed surfaces, one component each: the \emph{trunk} of the graph,
an interval starting at the centre (or at the inner wall). Above it
the level surface meets the wall, and $L_c$ is the part of the level
surface where the wall lies above it. As $c$ rises that part shrinks,
so components can split or die but never merge: the top of $\mathcal
R$ is a rooted \emph{tree}, branching at the saddles of $\Phi|_\Sigma$
and ending in leaves at its local maxima, which are the dome-shaped
tops where a component shrinks to a point. The local minima of
$\Phi|_\Sigma$ open holes in the level surface and are vertices of
degree two, which change the topology but not the number of
components; they can be ignored. At an inner wall, where $\nabla\Phi$
points into the fluid, the roles invert: components are born at the
local minima of $\Phi|_{\Sigma_i}$ and merge going up, and the tree
hangs downward from the trunk. A picture to hold on to: seen from
inside the fluid the wall is a landscape and the level is a sea level,
the fluid's level set being the land above water; as the sea rises,
islands separate at the passes and vanish at the peaks
(Figure~\ref{fig:reeb}).

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1.6cm,y=1.1cm]
  \fill[blue!8] plot[smooth,tension=0.6] coordinates
    {(0,-0.1) (0.7,1.3) (1.4,2.2) (2.0,1.6) (2.5,1.15) (3.1,1.7)
     (3.6,1.2) (4.1,0.65) (4.6,1.5) (5.1,2.4) (5.7,1.4) (6.2,-0.1)}
    -- (6.2,-0.7) -- (0,-0.7) -- cycle;
  \draw[thick] plot[smooth,tension=0.6] coordinates
    {(0,-0.1) (0.7,1.3) (1.4,2.2) (2.0,1.6) (2.5,1.15) (3.1,1.7)
     (3.6,1.2) (4.1,0.65) (4.6,1.5) (5.1,2.4) (5.7,1.4) (6.2,-0.1)};
  \foreach \y/\l in {0.35/1,0.9/2,1.4/3,2.0/4}
    \draw[gray,dashed] (0,\y) -- (6.2,\y) node[right] {\small $c_{\l}$};
  \node at (3.1,2.6) {solid};
  \node at (3.1,-0.4) {fluid};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{A cross-section through the top of the fluid, with potential
increasing upward and the wall bulging up in three places. At $c_1$ the level set (the part of the
dashed line inside the fluid) is one piece; at $c_2$ it has split at
the lower pass; at $c_3$ at the higher pass; at $c_4$ the middle piece
has died at its peak. The Reeb graph is a trunk, splitting into two
edges at the lower pass, the left one splitting again at the higher
pass, with three leaves.}
\label{fig:reeb}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{The level wall.} If a component of the wall is level, at
$c_w$, no level below $c_w$ meets it, and the level set at $c_w$ is
the wall itself; the trunk simply ends there. This is the Morse--Bott
case of Remark~\ref{rem:genericity}. After a small deformation of the
wall or of the potential, the wall is no longer level, $\Phi|_\Sigma$
has a Morse structure with many critical points, and the top of the
graph becomes a tree confined to a band of levels of width of the
order of the deformation: the islands of the perturbation's
topography.

\paragraph{Notation on the graph.} Edges are written $e$, with level
interval $(c_e^-, c_e^+)$ and component $L^e_c$; vertices $v$, at
level $c_v$; leaves are vertices of degree one at local extrema of
$\Phi|_\Sigma$. The \emph{tube} of an edge is $T_e := \pi^{-1}(e) =
\bigcup_cL^e_c$, an open subset of the fluid; the tubes partition the
fluid up to the measure-zero set of critical levels.

\subsection{Equilibrium on the Reeb graph}
\label{sec:exactgraph}
\label{sec:profiles}

\paragraph{The state factors through the graph.} Equation
\eqref{eq:hydro1} says that $\mathrm dp$ and $\mathrm d\Phi$ are
proportional, so $\mathrm dp\wedge\mathrm d\Phi = 0$ and the
restriction of $\mathrm dp$ to every level surface vanishes: \emph{$p$
is constant on each connected component of each level set}. That is
precisely the statement that $p$ factors through the quotient map,
\begin{equation*}
  p = \hat p\circ\pi,\qquad \hat p: \mathcal R \to \R .
\end{equation*}
Since $p$ is continuous on $\bar\Omega_F$ and $\pi$ is a quotient map,
$\hat p$ is continuous on the graph, vertices included. Then $\mathrm
dp = (\hat p\,'\circ\pi)\,\mathrm d\Phi$ along each edge, where the
prime is the derivative in the level coordinate, and
\eqref{eq:hydro1} gives $\rho = -\hat p\,'\circ\pi$: the density
factors too, $\rho = \hat\rho\circ\pi$, with
\begin{equation}
  \hat p\,' = -\hat\rho \quad\text{on every edge.}
  \label{eq:NL-profile}
\end{equation}
Finally $\partial_S\rho$ has one sign, so the label is determined by
$(p, \rho)$ and $S = \hat S\circ\pi$ as well. (The usual route takes
$\mathrm d$ of \eqref{eq:hydro1}, which gives $\mathrm d\rho\wedge
\mathrm d\Phi = 0$; the route above shows that nothing beyond the
proportionality of two 1-forms is used, and that what one gets is
constancy on \emph{components}, not on level sets. This is why the
Reeb graph, and not the level line, is the natural domain.) When the
levels are connected, (A2b), $\mathcal R$ is an interval, $\pi$ is
$\Phi$ itself, and the profiles are functions of the level in the
ordinary sense:
\begin{equation}
  p = \hat p(\Phi),\qquad S = \hat S(\Phi),\qquad
  \rho = \hat\rho(\Phi) := \rho\bigl(\hat p(\Phi), \hat S(\Phi)\bigr),
  \qquad \hat p\,' = -\hat\rho .
  \label{eq:profiles}
\end{equation}
In equilibrium the fluid carries the level structure of the
geopotential and nothing else: the entire state is a triple $(\hat p,
\hat\rho, \hat S)$ on a graph, two of which determine the third through
the equation of state. The only question left is which label sits on
which edge at which level.

\paragraph{Measures on the graph.} Push the volume and mass forms down
to the graph. On an edge $e$, parametrised by the level, their
densities are residue integrals over the edge's own component:
\begin{equation}
  \pi_*\vol\big|_e = A_e(c)\dc,\qquad
  \pi_*\varpi\big|_e = \hat\rho_e(c)A_e(c)\dc,\qquad
  A_e(c) := \int_{L^e_c}\frac{\vol}{\mathrm d\Phi} = \int_{L^e_c}\frac{\dS}{g} .
  \label{eq:graphmeasures}
\end{equation}
The second holds because $\rho = \hat\rho\circ\pi$ is constant on the
component, so it comes out of the residue integral; hence the density
profile is the ratio of the two push-forwards,
\begin{equation}
  \hat\rho = \frac{\pi_*\varpi}{\pi_*\vol} .
  \label{eq:Mprime}
\end{equation}
$A_e(c)\dc$ is the volume of the thin shell of the tube $T_e$ between
levels $c$ and $c + \mathrm dc$. By Sard these densities are smooth on
the edges; $A_e$ has an integrable singularity, of logarithmic type,
at a saddle value, and vanishes linearly at a nondegenerate leaf (the
dome top). Summed over the edges present at a level they recover the
global volume and mass per unit level, $A(c) = \sum_eA_e(c)$ and
$M'(c) = \sum_e\hat\rho_eA_e$. No level surface is ever constructed in
a computation: every integral of the form $\int_{T_e}h(\Phi)\,\vol$ is
a volume integral over the tube with the function composed with
$\Phi$.

\paragraph{Mass coordinates, and the arrangement.} When the levels are
connected the cumulative mass $M(c)$ is strictly increasing, by
\eqref{eq:NL-profile} and $\hat\rho > 0$, so $m = M(c)$ is a coordinate
on the levels, the \emph{mass coordinate}, counted from the bottom.
The label is then a function of it,
\begin{equation}
  \Lambda(m) := \hat S\bigl(M^{-1}(m)\bigr),\qquad
  S = \Lambda\circ M\circ\Phi ,
  \label{eq:Lambda}
\end{equation}
the label carried by the fluid that has mass $m$ beneath it: the
\emph{arrangement}. Think of a core sample drilled outward from the
centre, logging the label against the cumulative mass passed through;
that log is $\Lambda$. On a tree the global $M(c)$ no longer indexes
components, and the natural replacement counts from the end at which
the edge terminates. On an upper branch $e$, let $m_e(c)$ be the mass
of the \emph{pocket} above $(e, c)$: the fluid in the tube $T_e$ above
level $c$ together with every tube above it in the tree. It is well
defined, strictly decreasing up the edge, and zero at the leaf; on the
trunk it is $M_F - M(c)$. On a branch hanging from an inner wall,
count from the bottom instead. Each edge then carries its own log,
$\Lambda_e$, the label as a function of $m_e$. Equivalently, and often
more convenient, give each edge its share of the inventory,
\begin{equation}
  \mu_e := (\hat S_e)_*\bigl(\hat\rho_eA_e\dc\bigr),\qquad
  \mu = \sum_e\mu_e ,
  \label{eq:mue}
\end{equation}
the push-forward of the edge's mass by its label profile.

\paragraph{The inventory constraint, on an interval and on a tree.} On
an interval, push the mass form forward along the factorisation
\eqref{eq:Lambda}, using $\Phi_*\varpi = \mathrm dM$ and $M_*(\mathrm
dM) = \mathrm dm$, the Lebesgue measure on $[0, M_F]$:
\begin{equation}
  \mu = S_*\varpi = \Lambda_*\bigl(M_*\Phi_*\varpi\bigr) = \Lambda_*(\mathrm dm) .
  \label{eq:Lambdadist}
\end{equation}
Conversely, a $\Lambda$ with $\Lambda_*(\mathrm dm) = \mu$, placed on the
levels through \eqref{eq:Lambda}, gives a state with inventory $\mu$.
So in equilibrium the infinite-dimensional constraint on $(\varpi, S)$
becomes a statement about one function of one variable: regarded as a
function on $[0, M_F]$ with Lebesgue measure, $\Lambda$ must have
distribution $\mu$; it is a rearrangement of the inventory along the
mass axis. On a tree the same computation on each edge gives
$(\Lambda_e)_*(\mathrm dm_e) = \mu_e$, and the inventory constrains
only the sum \eqref{eq:mue}. How $\mu$ is divided among the edges is
not determined by $\mu$. This is the arrangement datum of
\S\ref{sec:arrangement}, and it is the fibred-Moser obstruction of
Proposition~\ref{prop:moser} in concrete form: equal fibre masses on
label surfaces that sit in different components.

\paragraph{The exact problem.} On each edge $e$ the equilibrium is the
pair of first-order equations
\begin{equation}
  \hat p_e' = -\rho\bigl(\hat p_e, \hat S_e\bigr),\qquad
  m_e' = \mp\rho\bigl(\hat p_e, \hat S_e\bigr)\,A_e(c),\qquad
  \hat S_e = \Lambda_e(m_e),
  \label{eq:exacttree}
\end{equation}
the sign according as $m_e$ is counted from the top or the bottom,
with $A_e$ given by the geometry of the container and the potential.
The coupling conditions are:
\begin{itemize}
\item at every vertex, \emph{continuity of pressure}, one condition for
  each edge beyond the first;
\item at every vertex, \emph{additivity of mass}: the pocket mass on
  the edge below equals the sum of the pocket masses on the edges above
  (one condition);
\item at every leaf, the pocket mass vanishes;
\item at the root, the mass below the lowest level vanishes and the
  total mass is $M_F$: the fluid fills its container.
\end{itemize}
The count closes: a rooted tree with $L$ leaves and internal vertices
of upward valence $k_v$ has $E = 1 + \sum_vk_v = L + \#\{v\}$ edges,
hence $2E$ constants of integration, and the conditions number $1 + L
+ \sum_v(k_v + 1) = 2E$. When the levels are connected there is one
edge, and \eqref{eq:exacttree} with its end conditions is
\begin{equation}
  \hat p\,' = -\rho\bigl(\hat p, \Lambda(M)\bigr),\qquad
  M' = \rho\bigl(\hat p, \Lambda(M)\bigr)\,\Vol'(c),\qquad
  M(c_{\min}) = 0,\quad M(c_{\max}) = M_F ,
  \label{eq:exact}
\end{equation}
two equations and two end conditions for the two functions $\hat p$
and $M$ of the single variable $c$, with $c_{\min}$ and $c_{\max}$ the
least and greatest values of the potential in the fluid. The second
end condition, that the fluid fill its container, fixes the one
additive constant of the pressure profile. That constant is the
degree-0 freedom of \S\ref{sec:dahlen}, and its linearisation is the
fill condition \eqref{eq:bcs}.

The exact secular state is therefore: the solid's motion, the
potential, and the profiles $(\hat p, m)$ on the graph. They are
coupled to the rest through three channels only: the level geometry,
that is the measure $\pi_*\vol$; the mass form $\hat\rho(\pi)\,\vol$
the fluid contributes to Poisson's equation; and the pressure
$\hat p(\pi)$ it exerts on the wall. Three things are absent: a fluid
displacement, a matching condition between fluid and wall
displacements, and any surface density on the wall.

\subsection{The arrangement: where \texorpdfstring{$\Lambda$}{Lambda} comes from}
\label{sec:arrangement}

\status{derived, except the energy-minimisation statement, which is
argued.}

Equations \eqref{eq:exacttree} and \eqref{eq:exact} are boundary-value
problems whose unknowns are the profiles; $\Lambda$ is a given
coefficient, like the equation of state. This subsection says what it
is, what fixes it, and in what form it is actually needed.

\paragraph{What is needed is the adiabat of each shell.} $\Lambda$
enters the equations only inside the equation of state, as $\rho(\hat
p, \Lambda(m))$. So what the problem needs, for each mass coordinate
$m$, is the function $p \mapsto \rho(p, \Lambda(m))$, the density of
the fluid of shell $m$ as its pressure varies at fixed label: the
shell's adiabat. By the gauge remark of \S\ref{sec:inventory}, the
label values themselves carry no information; the family of adiabats
indexed by mass does. This is the form in which the datum usually
arrives. A reference model gives, on each shell, a density, a pressure
and a bulk modulus, perhaps its pressure derivative too, and that is
the adiabat through that point to the order required, for instance
$\rho(p; m) \simeq \rho_0(m) + (p - p_0(m))\rho_0/\kappa_0$, or a
finite-strain form through the same point. Entropy and composition
never have to be evaluated.

\paragraph{The quantile function, and uniqueness.} Write $\Mcdf(S) =
\mu((-\infty, S])$; it is non-decreasing and right-continuous. Define
\begin{equation}
  \Lambda_\uparrow(m) := \inf\{S : \Mcdf(S) \ge m\},\qquad 0 \le m \le M_F .
  \label{eq:quantile}
\end{equation}
This is $\Mcdf$ read sideways: plot $\Mcdf$ with the label horizontal and
the mass vertical, go across at height $m$ until the graph is met, and
read off the label. Two features of $\Mcdf$ become two features of
$\Lambda_\uparrow$. An \emph{atom} of $\mu$, a finite mass at one label
such as a well-mixed layer, is a vertical jump of $\Mcdf$; a whole
interval of $m$ meets it, so $\Lambda_\uparrow$ is flat there. A
\emph{gap} in the labels, a range no fluid carries, such as the
contrast between two immiscible fluids, is a horizontal stretch of
$\Mcdf$; as $m$ passes its height, $\Lambda_\uparrow$ jumps across the
gap. Flats of $\Lambda$ are neutral layers and jumps of $\Lambda$ are
interfaces.

\begin{lemma}
\label{lem:quantile}
$\Lambda_\uparrow$ is non-decreasing and $(\Lambda_\uparrow)_*(\mathrm
dm) = \mu$. If $\tilde\Lambda$ is any non-decreasing function on $[0,
M_F]$ with $\tilde\Lambda_*(\mathrm dm) = \mu$, then $\tilde\Lambda =
\Lambda_\uparrow$ except possibly at the jump points of
$\Lambda_\uparrow$, which are countable.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Right-continuity of $\Mcdf$ makes the infimum attained,
$\Mcdf(\Lambda_\uparrow(m)) \ge m$, and with monotonicity this gives
$\Lambda_\uparrow(m) \le s \iff m \le \Mcdf(s)$. So $\{m :
\Lambda_\uparrow(m) \le s\} = [0, \Mcdf(s)]$, of length $\Mcdf(s) =
\mu((-\infty, s])$, which is the distributional claim. For
$\tilde\Lambda$ non-decreasing, $\{m : \tilde\Lambda(m) \le s\}$ is an
initial interval of length $\Mcdf(s)$, hence $[0, \Mcdf(s)]$ or $[0,
\Mcdf(s))$; thus $\tilde\Lambda \le s$ and $\Lambda_\uparrow \le s$
agree except possibly at the single point $m = \Mcdf(s)$. Letting $s$
range over the rationals, $\tilde\Lambda$ and $\Lambda_\uparrow$ agree
except on the countable set of such endpoints, which are the jump
points of $\Lambda_\uparrow$.
\end{proof}

The decreasing rearrangement is $\Lambda_\downarrow(m) =
\Lambda_\uparrow(M_F - m)$, with the same caveat. In the language of
optimal transport, $\Lambda_\uparrow$ is the monotone transport map
from Lebesgue measure on the mass axis to $\mu$; in one dimension that
map is unique, and it is optimal for every convex cost. That
uniqueness is exactly what fails on a tree.

\paragraph{Stability selects the quantile function.} The
stratification parameter and the buoyancy frequency along an edge are
(\S\ref{sec:background})
\begin{equation*}
  D := \hat\rho\,' + \frac{\hat\rho^2}{\kappa} = \partial_S\rho\,\hat S',
  \qquad N^2 := -\frac{g^2D}{\hat\rho} ,
\end{equation*}
the second equality by the chain rule through the equation of state
with $\hat p\,' = -\hat\rho$. $D$ measures the excess of the actual
density gradient over the adiabatic one; a lifted parcel is heavier
than its new surroundings by $-Dg\zeta$ for a lift $\zeta$, and
returns if $D < 0$. Stable or neutral stratification everywhere, $D
\le 0$, therefore says that $\hat S'$ has the sign opposite to
$\partial_S\rho$, that is that $\Lambda$ is monotone, increasing if
$\partial_S\rho < 0$, as for entropy or for the concentration of a
light element. By Lemma~\ref{lem:quantile}:

\begin{proposition}
\label{prop:stable-arrangement}
On an interval, a stably or neutrally stratified equilibrium with
inventory $\mu$ has $\Lambda = \Lambda_\uparrow$ (or $\Lambda_\downarrow$,
according to the sign of $\partial_S\rho$), uniquely up to its values
at interfaces.
\end{proposition}

Every other arrangement has $D > 0$ somewhere and is overturned. In
the energy picture the stable arrangement should be the minimiser of
the energy at fixed inventory and fixed geometry. For an
incompressible fluid in a fixed potential this is the
Hardy--Littlewood rearrangement inequality: $\int\Phi\,\varpi$ is least
when the densest fluid sits at the lowest potential, which is
Lorenz's reference state in the ocean literature. For a compressible
fluid the same conclusion is expected under the one-sign assumption on
$\partial_S\rho$, since the density ordering of any two parcels is then
the same at every pressure and no thermobaric reversal can occur; the
non-uniqueness of the compressible reference state found in the ocean
literature comes from two labels. This is argued, not proved.

\paragraph{The four sources.} In practice $\Lambda$, or rather the
family of adiabats, comes from one of four places.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{A background equilibrium.} When perturbing a given state,
  read the arrangement off it, $\Lambda = \hat S_0\circ M_0^{-1}$, or
  directly the adiabat of each shell from the reference profiles. For
  the linear theory even this is more than needed: the closure uses
  only $\rho$, $\kappa$, $\rho'$ and the geometry along the background,
  with $\Lambda$ entering through $D = \rho' + \rho^2/\kappa$, which is
  computed from $\rho'$ directly.
\item \emph{The inventory and stability.} Given $\mu$ and the
  assumption that the final state is stably stratified,
  Proposition~\ref{prop:stable-arrangement} fixes $\Lambda$. This is
  the case when an equilibrium is built from scratch, as in planet
  formation or thermal evolution.
\item \emph{An initial condition, in the time-evolution sense.} Any
  advective relaxation conserves $\mu$ exactly but conserves the
  arrangement only if nothing overturns. If the end state is stable,
  the path does not matter: only $\mu$ survives from the initial
  condition and source 2 applies. If it is not, $\Lambda$ depends on
  the history; such a state is an equilibrium but not a robust one,
  and it is where an assumption of fixed arrangement does real work.
\item \emph{Minimisation.} If the problem is posed as minimising the
  energy over all states with inventory $\mu$ rather than as
  \eqref{eq:exact}, then $\Lambda$ is an output: the minimiser picks
  the stable arrangement, and \eqref{eq:exact} is the Euler--Lagrange
  system after that choice. Solving \eqref{eq:exact} at a prescribed
  $\Lambda$ finds all equilibria with that arrangement, unstable ones
  included.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{On a tree.} Sources 1 and 4 carry over unchanged. Source 2
does not: stability makes each $\Lambda_e$ monotone along its edge,
with the label continuous with the edge below at the vertex, but by
\S\ref{sec:exactgraph} the inventory fixes only $\sum_e\mu_e$. Which
part of the inventory each pocket receives is additional data. A
background model built from a radial profile supplies it implicitly,
as ``the same adiabat at the same level in every pocket'', a
single-valued $\hat\rho(c)$; a model built from an inventory must
supply it explicitly, or appeal to a minimisation. Two pockets at the
same level are hydrostatically isolated and may hold different fluid
at different pressures, and the equilibrium problem has a solution for
every admissible division.

\subsection{The relabelling symmetry, and what (A4) says}
\label{sec:A4}

\status{derived.}

The maps that change no physical field form the stabiliser of the
state: the diffeomorphisms $\psi$ of the fluid with $\psi_*\varpi =
\varpi$ and $S\circ\psi^{-1} = S$. Its Lie algebra consists of the
vector fields with
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal L_{\bw}\varpi = 0\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal L_{\bw}S = 0 ,
  \label{eq:gauge}
\end{equation}
that is $\Div(\rho\bw) = 0$ and $\bw$ tangent to the label surfaces.
Where $D \ne 0$ the label surfaces are the level surfaces, so these
are the mass-divergence-free fields along the levels: the \emph{strict
relabelling class} of \S\ref{sec:setting}. Where the label is constant
the second condition is void and only $\mathcal L_{\bw}\varpi = 0$
remains: the mass-preserving fields of a neutral layer, \emph{class K}.
These fields are the kernel of the energy's second variation, and they
are what any displacement description of the fluid cannot help
carrying. The linear classes of \code{doc/static\_fluid\_core.tex} are
exactly these infinitesimal relabellings at a configuration. In the
Reeb-graph picture they are the fields tangent to the fibres of $\pi$
that preserve the residue $\varpi/\mathrm d\Phi$ on each fibre: motions
within a component of a level set that redistribute nothing the graph
can see.

The label can serve as a coordinate on the
levels only through the inverse of $\hat S$. That inverse fails to exist
exactly where the stratification is neutral --- there the label is
locally uniform and does not identify a level --- and wherever $\hat S$
is not monotone. The mass coordinate has neither defect, by
\eqref{eq:Mprime}, and this is the source of the uniformity in $D$
found in Part~II.

Assumption (A4) is that the perturbed equilibrium has the same
$\Lambda$ as the background. For a strictly monotone $\Lambda$ this is
automatic for perturbations small in $C^1$: such a perturbation stays
monotone in the same sense, and the monotone rearrangement of a given
inventory is unique (Lemma~\ref{lem:quantile}). In general, (A4)
selects the branch of equilibria continuously connected to the
background. Overturned arrangements are a separate family of states.
They matter for the stability of unstably stratified fluid, not for
its linear response. Where $\Lambda$ is constant on an interval (a
neutral layer), rearrangements within that interval change nothing,
and (A4) is empty there.

\subsection{The variational form in the mass coordinate}
\label{sec:NL-variational}

\status{derived; the global convexity remark is argued.}

Hold the geopotential and the wall fixed, so that the level geometry
$\Vol$ is given, and take the levels connected. An equilibrium is then
determined by the \emph{volume-of-mass map} $v(m)$, the volume occupied
by the fluid below mass coordinate $m$, with $\tau = 1/\hat\rho =
v'(m)$ and level $c(m) = C(v(m))$, where $C := \Vol^{-1}$; in this
subsection a prime on $v$ denotes $\mathrm d/\mathrm dm$. The fluid's
energy in the given potential, internal plus potential, counted with
the potential at full weight because the potential is held fixed, is
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal E[v] = \int_0^{M_F}\Bigl(e\bigl(v'(m), \Lambda(m)\bigr)
  + C\bigl(v(m)\bigr)\Bigr)\,\mathrm dm,\qquad
  v(0) = 0,\quad v(M_F) = |\Omega_F| ,
  \label{eq:Ev}
\end{equation}
with $e$ written as a function of the specific volume and the label.
Three things are visible at once. The inventory enters only as the
coefficient $\Lambda(m)$; the geometry only through $C$; and the
internal energy depends on $v'$ alone and is convex in it,
$\partial_\tau^2e = -\partial_\tau p = \kappa/\tau$. The end conditions
are the fill: the fluid's lowest and highest levels are the
container's.

\emph{First variation.} With $\partial_\tau e = -p$,
\begin{equation*}
  \delta\mathcal E = \int\bigl(-p\,\delta v' + C'(v)\,\delta v\bigr)\,\mathrm dm
  = \int\bigl(p' + C'(v)\bigr)\,\delta v\,\mathrm dm ,
\end{equation*}
so stationarity is $\mathrm dp/\mathrm dm = -C'(v) = -1/A(c)$, which is
\eqref{eq:NL-profile} in the mass coordinate. This is the
one-dimensional energy whose stationarity is the exact problem; it is
legitimate as a reduction only because \S\ref{sec:exactgraph} has
already shown that three-dimensional critical points have
level-function fields.

\emph{Second variation.} With $C'' = -A'/A^3$ and $\mathrm dm =
\hat\rho A\dc$,
\begin{equation}
  \delta^2\mathcal E
  = \int\Bigl(\frac{\kappa}{\tau}\,\delta v'^2 - \frac{A'}{A^3}\,\delta v^2\Bigr)\mathrm dm
  = \int\Bigl(\frac{\kappa\,\delta v_c'^2}{A}
    + \hat\rho\Bigl(\frac1A\Bigr)'\delta v^2\Bigr)\dc ,
  \label{eq:d2E}
\end{equation}
where on the right $\delta v$ is regarded as a function of the level
and $\delta v_c' := \mathrm d\,\delta v/\mathrm dc$. This is the
quadratic functional of the linear closure, \eqref{eq:J} of
\S\ref{sec:secondvar}, obtained here in two lines. Its first term is
the compression of the shells; its second is the concavity of the
potential energy in volume, $C$ being concave wherever $A$ increases
with level, as it does in a core. So $\mathcal E$ is not globally
convex, and the positivity of \eqref{eq:d2E} for stable
