stratification, proved in \S\ref{sec:uniqueness}, is a Hardy-type
inequality holding at the equilibrium, not a convexity property of the
problem. Uniqueness of the exact equilibrium for a given geometry and
arrangement is therefore not settled by this functional, and no proof
of it is known.

On a tree the functional is a sum over edges of the same form, with
the volume-of-mass maps counted from the appropriate end, the leaf and
root conditions essential, mass additivity at the vertices essential,
and pressure continuity at the vertices arising as the natural
condition from the boundary terms $\partial L/\partial v' = -p$.

\subsection{The fluid at the wall}
\label{sec:fluidatwall}
\label{sec:NL-finite}

\status{argued.}

Two situations, and what they mean for the foliation assumption.

\paragraph{A transverse wall.} Its band of levels has width of order
one, and its tree is part of the background. A deformation of
amplitude $\varepsilon$ shifts the wall-critical values, and the levels
near the ends of the band, by $O(\varepsilon)$. At the top of the
fluid, a nondegenerate level point, the highest levels form a small
dome-shaped pocket; a nondegenerate maximum stays a single maximum
under a small smooth perturbation, so the leaves persist, and the
pocket at a dome-shaped top, of height $O(\varepsilon)$, changes its
mass only at $O(\varepsilon^2)$.

\paragraph{A level wall.} In the background the trunk ends at $c_w$:
the whole wall is critical for $\Phi_0|_\Sigma$. After deformation the
wall is no longer level, and the geopotential on it ranges over an
interval of width $O(\varepsilon)$ about $c_w$. Within this narrow band
of levels, $\Vol'(c)$ falls from its value just below to zero, and the
band holds a first-order amount of fluid: the top of the mass
coordinate, spread along the wall wherever the wall has risen above
the levels. Nothing about it is singular: it has the ambient density,
and there is no surface mass. But it is first order in mass while
being of zero width in level at first order. This, and only this, is
why level walls need the extra discussion of \S\ref{sec:levelwalls}.

\paragraph{The band is a tree.} In the band the level sets are
generically disconnected: the deformed wall rises above a given level
$c$ only in isolated patches, so $\{\Phi = c\}\cap\Omega_F$ consists of
disjoint pockets, one under each patch, each connected to the main
body of fluid below the saddle levels. The band's tree is the tree of
islands of the deformation's topography. Two points follow. First, the
connectedness of the levels fails after
perturbation in this band, as a matter of course; it is required only
below the band, which is how (A2b) is stated. Second, the
arrangement among the pockets is not fixed by the inventory
(\S\ref{sec:arrangement}), so the exact secular state near a level wall
is a family rather than a point: in each pocket the pressure is a
function of the level, continuous with the main profile where the
pocket joins it, but two pockets at the same level may hold fluid of
different labels at different pressures. The family is degenerate at
second order only: a pocket of height $O(\varepsilon)$ carries
$O(\varepsilon)$ mass, and exchanging fluid between two pockets at the
same level changes the energy by that mass times a label difference of
$O(\varepsilon)$. The linear theory is therefore untouched; it needs the
band's total mass and volume, which are fixed, and nothing of its
internal arrangement. In the exact problem \eqref{eq:exact} the band
should be read on its tree, with one profile per pocket; everything
below the band is the single interval the assumption describes. This
is the exact-theory origin of everything the linear theory has to say
about level walls: the surface density $\sigma$ of \S\ref{sec:levelwalls}
is this band measured against an equipotential, and the moving-wall
term $F3$ is its first variation (\S\ref{sec:feels}).

\paragraph{The foliation at finite amplitude.} At finite amplitude the
level structure is that of the deformed body's geopotential, and the
questions of \S\ref{sec:foliation} are asked of it: the convexity
results apply to the deformed region, and the implementation's
assertion (\S\ref{sec:implementation}) is made on the current iterate.
The level-point behaviour of the linear theory on a transverse wall
(\S\ref{sec:transverse}) is the derivative of this regular picture,
taken where the support of a pocket degenerates.

\subsection{The nonlinear computation}
\label{sec:NL-compute}

\status{programme.}

The unknowns are the solid's finite motion (the referential class's
variables, with $\varphi_e$ now the current iterate), the potential,
and one pair of profiles $(\hat p, M)$ per fluid region on its Reeb
graph --- an interval below the band of a level wall. The coordinate in
which the arrangement is fixed, by (A4), is the mass coordinate, so the
natural one-dimensional unknown is the level-of-mass map $c(m)$ of
\S\ref{sec:energy1d}, equivalently the volume-of-mass map $v(m)$ of
\eqref{eq:Ev}. The level geometry $\Vol(c)$ is read from the current
potential by the same histogram of $\Phi$ over the fluid quadrature
points that the linear closure uses (\S\ref{sec:implementation}). The
coupled problem has three parts:
\begin{itemize}
\item the solid's finite elasticity, with the follower load $\hat
  p(\Phi)$ on the wall;
\item Poisson's equation, with the fluid's density $\rho(\hat p,
  \Lambda(M))$ on the level foliation;
\item the profile equations \eqref{eq:exact}, or \eqref{eq:exacttree}
  on a tree, on the one-dimensional grid.
\end{itemize}
Its Newton step is the bordered system of \S\ref{sec:weak} assembled on
the current iterate, with the level geometry $A(c)$ and the wall
levels re-read from the current potential at every step. Nothing in
the fluid is a field on a mesh: the fluid's whole state is a few
hundred numbers.

Three relations place this programme.
\begin{itemize}
\item The wholly fluid body (no solid) is the hydrostatic figure
  (\code{doc/equilibrium\_figures.tex}). Its existence theory is that of
  self-gravitating fluid masses as energy minimisers at fixed mass and,
  for rotation, fixed angular momentum (Auchmuty and Beals 1971; Lieb
  and Yau 1987 for the non-rotating case), with the label structure
  adding the rearrangement constraint of Burton. The nonlinear closure
  is a hydrostatic figure inside a deformable elastic container, and
  the two programmes should share their fluid machinery.
\item With $\bOmega$ dynamical, the exact angular momentum carries the
  fluid only through $\int\rho\,\bx\times(\bOmega\times\bx)$, there
  being no relative fluid motion in a secular state. This is the
  nonlinear form of the statement in \S\ref{sec:rotation} that the
  solid-restricted Tisserand gauge is forced.
\item The library's nonlinear gauged and Maxwell routes carry a fluid
  displacement, finite-elastic with a gauge or viscoelastically
  relaxed. Their nonlinear content is a trajectory through the
  rearrangement class. This route carries none. It is for that reason
  the natural nonlinear statement of the secular state, while those
  routes are the natural statements of how the secular state is
  reached.
\end{itemize}

%=====================================================================
\section{The background, and the stratification parameter}
\label{sec:background}

\status{derived.}

Write the background quantities without hats or subscripts where no
confusion arises: $\Phi_0$ the background geopotential, $g$ and $\nuh$
as in \S\ref{sec:setting}; $L_c$ a background level surface in $F$,
closed or with boundary on a transverse wall, with weighted area
$A(c)$; $\rho(c)$, $p_0(c)$, $\kappa(c)$ the background profiles, with
$p_0' = -\rho$; $M_0(c)$ the background mass coordinate and $\hat
S_0(c) = \Lambda(M_0(c))$ the background label profile; and $c_{\min}
< c < c_{\mathrm{top}}$ the range of levels in the fluid, so that
$c_{\mathrm{top}} = c_w$ for a top level wall.

Two density gradients along the foliation matter, and their
difference controls the fluid's static behaviour throughout the note.
By the chain rule through the equation of state, with $(\partial\rho/
\partial p)_S = \rho/\kappa$ and $p_0' = -\rho$,
\begin{equation*}
  \rho' = \Bigl(\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial p}\Bigr)_S p_0'
  + \partial_S\rho\,\hat S_0'
  = -\frac{\rho^2}{\kappa} + \partial_S\rho\,\hat S_0' .
\end{equation*}
The first term is the \emph{adiabatic} gradient: the density change of
a parcel compressed along the hydrostatic pressure profile, keeping
its label. The left side is the \emph{in-situ} gradient of its
surroundings. The stratification parameter is the excess of the
actual gradient over the adiabatic one:
\begin{equation}
  D := \rho' + \frac{\rho^2}{\kappa}
  = \partial_S\rho\,\hat S_0'
  = \partial_S\rho\,\Lambda'(M_0)\,\rho A .
  \label{eq:D}
\end{equation}
The name ``buoyancy'' is earned by a parcel argument. Lift a parcel by
a small height $\zeta$ along $\nuh$, so that its level rises by
$g\zeta$, keeping its label. Its pressure falls by $\rho g\zeta$, so
its density changes by $-(\rho/\kappa)\rho g\zeta$. The ambient density
at the new level is $\rho + \rho' g\zeta$. The parcel is therefore
denser than its surroundings by $-(\rho^2/\kappa + \rho')g\zeta =
-Dg\zeta$, and gravity acts on that excess with force $-(-Dg\zeta)\,
g\nuh$ per unit volume, so
\begin{equation*}
  \rho\,\ddot\zeta = D\,g^2\zeta,
  \qquad\text{that is}\qquad
  \ddot\zeta = -N^2\zeta
  \quad\text{with}\quad
  N^2 = -\frac{g^2D}{\rho} .
\end{equation*}
\begin{itemize}
\item $D < 0$ ($N^2 > 0$) is stable stratification: a lifted parcel
  returns;
\item $D > 0$ ($N^2 < 0$) is unstable;
\item $D = 0$ ($N^2 = 0$) is the neutral, or Adams--Williamson, state,
  in which the density profile is exactly the adiabatic,
  self-compressed one.
\end{itemize}
On a radial background $N^2 = -g(\rho^{-1}\partial_r\rho +
g\rho/\kappa)$, the usual form.

The important reading of \eqref{eq:D} is that $D = 0$ exactly where the
label is locally uniform in the mass coordinate. A neutral layer is a
layer of one substance. It corresponds to an atom of the inventory, or
more generally to an interval of the mass axis on which $\Lambda$ is
constant. Within it, any parcel can stand in for any other. The
closure of Part~II never divides by $D$ or $N^2$; $D$ re-enters only
when one asks about labels or about displacements.

%=====================================================================
\part{The linear theory}

\section{Linearisation: the closure and its end conditions}
\label{sec:lin}
\label{sec:general}

\status{derived, except Remark~\ref{rem:dome}, which is a leading-order
local expansion. Measured in one dimension (\S\ref{sec:verification}).}

Perturb everything to first order in $\varepsilon$. The solid's
displacement is $\bu_s$. The geopotential becomes $\Phi_0 + \phi$,
where $\phi$ includes both the applied potential and the self-gravity
of the perturbed body. The inventory is unchanged, and by (A4) so is
$\Lambda$. This section is the centre of the note: Dahlen's
elimination (\S\ref{sec:dahlen}) leaves one function of the level free
and silently sets it to zero, which on the sphere is wrong only at
degree $0$ and off the sphere at every degree; the closure determines
it.

\subsection{The level geometry}
\label{sec:levelgeom}

One formula does the work of this subsection. If a map is perturbed,
$\Phi_\varepsilon = \Phi_0 + \varepsilon\phi$, while a top form
$\alpha$ is held fixed, then
\begin{equation}
  \frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm d\varepsilon}\Big|_{0}(\Phi_\varepsilon)_*\alpha
  = -\partial_c\bigl(\Phi_{0*}(\phi\,\alpha)\bigr),
  \label{eq:dpush}
\end{equation}
because $\int h(\Phi_0 + \varepsilon\phi)\,\alpha = \int h(\Phi_0)\,
\alpha + \varepsilon\int h'(\Phi_0)\,\phi\,\alpha + O(\varepsilon^2)$,
and the second term is $\langle\Phi_{0*}(\phi\alpha), h'\rangle =
-\langle\partial_c\Phi_{0*}(\phi\alpha), h\rangle$. Applied to $\alpha =
\vol$ it gives $\delta A = -\bar\Phi'$, the change of the level
geometry due to the potential; the motion of the wall adds the volume
it sweeps. In words: the region of fluid below level $c$ is bounded by
two kinds of surface, part of the level surface $L_c$ and part of the
wall. Each moves. The level surface moves to where $\Phi_0 + \phi =
c$, a distance $-\phi/g$ along $\nuh$, and the outward normal of the
region there is $\nuh$. The wall patch $\Sigma_c$ moves outward by
$\bu_s\cdot\bn$. So
\begin{equation}
  \delta\Vol(c) = -\bar\Phi(c) + S_c[\bu_s],\qquad
  \bar\Phi(c) := \oint_{L_c}\phi\,\frac{\dS}{g},\qquad
  S_c[\bu_s] := \int_{\Sigma_c}\bu_s\cdot\bn\dS .
  \label{eq:dV}
\end{equation}
Here $\bar\Phi$ is the level integral of the potential perturbation,
and $S_c$ the volume swept by the wall below level $c$ (a swept volume,
not to be confused with the label $S$). Write $S[\bu_s] :=
\oint_\Sigma\bu_s\cdot\bn\dS$ for the volume swept by the whole wall.
\begin{itemize}
\item On a transverse wall, $S_c$ grows continuously through the range
  of levels the wall spans.
\item On a top level wall, $\Sigma_c$ is empty below $c_w$, so
  $\delta\Vol = -\bar\Phi$ throughout the fluid.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{The profiles, and the lift}
\label{sec:lift}

Take as unknowns the perturbations of the two functions in
\eqref{eq:exact}, both taken at a fixed level value $c$:
\begin{equation*}
  f(c) := \delta\hat p(c),\qquad \delta M(c) .
\end{equation*}
At a fixed point $\bx$ in the fluid with $\Phi_0(\bx) = c$, the
Eulerian perturbations follow from $p = \hat p(\Phi_0 + \phi)$ and its
analogues:
\begin{equation}
  p_1 = f - \rho\phi,\qquad
  \rho_1 = \rho'\phi + \delta\hat\rho(c),\qquad
  S_1 = \hat S_0'\phi + \delta\hat S(c) .
  \label{eq:eulerian}
\end{equation}
So the free function of Dahlen's problem, $f = p_1 + \rho\phi$, is the
perturbation of the one-dimensional pressure profile at fixed level;
that is why it is a function of $\Phi_0$.

Now define the \emph{lift}
\begin{equation}
  s(c) := \frac{\delta M(c)}{\rho A} .
  \label{eq:lift}
\end{equation}
Its meaning comes from the mass coordinate. The fluid of background
mass coordinate $m = M_0(c)$ sat at level $c$. After the perturbation
it sits at the level $c^*$ with $M(c^*) = M_0(c)$, that is
$M_0(c^*) + \delta M(c^*) = M_0(c)$, so $c^* = c - s(c)$ to first order.
The lift is therefore the amount, in units of potential, by which the
fluid has dropped off the level it used to occupy. Equivalently, $s/g$
is the height by which that fluid's original equipotential now stands
above it. The lift is minus the perturbation of the inverse map
$M^{-1}$, since $\delta(M^{-1}) = -\delta M/M'$.

The lift is a function of $c$ by construction. It is built from
$\delta M$, which is a function of the level, through the mass
coordinate, which exists whatever the stratification. No displacement
field and no assumption about $D$ has been used. (A lift defined
through a fluid displacement, $-\Delta\Phi$, is the \emph{pointwise}
lift $\hat s$ of \S\ref{sec:realise}, a field in three dimensions whose
level mean is $s$.)

In the language of \S\ref{sec:exactgraph}: $\mathrm dM = \Phi_*\varpi$,
and perturbing both the map and the form, $\delta(\Phi_*\varpi) =
\Phi_{0*}(\rho_1\,\vol) - \partial_c\Phi_{0*}(\phi\,\varpi)$ by
\eqref{eq:dpush}, which with $\rho_1 = \rho'\phi + \delta\hat\rho$ is
$(A\,\delta\hat\rho - \rho\bar\Phi')\dc$: the mass law \eqref{eq:mass}
below, before any substitution.

Since $\Lambda$ is unchanged, $\delta\hat S = \Lambda'(M_0)\,\delta M =
\hat S_0'\,s$, using $\hat S_0' = \Lambda'\rho A$. The equation of state
then gives
\begin{equation}
  \delta\hat\rho = \frac{\rho}{\kappa}f + \partial_S\rho\,\delta\hat S
  = \frac{\rho f}{\kappa} + D\,s .
  \label{eq:drho}
\end{equation}
Perturbing $\hat p\,' = -\hat\rho$ gives $f' = -\delta\hat\rho$, that is
\begin{equation}
  D\,s = -\Bigl(f' + \frac{\rho f}{\kappa}\Bigr) .
  \label{eq:Ds}
\end{equation}
This is the first relation of the slaving equation, which can be
obtained by comparing two determinations of the density; here it is
simply the perturbed hydrostatic equation, with the label
tracked through the mass coordinate. Where $D = 0$ it constrains $f$
alone and says nothing about $s$, but $s$ remains defined by
\eqref{eq:lift}.

\begin{remark}[The Eulerian route, and the per-level budget]
\label{rem:eulerian-route}
Momentum balance alone gives the first two relations of
\eqref{eq:eulerian} without the exact theory, and that is how the 23
October note began. The along-level component of \eqref{eq:momentum}
has no $\rho_1$ term, since $\nabla_{\!\parallel}\Phi_0 = \mathbf 0$, and
$\rho$ is constant on each level component, so
$\nabla_{\!\parallel}(p_1 + \rho\phi) = \mathbf 0$ and
\begin{equation}
  p_1 + \rho\,\phi = f(\Phi_0) ,
  \label{eq:G-f}
\end{equation}
one number per level, or per component of a level set, continuous
where components merge. The component along $\nuh$, $-\partial_\nu p_1
- \rho\,\partial_\nu\phi - g\rho_1 = 0$, with $\partial_\nu p_1 = f'g -
\rho' g\,\phi - \rho\,\partial_\nu\phi$, gives
\begin{equation}
  \rho_1 = \rho'\,\phi - f'(\Phi_0) ,
  \label{eq:G-rho}
\end{equation}
which is \eqref{eq:eulerian} with $f' = -\delta\hat\rho$. Every piece of
local physics --- momentum balance, the constitutive law, mass
conservation --- acts point by point, while $f$ is constant along each
level, invisible to every along-level gradient the momentum equation
takes; so no local relation fixes it, and whatever does must be global
on each level. That global statement is the
conservation of the mass of fluid between equipotentials, the
per-level budget
\begin{equation}
  \Delta M(c) := \int_{R_c}\rho_1\dV + \rho(c)\,U(c)
  + \int_{\Sigma_c}\rho_F\,\bu_s\cdot\bn\dS = 0,
  \qquad U(c) = -\bigl(\bar\Phi + sA\bigr),
  \label{eq:G-band}
\end{equation}
with $\rho_F = \rho(\Phi_0(\bx))$ along a transverse wall. In the
present derivation it is built into the definitions: the change of the
mass below the perturbed level $c$ is $\delta M = \int_{R_c}\rho_1\dV -
\rho\bar\Phi + \int_{\Sigma_c}\rho_F\,\bu_s\cdot\bn\dS$, so $\Delta M =
\delta M - \rho A s$, and \eqref{eq:G-band} is the definition
\eqref{eq:lift} of the lift. The closure then follows from the three
laws below, with no displacement and no division by $D$.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The cumulative volume, and three laws}
\label{sec:threelaws}

Define
\begin{equation}
  V(c) := \delta\Vol(c) - s\,A .
  \label{eq:Vdef}
\end{equation}
Its meaning: the fluid that lay below level $c$, a fixed body of fluid
$B_c$ of mass $M_0(c)$, now lies below level $c - s$. It therefore
occupies volume $\Vol(c - s) = \Vol_0(c) + \delta\Vol(c) - sA$. So
$V(c)$ is the change in volume of $B_c$. It is a Lagrangian quantity,
but it has been defined without any displacement field. From
\eqref{eq:lift} and \eqref{eq:Vdef}, $\delta M = \rho(\delta\Vol - V)$.
We also write
\begin{equation*}
  U(c) := V(c) - S_c[\bu_s] = -\bigl(\bar\Phi + sA\bigr)
\end{equation*}
for the volume flux of fluid through the level surface $L_c$; on a
level wall $U = V$ below $c_w$, and the implementation carries $U$
(\S\ref{sec:implementation}).

\emph{Mass.} Linearise $M' = \hat\rho\,\Vol'$ to get $\delta M' =
A\,\delta\hat\rho + \rho\,\delta\Vol'$. Substitute $\delta M =
\rho(\delta\Vol - V)$ and cancel $\rho\,\delta\Vol'$:
\begin{equation}
  \delta\hat\rho = \rho'\,s - \frac{\rho V'}{A} .
  \label{eq:mass}
\end{equation}

\emph{Compression.} Equate \eqref{eq:mass} with \eqref{eq:drho} and use
$D = \rho' + \rho^2/\kappa$; the $\rho's$ terms cancel, leaving
\begin{equation}
  f + \rho s = -\kappa\,\frac{V'}{A} .
  \label{eq:compression}
\end{equation}
Both sides have direct meanings. The fluid at mass coordinate $M_0(c)$
now sits at level $c - s$, where the pressure is $\hat p(c - s) = p_0 +
f + \rho s$. So $f + \rho s$ is the pressure change following that
fluid. The shell between mass coordinates $M_0(c)$ and $M_0(c +
\mathrm dc)$ had volume $A\dc$ and now has $(A + V')\dc$, so $V'/A$ is
its fractional volume change. Equation \eqref{eq:compression} is the
adiabatic law for that shell.

\emph{Hydrostatics.} Combine $f' = -\delta\hat\rho$ with
\eqref{eq:mass}:
\begin{equation}
  f' = \frac{\rho V'}{A} - \rho'\,s .
  \label{eq:hydrolin}
\end{equation}
The stratification parameter has disappeared. Equations
\eqref{eq:Vdef}, \eqref{eq:compression} and \eqref{eq:hydrolin} are the
whole linear problem for the fluid, and none of them contains $D$.

\subsection{The closure equation and its end conditions}
\label{sec:closuresec}
\label{sec:closure}

Eliminate $f$ and $s$. Differentiating \eqref{eq:compression} gives $f'
= -(\kappa V'/A)' - \rho's - \rho s'$. Equating this with
\eqref{eq:hydrolin} cancels $\rho's$, leaving $-(\kappa V'/A)' - \rho s'
= \rho V'/A$. From \eqref{eq:Vdef}, $s = (\delta\Vol - V)/A$, so $s' =
(\delta\Vol/A)' - V'/A - (1/A)'V$. The $\rho V'/A$ terms cancel,
leaving
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{\;-\Bigl(\kappa\frac{V'}{A}\Bigr)' + \rho\Bigl(\frac1A\Bigr)'V
  = \rho\Bigl(\frac{\delta\Vol}{A}\Bigr)',\qquad
  c_{\min} < c < c_{\mathrm{top}},\qquad
  \delta\Vol = -\bar\Phi + S_c[\bu_s].\;}
  \label{eq:closure}
\end{equation}
The left-hand side is self-adjoint: a compressional stiffness, which is
the only place $\kappa$ appears, and a geometric term from the
variation of the shell volumes with level. The forcing is the change in
the level geometry. Where levels first meet a transverse wall, or at a
saddle-type level point, $A$ has a kink and $S_c'$ a jump or an
integrable singularity. The coefficients of \eqref{eq:closure} are then
piecewise smooth, which the self-adjoint (weak) form accommodates
without change.

\paragraph{The expanded form.} Expanding $\rho(\delta\Vol/A)'$ with
$\delta\Vol = -\bar\Phi + S_c$ and $U = V - S_c$ gives the general
equation
\begin{equation}
  -\Bigl(\frac{\kappa V'}{A}\Bigr)' + \rho\Bigl(\frac1A\Bigr)'U
  + \rho\Bigl(\frac{\bar\Phi}{A}\Bigr)' - \rho\,\frac{S_c'}{A} = 0 ,
  \qquad c_{\min} < c < c_{\mathrm{top}} .
  \label{eq:V-ode-general}
\end{equation}
The wall enters only through the volumes it sweeps: $S_c$ in the
gravity term and its density $S_c'$ as a forcing, both supported
exactly where the wall cuts the levels. On a \emph{level} wall $S_c =
0$ below $c_w$, $U = V$, and
\begin{equation}
  -\Bigl(\frac{\kappa V'}{A}\Bigr)' + \rho\Bigl(\frac1A\Bigr)'V
  + \rho\Bigl(\frac{\bar\Phi}{A}\Bigr)' = 0
  \qquad c_{\min} < c < c_w .
  \label{eq:V-ode}
\end{equation}
Its three terms are, in order: the compressional stiffness of the
shells; the gravity term, which acts through the level geometry $A$
and is the background gravity's (it survives the dead-gravity limit of
\S\ref{sec:dahlen}); and the forcing by the level integrals of the
potential. On a shell between two level walls, $S_c = S_i[\bu_s]$ is
constant above the inner wall, $S_c' = 0$, and the equation holds with
$U = V - S_i$ in the gravity term, the stiffness and forcing
unchanged (\S\ref{sec:shell}). Equation
\eqref{eq:V-ode-general} is also the $c$-derivative of the
budget \eqref{eq:G-band} with the hydrostatic density \eqref{eq:G-rho};
precisely, $\mathrm d(\Delta M)/\mathrm dc$ is $-A$ times its left-hand
side. The derivation above reaches it from the three laws of a body of
fluid. Nothing in it divides by $D$ or $N^2$: the closure is regular
where $D$ changes sign, that is where the stratification passes from
stable to unstable, and at $D = 0$.

\paragraph{End conditions, for any wall.} Integrating the compression
law \eqref{eq:compression} from the bottom,
\begin{equation}
  V(c) = -\int_{c_{\min}}^{c}\frac{A\,(f + \rho s)}{\kappa}\,\mathrm dc'
  = -\int_{B_c}\frac{\Delta p}{\kappa}\,\mathrm dV_0 ,
  \label{eq:Vsum}
\end{equation}
with $\Delta p = f + \rho s$ the pressure change following the fluid
and $\mathrm dV_0$ the background volume element. So $V(c)$ is the sum
of the adiabatic volume changes of the shells making up the body
$B_c$. This gives both end conditions at once:
\begin{equation}
  V(c_{\min}) = 0,\qquad V(c_{\mathrm{top}}) = S[\bu_s] .
  \label{eq:bcs}
\end{equation}
At the bottom, $B_c$ is empty. At the top, $B_c$ is all the fluid, and
the fluid fills its container, whose volume has changed by $S[\bu_s]$.
This is the \emph{fill condition}, in the form ``volume change of the
fluid equals volume change of the container''. It holds for every wall
shape, and for a lower boundary that is a centre or a wall of either
kind.

Passing to the limit $c \to c_{\mathrm{top}}$ through the linearisation
needs one estimate. The volume of the fluid with mass coordinate
between $M_0(c)$ and $M_F$ is $\int\mathrm dm/\rho$, taken over
parcels. The density of each parcel is $\rho(p, S)$ with its label
fixed and its pressure perturbed by $O(\varepsilon)$. So that volume
differs from its background value by at most $C\varepsilon\,(M_F -
M_0(c))$, uniformly. Hence the limits $c \to c_{\mathrm{top}}$ and
$\varepsilon \to 0$ commute. This is where the first-order band at a
level wall (\S\ref{sec:fluidatwall}) might have caused trouble, and
does not: $V$ counts bodies of fluid by mass, not by level.

For a top level wall the same condition can be read from the total
mass. Linearising $M_F =
\int_{\Omega_F}\hat\rho(\Phi)\dV$ gives $\int_F\rho_1\dV +
\rho_F\oint_\Sigma\bu_s\cdot\bn\dS = 0$, where $\rho_F := \rho(c_w)$.
Evaluating the volume integral by the coarea formula and the
linearised mass law gives $\rho_F\bar\Phi(c_w) + \delta M(c_w)$. Then
$\delta M = \rho(\delta\Vol - V)$ and $\delta\Vol(c_w) = -\bar\Phi(c_w)$
return $V(c_w) = S[\bu_s]$.

\begin{remark}[A dome-shaped top]
\label{rem:dome}
On a transverse wall the top of the fluid is a level point, where the
highest levels shrink to a point. Near a nondegenerate one, $A \simeq
\alpha t$ with $t := c_{\mathrm{top}} - c$ and $\alpha > 0$. Here
$\bar\Phi \to 0$ and $S_c \to S[\bu_s]$, so $\delta\Vol \to S[\bu_s]$.
Multiply \eqref{eq:closure} by $\alpha t^2$ and keep leading orders,
treating $\kappa$ and $\rho$ as constant:
\begin{equation*}
  -\kappa\,t\,V_{tt} + \kappa V_t + \rho V
  = \rho\,\bigl(\delta\Vol - t\,\delta\Vol_t\bigr) + \dots
\end{equation*}
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Indicial roots.} The homogeneous equation has roots $0$ and
  $2$, so both local solutions are bounded. The top is a regular
  singular point at which a Dirichlet condition is meaningful.
\item \emph{The fill condition is the condition for a finite
  pressure.} At $t = 0$ the equation reads $\kappa V_t(0) + \rho V(0) =
  \rho\,S[\bu_s]$, which fixes $V_t(0)$ in terms of $V(0)$ for either
  local solution. The pressure is best read from the two laws in the
  form $f' = -\rho f/\kappa - Ds$, which is \eqref{eq:Ds}, with $s =
  (\delta\Vol - V)/A \simeq (\delta\Vol - V)(c_{\mathrm{top}})/(\alpha
  t)$:
  \begin{equation*}
    f \simeq -\frac{D}{\alpha}\bigl(\delta\Vol - V\bigr)(c_{\mathrm{top}})\,\log t ,
    \qquad
    s \simeq \frac{(\delta\Vol - V)(c_{\mathrm{top}})}{\alpha t} .
  \end{equation*}
  So unless $V(c_{\mathrm{top}}) = \delta\Vol(c_{\mathrm{top}}) =
  S[\bu_s]$, the lift has a $1/t$ singularity and the pressure a
  logarithmic one: the missing volume would have to be made up by fluid
  of vanishing cross-section lifted by a diverging amount, and the
  buoyancy of that lift makes the pressure singular. (The two terms of
  $f = -\kappa V'/A - \rho s$ are each of order $1/t$ and cancel at
  leading order; the singularity is one order weaker than either.)
\item \emph{A neutral top.} For $D(c_{\mathrm{top}}) = 0$ the pressure is
  bounded for either local solution, and the fill condition must be
  imposed as the mass condition it is.
\end{itemize}
This makes precise the reading of the end condition at a transverse
wall as a regularity statement on $U$.
\end{remark}

\paragraph{The remaining fields.} Once $V$ is known, they follow
without division by anything that can vanish in the interior:
\begin{equation}
  s = \frac{\delta\Vol - V}{A},\qquad
  f = -\kappa\,\frac{V'}{A} - \rho s,\qquad
  p_1 = f - \rho\phi,\qquad
  \rho_1 = \rho'\phi - f' = \rho'(\phi + s) - \frac{\rho V'}{A} .
  \label{eq:V-dict}
\end{equation}
That $D$ appears nowhere is why the closure is regular across neutral
layers and across levels where the stratification changes sign. $D$
re-enters only when one asks about labels, through \eqref{eq:Ds}, or
about displacements (\S\ref{sec:realise}).

\begin{remark}[The sphere]
\label{rem:sphere}
On a spherically symmetric background, with a level wall, take a
perturbation of harmonic degree $\ell \ge 1$. Every level integral
vanishes, so $\bar\Phi = 0$, $\delta\Vol = 0$ and $S[\bu_s] = 0$. Then
\eqref{eq:closure}--\eqref{eq:bcs} give $V \equiv 0$, by the
uniqueness of \S\ref{sec:uniqueness}. Hence $s = 0$, $f = 0$, $p_1 =
-\rho\phi$ and $\rho_1 = \rho'\phi$, which are Dahlen's relations. At
degree zero, \eqref{eq:closure} is non-trivial and supplies the physics
that the reduction alone omits; \S\ref{sec:dahlen} works both cases
out.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Disconnected levels: the closure on the tree}
\label{sec:tree}

\status{derived. Not built (\S\ref{sec:implementation}).}

When the wall is transverse and not level over a range of levels, as
it is for a non-hydrostatic solid, the level sets in the fluid are
generically disconnected over the band of levels between
$\min\Phi_0|_\Sigma$ and $\max\Phi_0|_\Sigma$, and (A2b) fails in the
background. Nothing above breaks, because every relation is local on a
connected component; what is needed is bookkeeping on the Reeb tree of
\S\ref{sec:foliation}, given here. The sublevel regions $R_c$ stay
connected; it is the level surfaces that disconnect.

\paragraph{The tree.} The level set $L_c$ is the level surface
restricted to where the wall lies above it, and that region shrinks as
$c$ rises, so components can only split or vanish going up, never
merge. The components therefore form a rooted tree $\mathcal T$: a
trunk from $c_{\min}$ to the first saddle of $\Phi_0|_\Sigma$, then
branches ending at the local maxima of $\Phi_0|_\Sigma$, dome-shaped
tops where the component shrinks to a point; at an inner wall the
mirror image hangs downward. For an edge $e$ with component $L^e_c$ on
$c_e^- < c < c_e^+$, write $A_e$ for its weighted area and $T_e$ for its
tube; the tubes partition the fluid.

\paragraph{The lift, read materially.} The lift $s$ of
\S\ref{sec:lift} is defined through amounts, the mass below a level,
and its reading as the level shift of the fluid of a given mass
coordinate assumes that fluid enters the body counted only through its
top. On a branch, fluid also crosses the saddle at its base, and the
amount-based lift then differs from the parcels' motion by the inflow
divided by the local area, which diverges at the branch's top. On an
edge let $\sigma_e(c)$ be the \emph{material lift}, the level shift of
the parcels on $L^e_c$ (a level function on each component where $D
\ne 0$, by the argument of \S\ref{sec:realise}; the subscript
distinguishes it from the sheet density $\sigma$ of
\S\ref{sec:levelwalls}). With the tube's base fixed at the background
vertex value $c_e^-$, let $\delta\Vol_e(c) = -\bar\Phi_e(c) +
\bar\Phi_e(c_e^-) + S^e_c[\bu_s]$ be the change of the tube's volume
below $c$, and
\begin{equation*}
  \Vt_e := \delta\Vol_e + \sigma_eA_e ,
\end{equation*}
the volume change of the parcels originally in the tube below $c$ plus
the volume that has entered the tube across its base. The equation of
state with labels carried gives $\delta\hat\rho_e = \rho f_e/\kappa -
D\sigma_e$; the mass of a slab of the component, equal to the
background mass of the slab its parcels came from, gives
$A_e\delta\hat\rho_e = -\rho\,\delta\Vol_e' - (\rho A_e\sigma_e)'$; and
hydrostatics gives $f_e' = -\delta\hat\rho_e$. Eliminating
$\delta\hat\rho_e$, the $\rho'\sigma_e$ terms cancel and the three laws
of \S\ref{sec:threelaws} return with $s = -\sigma_e$ and $V = \Vt_e$, so
the closure \eqref{eq:closure} holds on every edge with $\Vt_e$ in
place of $V$. On the trunk, where nothing crosses the base, $\Vt = V$.

\paragraph{End and junction conditions.} At the root, $\Vt(c_{\min}) =
0$. At a leaf, $A_e \to 0$ with $\sigma_e$ bounded, so $\Vt_e(c_e^+) =
\delta\Vol_e(c_e^+)$: the branch's parcels and its inflow fill the
branch, which by Remark~\ref{rem:dome} is also the condition for a
bounded pressure there. At a vertex of value $c_v$ with lower edges $e$
and upper edges $e'$, the lower edges' parcels pushed above the fixed
level $c_v$, of volume $\sigma_e(c_v^-)A_e(c_v) = \Vt_e(c_v^-) -
\delta\Vol_e(c_v)$, occupy the slabs between the upper tubes' fixed
bases and their material base surfaces, of volume $\Vt_{e'}(c_v^+)$;
parcel volumes being preserved to first order,
\begin{equation}
  \sum_{e\ \text{below}}\bigl[\Vt_e(c_v^-) - \delta\Vol_e(c_v)\bigr]
  = \sum_{e'\ \text{above}}\Vt_{e'}(c_v^+) ,
  \label{eq:vertex}
\end{equation}
and the pressure $f_e(c_v)$ is the same on every edge at the vertex.
With one second-order equation per edge, these conditions are exactly
as many as the unknowns. Two checks: a uniform shift of the potential,
under which nothing moves, satisfies all of them with $\sigma_e \equiv
\phi_0$; and for an incompressible fluid with one pocket's wall moving
out by $S^1$ and another's in by $-S^1$ they give the inflow $S^1$ into
the first pocket and a bounded lift of its parcels. Summed over the
tree they reproduce the global fill; the wall at a point of tube $e$
feels $f_e(\Phi_0(\bx))$.

\paragraph{The variational form.} The energy of \S\ref{sec:energy1d} is
a sum over tubes, so its second variation is $\sum_eJ_e[\Vt_e]$. With
the root, leaf and vertex volume conditions essential, varying the
upper edges' free base values gives, through the boundary terms
$\partial L/\partial\Vt' = -f$, pressure continuity at the vertex as
the natural condition. The bordered system of \S\ref{sec:weak} then
carries one one-dimensional unknown per edge and one constraint with
its pressure multiplier per vertex and per leaf; existence and
uniqueness for $D \le 0$ follow as in \S\ref{sec:uniqueness}.

\paragraph{What the background must supply.} The inventory fixes
amounts only; two pockets at the same level are hydrostatically
isolated and may hold different fluid. This is the fibred Moser
argument of \S\ref{sec:allconserved} failing for want of matching fibre
topology: equal residue integrals on surfaces that are not
diffeomorphic. The background must therefore specify the arrangement
among branches (\S\ref{sec:arrangement}). A single-valued
$\hat\rho(c)$, with labels continuous across the saddles, is the
natural choice;
(A4) is then applied edge by edge. The band at a deformed level wall
(\S\ref{sec:fluidatwall}) is this structure at $O(\varepsilon)$ width.
Whether to treat a core--mantle boundary with kilometre-scale
topography as level, carrying the band as the sheet with errors of
order topography over radius, or to resolve the tree with one short
edge per pocket, is a modelling choice; mass conservation to $10^{-6}$
needs the latter.

%=====================================================================
\section{What the solid feels}
\label{sec:feels}

\status{derived.}

The pressure at a wall point $\bx$ is $\hat p$ evaluated at the
geopotential there. Following the wall point, the pressure perturbation
is
\begin{equation}
  \Delta p_{\mathrm{wall}}(\bx) = p_1 + \bu_s\cdot\nabla p_0
  = f\bigl(\Phi_0(\bx)\bigr)
  - \rho\bigl(\Phi_0(\bx)\bigr)\bigl(\phi + \bu_s\cdot\nabla\Phi_0\bigr) .
  \label{eq:wallp}
\end{equation}
Each wall point feels the pressure of its own level. On a transverse
wall the first term therefore varies along the wall. On a level wall it
is a constant, $f(c_w)$, the multiplier of the fill constraint
(\S\ref{sec:weak}). The second term is the $\bv$-half of $F3$, the
pressure $-\rho_F\phi$, and the third is the buoyancy of the displaced
wall, $F2$ (both displayed in \S\ref{sec:dahlen}). With the border,
$F3$ and $F2$, the solid therefore feels the Lagrangian pressure
perturbation at the displaced wall: $f$ from the border, $-\rho_F\phi$
from $F3$, and $-\rho_F\,\bu_s\cdot\nabla\Phi_0$ from $F2$.

For gravity, the fluid contributes its regular Eulerian density
$\rho_1 = \rho'\phi + \delta\hat\rho$ inside $F$. On the wall there is
the ordinary surface term of a moving interface,
$\bigl(\rho(\Phi_0) - \rho_S\bigr)\,\bu_s\cdot\bn$. Its fluid half,
$\rho(\Phi_0)\,\bu_s\cdot\bn$, is the $\chi$-half of $F3$. Its solid
half sits inside the solid's volume coupling: integrating by parts,
\begin{equation*}
  \int_{\text{solid}}\rho\,\nabla\chi\cdot\bu_s\dV
  = -\int_{\text{solid}}\chi\,\Div(\rho\bu_s)\dV
    - \int_\Sigma\rho_S\,\chi\,(\bu_s\cdot\bn)\dS
    + (\text{outer surface}) ,
\end{equation*}
so that, together with $F3$, the wall carries the density-jump term
$(\rho_F - \rho_S)\,\bu_s\cdot\bn$ in the potential row, exactly the
term a displacement continuous across the wall would produce. There is
no other surface term, on a wall of either kind. The state of the
fluid carries no sheet.

The fill constraint and its multiplier are one piece of bookkeeping:
the end condition of the body of fluid, and the force with which the
fluid holds the wall to it. The pair is needed to make the system
square: the wall pressure acts on the solid, so it is an unknown with a
column in the solid's row, and the fill condition \eqref{eq:bcs} is the
row that determines it.

%=====================================================================
\section{The fluid's energy, and the closure as its Euler--Lagrange equation}
\label{sec:var}

\status{derived, except that \S\ref{sec:eulerianform} takes the classical
second variation \eqref{eq:E2xi} as given, and the stability remarks of
\S\ref{sec:uniqueness} on unstable stratification are argued.}

The closure \eqref{eq:closure} was derived in \S\ref{sec:lin} from three
laws for a body of fluid. This section shows that it is also the
Euler--Lagrange equation of the fluid's own energy, written at fixed
level geometry. That settles existence and uniqueness for stable
stratification, explains why $D$ never appears, and generates every
term of the bordered weak form of the coupled problem, including the
one that a construction by requirements has to introduce by hand.

\subsection{The fluid's energy at fixed level geometry}
\label{sec:energy1d}

Hold the geopotential $\Phi$ and the wall fixed, so that the level
geometry $\Vol(c)$, and with it $A = \Vol'$, is given. By
\S\ref{sec:exactgraph} an equilibrium state of the fluid is then
determined by its \emph{level-of-mass map} $c(m)$, $0 \le m \le M_F$,
the inverse of $M(c)$: the level at which the fluid of mass coordinate
$m$ sits. Its density follows from \eqref{eq:Mprime}, which says that
$\hat\rho$ is the ratio of the push-forwards $\Phi_*\varpi/\Phi_*\vol$:
\begin{equation*}
  \hat\rho(m) = \frac{1}{A(c(m))\,c'(m)} ,
\end{equation*}
where in this subsection a prime on $c$ denotes $\mathrm d/\mathrm dm$.
The fluid's energy in the given potential is its internal energy plus
its potential energy, and both are integrals over mass:
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal E_F[c] := \int_0^{M_F}\Bigl(e\bigl(\hat\rho(m), \Lambda(m)\bigr)
  + c(m)\Bigr)\,\mathrm dm .
  \label{eq:EF}
\end{equation}
This is the functional \eqref{eq:Ev} of \S\ref{sec:NL-variational},
written in the level-of-mass map instead of the volume-of-mass map $v =
\Vol\circ c$. The first term is $\int\rho e\dV$ and the second
$\int\rho\Phi\dV$, by the change of variables $\mathrm dm = \rho\dV$.
The potential energy is counted at full weight, not half, because this
is the fluid's energy at fixed potential; the self-consistency of the
potential is restored by Poisson's equation, which in the mixed
organisation of \S\ref{sec:weak} is the potential's own stationarity
condition. The fill condition is the pair of end conditions $c(0) =
c_{\min}$ and $c(M_F) = c_{\max}$: the fluid's lowest and highest levels
are the container's.

\emph{First variation.} Vary $c$ by $\delta c$ vanishing at the ends.
With $\partial_\rho e = p/\rho^2$ and $\delta\hat\rho =
-\hat\rho^2\,\delta(Ac') = -\hat\rho^2\bigl(A'c'\,\delta c + A\,\delta
c'\bigr)$,
\begin{equation*}
  \delta\mathcal E_F
  = \int_0^{M_F}\Bigl(-p\bigl(A'c'\,\delta c + A\,\delta c'\bigr) + \delta c\Bigr)\mathrm dm
  = \int_0^{M_F}\Bigl(A\,\frac{\mathrm dp}{\mathrm dm} + 1\Bigr)\delta c\,\mathrm dm ,
\end{equation*}
after integrating the $\delta c'$ term by parts; the $pA'c'$ terms
cancel. Stationarity gives $A\,\mathrm dp/\mathrm dm = -1$, that is
$\mathrm dp/\mathrm dc = -\hat\rho$, which is hydrostatic balance in the
form \eqref{eq:profiles}. So the one-dimensional problem
\eqref{eq:exact} is the stationarity of \eqref{eq:EF}: the fluid
chooses the level-of-mass map that makes its energy stationary in the
given container.

\subsection{The second variation, and the closure}
\label{sec:secondvar}

Now perturb both the map and the geometry. Write the perturbed map as
$c(m) = c_0(m) - \varepsilon s$, with $s$ the lift of \S\ref{sec:lift}
regarded as a function of the background level, and the perturbed
geometry as $A \to A + \varepsilon\,\delta\Vol'$. Let $\ell :=
\delta\ln\hat\rho$ be the change in log-density following a mass
element, expanded as $\varepsilon\ell_1 + \varepsilon^2\ell_2$.
Expanding $\ln\hat\rho = -\ln A(c) - \ln c'$ gives
\begin{equation}
  \ell_1 = \frac{(As)' - \delta\Vol'}{A} = -\frac{V'}{A},\qquad
  \ell_2 - \tfrac12\ell_1^2 = -\frac{1}{2A}\bigl(A's^2 - 2\,\delta\Vol'\,s\bigr)' ,
  \label{eq:ell}
\end{equation}
with $V = \delta\Vol - sA$ as in \eqref{eq:Vdef} and primes now
$\mathrm d/\mathrm dc$. The first identity is the compression law
\eqref{eq:compression} read as $\ell_1 = \Delta p/\kappa$: the
fractional density change of a shell is minus its fractional volume
change.

The second-order change of the energy per unit mass is $\partial_\rho
e\,\delta_2\hat\rho + \tfrac12\partial_{\rho\rho}e\,(\delta_1\hat\rho)^2$,
and the potential energy $\int c\,\mathrm dm$ has no second-order part.
With $p = \rho^2\partial_\rho e$ and $\kappa = \rho\,\partial_\rho p = 2p
+ \rho^3\partial_{\rho\rho}e$, this is
\begin{equation*}
  \frac{p}{\hat\rho}\Bigl(\ell_2 - \tfrac12\ell_1^2\Bigr)
  + \frac{\kappa}{2\hat\rho}\,\ell_1^2 .
\end{equation*}
Multiply by $\mathrm dm = \rho A\dc$, insert \eqref{eq:ell}, and
integrate the first term by parts using $p' = -\rho$:
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal E_{F,2} = \frac12\int\frac{\kappa V'^2}{A}\dc
  - \frac12\int\rho A's^2\dc + \int\rho\,\delta\Vol'\,s\dc
  + (\text{boundary terms}) .
  \label{eq:E2s}
\end{equation}
Substituting $s = (\delta\Vol - V)/A$ and discarding terms independent
of $V$ and total derivatives, this is
\begin{equation}
  J[V] := \frac12\int_{c_{\min}}^{c_{\mathrm{top}}}\Bigl(\frac{\kappa V'^2}{A}
  + \rho\Bigl(\frac1A\Bigr)'V^2\Bigr)\dc
  - \int_{c_{\min}}^{c_{\mathrm{top}}}\rho\Bigl(\frac{\delta\Vol}{A}\Bigr)'V\dc .
  \label{eq:J}
\end{equation}
Its Euler--Lagrange equation is exactly the closure \eqref{eq:closure},
and its end conditions \eqref{eq:bcs} are the linearised fill
conditions $c(0) = c_{\min}$, $c(M_F) = c_{\max}$. (Both the identities
\eqref{eq:ell} and the agreement of the Euler--Lagrange equation of
\eqref{eq:E2s} with \eqref{eq:closure} were checked symbolically.)

So the closure is not a mass budget imposed from outside. It is the
stationarity of the fluid's own energy, at fixed level geometry, with
respect to how the fluid distributes its mass among the levels. The two
terms of the quadratic part of \eqref{eq:J} are the compression energy
of the shells and the work the pressure does as the shells' volumes
change with level, the latter being the only place the geometry's
variation with level enters. $D$ does not appear because nothing in
\eqref{eq:EF} knows about labels beyond the fixed profile $\Lambda$; the
buoyancy of a lifted parcel is a property of the Eulerian description,
and the next subsection shows where it goes.

\subsection{The Eulerian form, and where \texorpdfstring{$F1$ and $F3$}{F1 and F3} come from}
\label{sec:eulerianform}
\label{sec:NL-second}

The second variation of the whole energy in displacement form is the
classical one. Its fluid part, with $\bxi$ a fluid displacement and
$\xi_\nu := \bxi\cdot\nuh$, is
\begin{equation}
  E_2^{\mathrm{fluid}}[\bxi]
  = \frac12\int_F\kappa\Bigl(\Div\bxi - \frac{\rho g\,\xi_\nu}{\kappa}\Bigr)^2\dV
  + \frac12\int_F\rho N^2\xi_\nu^2\dV
  + \int_F\rho\,\bxi\cdot\nabla\phi\dV ,
  \label{eq:E2xi}
\end{equation}
to which the potential's own term $\tfrac{1}{8\pi G}\int|\nabla\phi|^2$
is added in the mixed organisation. The four terms are the acoustic
square, the buoyancy term, the gravitational coupling and the
potential's energy. The first is $\tfrac12\int p_1^2/\kappa$, since $p_1
= \Delta p - \bxi\cdot\nabla p_0 = -\kappa\Div\bxi + \rho g\xi_\nu$; the
buoyancy term is $\tfrac12\int\rho N^2\xi_\nu^2 = -\tfrac12\int D\,
(g\xi_\nu)^2$, the energy of moving material across the stratification
against the actual-minus-adiabatic gradient. We take \eqref{eq:E2xi} as
given and restrict it to the equilibrium family, parametrised by $V$
through $s$ and $f$, by substituting $p_1 = f - \rho\phi$, $g\xi_\nu =
-(\phi + s)$ and, in the coupling term after one integration by parts,
the kinematic density $\rho_1 = -\Div(\rho\bxi) = \rho'\phi + \rho
f/\kappa + Ds$ (not its hydrostatic form $\rho'\phi - f'$: that would be
substituting an Euler--Lagrange equation into the functional before
varying). The cross terms between $\bar\Phi$ and $(f, s)$ from the
three terms cancel exactly, and what remains is
\begin{equation}
  E_2^{\mathrm{fluid}}
  = \frac12\int_F\rho'\phi^2\dV
  + \oint_\Sigma\rho_F\,\phi\,\bu_s\cdot\bn\dS
  + \frac12\int_{c_{\min}}^{c_{\mathrm{top}}}A\Bigl(\frac{f^2}{\kappa} - D\,s^2\Bigr)\dc .
  \label{eq:E2state}
\end{equation}
The three terms are, in order, $F1$, the $\chi$-half of $F3$, and a
second form of the fluid's quadratic functional. That the two forms
agree is the identity
\begin{equation}
  A\Bigl(\frac{f^2}{\kappa} - Ds^2\Bigr)
  = \frac{\kappa V'^2}{A} + \rho\Bigl(\frac1A\Bigr)'V^2
  - \Bigl(\frac{\rho V^2}{A}\Bigr)'
  \qquad(\delta\Vol = 0),
  \label{eq:nulllag}
\end{equation}
with the forced versions differing by a null Lagrangian, a total
derivative plus terms independent of $V$. The $D$-dependence of the
energy is therefore a null Lagrangian. \emph{That is the precise reason
why the closure, which is the common Euler--Lagrange equation, is
$D$-free, while the energy in its Eulerian form is not.}

The form \eqref{eq:E2state} also has a direct reading: the fluid's
energy at fixed potential is the compression energy of its pressure
profile, $\tfrac12\int Af^2/\kappa$, minus the buoyancy energy of its
lift, $\tfrac12\int ADs^2$, the latter positive for stable
stratification; the potential enters the fluid's own terms only
through $\delta\Vol$, which is the geometry. At a neutral level $D = 0$
and the lift costs nothing, which is the class-K freedom of
\S\ref{sec:realise}.

\paragraph{The evaluation, term by term (derived).} With $\Theta :=
\Div\bxi - \rho g\,\xi_\nu/\kappa$, so that $\kappa\Theta = -p_1 = \rho\phi
- f$, and the realising family of \S\ref{sec:realise} ($g\xi_\nu =
-(\phi + s)$, $\Div\bxi = V'/A$), the three terms of \eqref{eq:E2xi}
are, using the coarea formula on every level-constant factor,
\begin{align*}
  \int_F\frac{\kappa\Theta^2}{2}\dV
  &= \frac12\int_F\frac{\rho^2\phi^2}{\kappa}\dV
   - \int\frac{\rho f}{\kappa}\,\bar\Phi\dc
   + \frac12\int\frac{f^2}{\kappa}\,A\dc ,\\
  \int_F\frac{\rho N^2\xi_\nu^2}{2}\dV
  &= -\frac12\int_F D\,\phi^2\dV
   - \int D\,s\,\bar\Phi\dc
   - \frac12\int D\,s^2\,A\dc ,\\
  \int_F\rho\,\bxi\cdot\nabla\phi\dV
  &= \int_F\rho'\,\phi^2\dV
   + \int\rho'\,s\,\bar\Phi\dc
   - \int\frac{\rho V'}{A}\,\bar\Phi\dc
   + \oint_\Sigma\rho_F\,\phi\,(\bu_s\cdot\bn)\dS ,
\end{align*}
the second using $\rho N^2\xi_\nu^2 = -D\,(\phi + s)^2$, the third
after one integration by parts, $\int\rho\,\bxi\cdot\nabla\phi =
\int\phi\,\rho_1 + \oint\rho_F\phi\,\bxi\cdot\bn$, with the kinematic
density $\rho_1 = \rho'(\phi + s) - \rho V'/A$ and the \emph{welded}
trace $\bxi\cdot\bn = \bu_s\cdot\bn$. (At a stratified level wall no
regular welded field realises the state; the trace is that of the
welded realising sequence of Proposition~\ref{prop:layer}, along which
the divergence and normal component converge and with them the energy,
Remark~\ref{rem:energy}. No surface density enters the density.) Three
cancellations assemble the sum. The pointwise $\phi^2$ terms give
$\tfrac12\rho^2/\kappa - \tfrac12D + \rho' = \tfrac12\rho'$, which is
$F1$'s quadratic part. The $\bar\Phi$ cross terms give $-\rho f/\kappa -
Ds + \rho's - \rho V'/A = -(\rho/\kappa)(f + \rho s) - \rho V'/A = 0$, by
the compression law alone. The pure one-dimensional terms are
$\tfrac12\int A(f^2/\kappa - Ds^2)\dc$. That is \eqref{eq:E2state}.

\paragraph{Two further identifications (derived).} Stationarity of
\eqref{eq:E2xi} under along-level variations alone ($\delta\bxi$
tangent to the levels, $\delta\xi_\nu = 0$) gives, after one
integration by parts and using the level-constancy of $\rho$,
$\nabla_{\!\parallel}(\kappa\Theta - \rho\phi) = \mathbf 0$, that is $p_1
+ \rho\phi = f(\Phi_0)$: the free function is the first integral of
the tangential stationarity, undetermined by construction. And the
full Euler--Lagrange equation of \eqref{eq:E2xi} in $\bxi$ is
\begin{equation*}
  -\nabla(\kappa\Theta) - \bigl(\rho g\,\Theta - \rho N^2\xi_\nu\bigr)\nuh
  + \rho\,\nabla\phi = \mathbf 0 ,
\end{equation*}
which with $\kappa\Theta = -p_1$ and $\rho_1 = -\Div(\rho\bxi) =
-\rho\Theta - gD\,\xi_\nu$ is exactly the linearised momentum balance
\eqref{eq:momentum}. The realising fields are therefore stationary
points of $E_2^{\mathrm{fluid}}$ against every smooth interior
variation, whatever the sign of $N^2$.

\paragraph{The Schur complement, as a corollary.} The $D$-freeness of
the closure can also be read from a Schur complement:
stationarity of \eqref{eq:E2xi} in the pointwise normal displacement at
fixed $(\phi, s, \bu_s)$ gives the slaving $g\xi_\nu = -(\phi + s)$,
exactly when $N^2 \ne 0$, and eliminating that block, positive for $N^2
> 0$ and negative for $N^2 < 0$, leaves the closure. That is a
corollary of \eqref{eq:nulllag}: the eliminated block's $D$-dependence
survives in the reduced energy only as a null Lagrangian, so the
reduced Euler--Lagrange equation is the same object for every $D$. At
$D = 0$ the eliminated block is singular, $\xi_\nu$ joins the kernel
(class K), and the level-mean sector keeps its compressional stiffness.
The ``wall's quadratic bookkeeping'' is
settled the same way: the rank-one wall term of the bordered functional
is the boundary term of \eqref{eq:nulllag} (\S\ref{sec:weak}), and $F2$
is the buoyancy term of the wall pressure \eqref{eq:wallp}.

\subsection{The bordered weak form of the coupled problem}
\label{sec:bordered}
\label{sec:weak}

Let $\Ltot_D[\bu_s, \phi]$ be the quadratic functional of the solid and
the potential in the mixed organisation, with $F1$, $F2$ and $F3$
included: one half of the solid's elastic-gravitational form, plus
$\tfrac{1}{8\pi G}\int|\nabla\phi|^2$ with the exterior condition, plus
the solid--potential coupling, plus $\tfrac12\int_F\rho'\phi^2\dV$, plus
the wall terms $F2$ and $F3$ of \S\ref{sec:feels}. Its stationarity in
$(\bu_s, \phi)$ is Dahlen's reduction (\S\ref{sec:dahlen}). The closure
is obtained by adding the fluid's functional and the fill constraint:
\begin{equation}
  \Ltot[\bu_s, \phi, V, \lambda] := \Ltot_D[\bu_s, \phi]
  + J\bigl[V; \delta\Vol(\phi, \bu_s)\bigr]
  + \lambda\bigl(V(c_{\mathrm{top}}) - S[\bu_s]\bigr)
  - \ell_u(\bu_s) - \ell_\phi(\phi),
  \label{eq:Lv}
\end{equation}
with $V(c_{\min}) = 0$ essential, $J$ as in \eqref{eq:J} or
equivalently \eqref{eq:E2state}, $\delta\Vol = -\bar\Phi +
S_c[\bu_s]$, and $\ell_u$, $\ell_\phi$ the load functionals of a surface
load or a tidal potential. Its rows, with test functions $\bv$, $\chi$,
$W$ ($W(c_{\min}) = 0$) and $\bar X := \bar\chi$, follow from the
derivatives
\begin{equation*}
  \frac{\partial J}{\partial(\delta\Vol)}
  = -\Bigl(\frac{\rho f}{\kappa} + Ds\Bigr) = -\delta\hat\rho,
  \qquad
  \frac{\partial L}{\partial V'} = -f ,
\end{equation*}
where $L$ is the integrand of $J$ in the form \eqref{eq:E2state}:
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(P)] \emph{Potential row.} The mixed potential row, plus
  $\int\delta\hat\rho\,\bar X\dc$, equals $\ell_\phi(\chi)$. Since
  $\rho_1 = \rho'\phi + \delta\hat\rho$, this is Poisson's equation with
  the full density perturbation of \S\ref{sec:lin} in place of $F1$'s
  $\rho'\phi$ alone.
\item[(V)] \emph{Border row.} With $\delta f = -\kappa W'/A + \rho W/A$
  and $\delta s = -W/A$,
  \begin{equation*}
    -\int f\,W'\dc + \int\delta\hat\rho\,W\dc + \lambda\,W(c_{\mathrm{top}}) = 0 ,
  \end{equation*}
  in which $f$ and $\delta\hat\rho$ are the expressions in $V$ and
  $\delta\Vol$ of \S\ref{sec:lin}. Integrating by parts gives $f' =
  -\delta\hat\rho$ in the interior, which is hydrostatics and hence the
  closure, and at the top the natural condition $\partial L/\partial V'
  + \lambda = 0$, that is
  \begin{equation}
    \lambda = f(c_{\mathrm{top}}) .
    \label{eq:lambda-top}
  \end{equation}
  The multiplier of the fill constraint is the pressure perturbation at
  the top level.
\item[(S)] \emph{Solid row.} The mixed solid row, plus $-\lambda S[\bv]$
  from the constraint, plus, on a transverse wall, $-\int\delta\hat\rho\,
  S_c[\bv]\dc$ from $J$'s dependence on $S_c[\bu_s]$. With $\delta\hat\rho
  = -f'$ and \eqref{eq:lambda-top}, integrating by parts in $c$ and using
  the coarea formula on the wall turns these two into
  \begin{equation*}
    -f(c_{\mathrm{top}})S[\bv] + \int f'S_c[\bv]\dc
    = -\int_\Sigma f\bigl(\Phi_0(\bx)\bigr)\,\bv\cdot\bn\dS ,
  \end{equation*}
  the pointwise wall pressure of \S\ref{sec:feels}. On a level wall only
  the first term is present. This is the transverse-wall coupling,
  written as a row of the functional.
\item[(C)] \emph{Constraint.} $V(c_{\mathrm{top}}) - S[\bu_s] = 0$.
\end{enumerate}
The system is symmetric by construction. On a level wall, $J$'s
$\phi$-dependence enters (P) through $\bar X$ and its $\bu_s$-dependence
only through the constraint. Writing $J$ in the $D$-free form
\eqref{eq:J} instead changes the rows by boundary terms only, and shifts
the multiplier by $\rho_Fs(c_{\mathrm{top}})$; the solutions are the
same.

\paragraph{The level-wall form written out.} On a level wall, expanding
\eqref{eq:E2state} with $\delta\Vol = -\bar\Phi$ gives the border terms
in the form the one-dimensional prototype assembles
(\S\ref{sec:verification}). With integrals over $(c_{\min}, c_w)$ and
$A_w = A(c_w)$,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
  L = L_D(\bu_s,\phi)
  &+ \frac12\int\frac{\kappa V'^2}{A}\dc
   + \frac12\int\rho\Bigl(\frac1A\Bigr)'V^2\dc
   - \int\Bigl(\frac{\rho'\bar\Phi V}{A}
            + \frac{\rho\,\bar\Phi V'}{A}\Bigr)\dc
   - \frac12\int\frac{\rho'\bar\Phi^2}{A}\dc \\
  &- \frac{\rho_F}{2A_w}\,S[\bu_s]^2
   + \lambda\bigl(V(c_w) - S[\bu_s]\bigr) - \text{loads},
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:L}
\end{equation}
with $V(c_{\min}) = 0$ essential, $L_D$ the mixed functional of
\S\ref{sec:dahlen}, and ``loads'' $= \ell_u(\bu_s) + \ell_\phi(\phi)$.
Its rows are
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
  \text{(S)}&\quad
  \text{mixed solid row} - \lambda\,S[\bv]
  - \frac{\rho_F}{A_w}S[\bu_s]\,S[\bv] = \ell_u(\bv),\\
  \text{(P)}&\quad
  \text{mixed potential row}
  - \int\frac{\rho'\bar\Phi\bar X}{A}\dc
  - \int\Bigl(\frac{\rho' V}{A} + \frac{\rho V'}{A}\Bigr)\bar X\dc
  = \ell_\phi(\chi),\\
  \text{(V)}&\quad
  \int\frac{\kappa V'W'}{A}\dc + \int\rho\Bigl(\frac1A\Bigr)'VW\dc
  - \int\Bigl(\frac{\rho'\bar\Phi W}{A} + \frac{\rho\,\bar\Phi W'}{A}\Bigr)\dc
  + \lambda\,W(c_w) = 0,\\
  \text{(C)}&\quad V(c_w) - S[\bu_s] = 0 .
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:rows}
\end{equation}
The (V)--(P) coupling is one bilinear form, $-\int(\rho' V/A + \rho
V'/A)\bar X\dc$, read both ways. The first three groups of border terms
in \eqref{eq:L} are \eqref{eq:E2state} expanded term by term; the
$\bar\Phi^2$ term is the degree-0 part of $F1$ that the slaved density
does not supply. The fourth, $-(\rho_F/2A_w)S[\bu_s]^2$, which the 23
October note introduced as ``the boundary term at the wall that a
symmetric treatment of the gravity term leaves behind'', is the
boundary term of the null Lagrangian in \eqref{eq:nulllag},
$-\tfrac12\rho_FV(c_w)^2/A_w$, read on the constraint set. Written on
the border side it makes the multiplier $f(c_w)$, as in
\eqref{eq:lambda-top}; written on the solid side, as in \eqref{eq:L},
it makes the multiplier
\begin{equation}
  \lambda = -\frac{\kappa V'(c_w)}{A_w} + \frac{\rho_F\bar\Phi(c_w)}{A_w}
  = f(c_w) - \frac{\rho_F S[\bu_s]}{A_w} ,
  \label{eq:lambda}
\end{equation}
the natural condition of the (V) row of \eqref{eq:rows}, the second
form following from \eqref{eq:V-dict} at $c_w$ on the constraint set,
$s(c_w) = -(\bar\Phi(c_w) + S[\bu_s])/A_w$. In the (S) row, therefore,
$-\lambda S[\bv] - (\rho_F/A_w)S[\bu_s]S[\bv] = -f(c_w)S[\bv]$: the solid
feels exactly the wall pressure $f(c_w)$. The two forms give the same
solution.

\paragraph{The rank-one term from the built form.} The same boundary
term appears when \eqref{eq:L} is derived from the built form
\eqref{eq:L-built} of \S\ref{sec:implementation}, which keeps the lift
$s$ as an unknown. On the constraint set of \eqref{eq:L-built} the lift
is $s = -(\bar\Phi + U)/A$, with $U = V$ at a single level wall.
Substitute it into the two lift terms of \eqref{eq:L-built}:
\begin{equation*}
  \int\rho\,s\,U'\dc - \frac12\int\rho' A\,s^2\dc
  = -\int\frac{\rho\,UU'}{A}\dc - \int\frac{\rho\,\bar\Phi U'}{A}\dc
    - \frac12\int\frac{\rho'\bigl(\bar\Phi + U\bigr)^2}{A}\dc .
\end{equation*}
Integrate the first term by parts, with $UU' = \tfrac12(U^2)'$ and
$U(c_{\min}) = 0$:
\begin{equation*}
  -\frac12\int\frac{\rho\,(U^2)'}{A}\dc
  = -\frac{\rho_F\,U(c_w)^2}{2A_w}
    + \frac12\int\frac{\rho' U^2}{A}\dc
    + \frac12\int\rho\Bigl(\frac1A\Bigr)'U^2\dc .
\end{equation*}
The $\rho' U^2$ term cancels the expanded square's $U^2$ part, and what
remains is exactly the border terms of \eqref{eq:L} --- the gravity
term, the two coupling terms and the $\bar\Phi^2$ term, with $V = U$ ---
plus the boundary term $-\tfrac12\rho_FU(c_w)^2/A_w$. That is why the
built border's multiplier is $f$ itself (\S\ref{sec:implementation}).

\subsection{Existence, uniqueness and stability}
\label{sec:uniqueness}
\label{sec:settled}

\paragraph{The fluid block.} By \eqref{eq:E2state} with $\delta\Vol =
0$, $J[V] = \tfrac12\int A(f^2/\kappa - Ds^2)\dc$ on the homogeneous
problem. For $D \le 0$ everywhere, neutral layers included, $J \ge 0$.
A solution of the homogeneous closure with $V = 0$ at both ends has
$J[V] = 0$, hence $f \equiv 0$, hence $V' = \rho V/\kappa$, hence $V
\equiv 0$. Coercivity in the natural weighted space follows from the
substitution $W = V\exp(-\int\rho/\kappa)$, under which $\int
Af^2/\kappa = \int\kappa e^{2\int\rho/\kappa}W'^2/A$, and Poincar\'e's
inequality with $W(c_{\min}) = 0$; the weights near a centre or a dome
top are handled by the local behaviour of Remark~\ref{rem:dome}. So for
stable or neutral stratification the closure has a unique solution
for every forcing, by the Lax--Milgram theorem. This is a theorem.

For $D > 0$ somewhere, $J$ is indefinite, and uniqueness holds exactly
when the Sturm--Liouville operator of \eqref{eq:closure} has no zero
eigenvalue with Dirichlet ends. A zero eigenvalue is a secular
instability of the fluid at frozen gravity: a rearrangement of mass
among the levels that costs no energy. On a uniform compressible
sphere, $D = \rho^2/\kappa > 0$, the $D$-free form gives, with $V =
4\pi r^2u_r$,
\begin{equation*}
  J[V] = 2\pi\int\Bigl(\kappa\,(\Div\bu)^2 - \frac{\rho g}{r}\,u_r^2\Bigr)r^2\,\mathrm dr ,
\end{equation*}
positive for Earth-like values of $\kappa$ but not for all parameters.
So \emph{the equation is $D$-free, the energy is not}: for $D \le 0$
coercivity is a theorem; for $D > 0$ it is a check, which the uniform
compressible sphere can fail at low $\kappa$. In the neutral case $J =
\tfrac12\int Af^2/\kappa$, and uniqueness rests on the compression term
alone. (The check is built as a probe of the border's fluid block,
\S\ref{sec:verification}, restricted to the interior nodes. The
restriction matters: with the end values free, the form at $D = 0$ is
$\tfrac12\int Af^2/\kappa$, only semi-definite, and on a shell it has
the null direction $f \equiv 0$, $V \propto \exp\int\rho/\kappa$;
coercivity is a statement on the constraint set, with the Dirichlet
ends of this paragraph.)

\paragraph{The coupled problem.} Uniqueness of \eqref{eq:Lv} is the
coercivity of the whole bordered form on the complement of the rigid
modes, once the potential is eliminated through its own row. That is
the gravitational stability of the level-mean sector of the body (on
the sphere, degree $0$), and it cannot be settled by the fluid alone:
the gravitational term is negative, and the solid's stiffness and the
fluid's compression must together outweigh it. On a sphere it is the
coercivity of the degree-0 problem. Off the sphere it is
\emph{argued} by continuity, not proved. (The library's
minimum-eigenvalue probe, \code{PotentialBlockMinEigenvalue}, inspects the
potential block alone, and the fluid-block probe of
\S\ref{sec:verification} the border alone, so the coercivity of the
coupled bordered form off the sphere is not measured directly.) For a
shell the rigid modes include the inner core's rotations, which are
projected, but not its translation, which the mixed rows determine
(\S\ref{sec:shell}). At $D = 0$ the level-mean sector carries no
relabelling freedom, and the closure needs no special case: a
relabelling within it would be a level-mean field $\bxi$ with
$\Div(\rho\bxi) = 0$, hence on the sphere radial with $\xi_r \propto
1/(\rho r^2)$, which is singular at a centre and annihilated by the
walls of a shell.

\paragraph{Stability of the state, and what the $D > 0$ answer means.}
The admissible set contains the overturning motions
(\S\ref{sec:NL-energy}), so where $N^2 < 0$ the critical point is a
saddle of the full energy, and the gauge-penalty treatment shows
Rayleigh--Taylor growth there: the dynamics finding the descent
direction. The closure, which imposes equilibrium level by level and
linearises about the background's own arrangement (A4), does not look
for one.

\begin{remark}[Existence against attainability]
\label{rem:attainability}
The closure equation never consults the sign of $D$, and hydrostatic
equilibrium exists for any stratification; the energy, and with it
attainability, does see the sign. The three statements together
resolve what would otherwise look paradoxical: a static theory that
works for a fluid that cannot be static.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Existence.} Hydrostatic equilibrium is blind to the sign of
  $D$: an unstably stratified profile is an exact equilibrium, and the
  secular state exists as a critical point of the energy on the
  rearrangement class for every stratification (solving
  \eqref{eq:exact} at a prescribed $\Lambda$ finds unstable equilibria
  too). Its linear response exists and is unique for $D \le 0$, and for
  $D > 0$ whenever the fluid block has no zero eigenvalue --- the check
  above, which the uniform compressible core passes at Earth-like
  $\kappa$.
\item \emph{Attainability, $D \le 0$ only.} For $N^2 \ge 0$ every
  dissipative completion relaxes to the critical point (Dahlen and
  Fels 1978, \S\ref{sec:literature}). For $N^2 < 0$ it is a saddle, and
  dissipation does not rescue it: the evolution finds the descent
  direction --- the Rayleigh--Taylor growth above --- and its end state
  is the same inventory re-sorted into a stable arrangement, at a
  distance from the background set by the unstable layer's extent, not
  by the load. Dahlen and Fels's unstable-core statement, that the
  transient problem has no final state near the background, is this
  saddle seen from the transient side.
\item \emph{What the $D > 0$ branch is for.} The closure's answer at
  unstable stratification is the analytic continuation of the stable
  family across $D = 0$. As physics it carries a timescale hypothesis
  of the same shape as the rotating limit's
  (\S\ref{sec:rotating-limit}): it is the response only on loading times
  short against the instability's growth time $\sim 1/|N|$, which for a
  genuinely unstable core no secular loading satisfies. As mathematics
  it is exactly the right benchmark object. The uniform compressible
  core of \S\ref{sec:verification} --- Longman's ``impossible'' core ---
  is unstably stratified, so the degree-0 numbers computed there, by
  this library and by the spectral references alike, are the
  continuation, agreed on because both methods compute the same
  critical point.
\end{enumerate}
The older papers' inversion is consistent with this reading: Pekeris
and Accad found the benign $\sigma \to 0$ limit at the \emph{unstable}
core and the ``infrared catastrophe'' at the stable one, while Dahlen
and Fels found no final state for the unstable core. A forced inviscid
limit and a dissipative transient are different limits, and the saddle
supports both.
\end{remark}

\paragraph{Two items that need no argument.} \emph{$F3$ needs no
limiting argument.} One might expect a surface term on $\Sigma$ to need
justification as the limit of a thin layer of fluid. It needs none: it
is the fluid half of the ordinary moving-interface term
(\S\ref{sec:feels}), on level and transverse walls alike, the first
variation of the finite-amplitude statement that the fluid fills its
container. \emph{Welding is not in question.} Welding the fluid to a
transverse wall would add tangential conditions to the count of
\S\ref{sec:why}, but an inviscid fluid slips, and the secular state has
a free tangential trace.

%=====================================================================
\part{The wall}

\section{Displacements, and the wall sheet}
\label{sec:disp}

\status{\S\ref{sec:realise} derived; \S\ref{sec:transverse} derived
away from level points, argued at them; \S\ref{sec:levelwalls} derived
apart from its closing paragraph, which is argued; \S\S\ref{sec:notsmall}
and \ref{sec:dissipative} argued.}

Nothing so far has needed a fluid displacement. This section asks
which displacement fields, if any, reproduce the state, because that
is where the displacement formulations' difficulties lie
(\S\ref{sec:why}).

\subsection{Which displacements realise the state}
\label{sec:realise}

Let $\bu$ be a linear displacement of the fluid parcels. It
\emph{realises} the state if the Eulerian changes it generates,
$\delta\varpi = -\mathcal L_{\bu}\varpi$ and $\delta S = -\mathcal
L_{\bu}S$ as in \eqref{eq:admissible}, are those of the state, that is
if two things hold. First, the Eulerian fields it implies, through
label conservation, mass conservation and the adiabatic law, are
\eqref{eq:eulerian}. Second, it is welded to the walls: $\bu\cdot\bn =
\bu_s\cdot\bn$ there. Define the \emph{pointwise lift}
\begin{equation*}
  \hat s := -\bigl(\phi + \bu\cdot\nabla\Phi_0\bigr) = -\Delta\Phi ,
\end{equation*}
minus the Lagrangian change of potential following a parcel. A parcel
that started on level $c$ ends at potential $c - \hat s$, and
$\bu\cdot\nabla\Phi_0 = -\phi - \hat s$. It is a priori a field in three dimensions.

\emph{Labels.} Label conservation gives $S_1 = -\bu\cdot\nabla S_0 =
\hat S_0'(\phi + \hat s)$. The state has $S_1 = \hat S_0'(\phi + s)$, by
\eqref{eq:eulerian} and $\delta\hat S = \hat S_0's$. Multiplying the
difference by $\partial_S\rho$ and using \eqref{eq:D}:
\begin{equation}
  D\,(\hat s - s) = 0\quad\text{pointwise.}
  \label{eq:Dss}
\end{equation}

\emph{Density.} Following a parcel, write $\Delta$ for the first-order
(Lagrangian) change. Mass conservation gives $\Delta\rho = \rho_1 +
\bu\cdot\nabla\rho = -\rho\Div\bu$. The equation of state at fixed
label gives $\Delta\rho = (\rho/\kappa)\,\Delta p$. Together these give
the adiabatic law $\Div\bu = -\Delta p/\kappa$. Here $\Delta p = p_1 +
\bu\cdot\nabla p_0 = f - \rho\phi - \rho\,\bu\cdot\nabla\Phi_0 = f +
\rho\hat s$, so $\Div\bu = -(f + \rho\hat s)/\kappa$. Then
$-\Div(\rho\bu) = \rho'\phi + \rho f/\kappa + D\hat s$. This equals the
state's $\rho_1$ by \eqref{eq:Dss}, so it imposes nothing new.

\emph{Volumes.} Integrate $\Div\bu$ over the fluid below level $c$,
using the divergence theorem and welding on $\Sigma_c$:
\begin{equation*}
  \int_{R_c}\Div\bu\dV = \delta\Vol - \langle\hat s\rangle A ,
\end{equation*}
where $\langle\hat s\rangle := A^{-1}\oint_{L_c}\hat s\dS/g$ is the
level mean of the pointwise lift. By the adiabatic law the left-hand
side is also $-\int_{c_{\min}}^cA\bigl(f + \rho\langle\hat
s\rangle\bigr)/\kappa\,\mathrm dc'$. Subtract the corresponding
identities for the state, \eqref{eq:Vdef} and \eqref{eq:Vsum}, and set
$Y := (\langle\hat s\rangle - s)A$. The result is $Y =
\int_{c_{\min}}^c(\rho/\kappa)\,Y\,\mathrm dc'$, so $Y \equiv 0$ by
Gr\"onwall's inequality:
\begin{equation}
  \langle\hat s\rangle = s .
  \label{eq:mean}
\end{equation}
Together, \eqref{eq:Dss} and \eqref{eq:mean} settle whether the lift is
a field in three dimensions.
\begin{itemize}
\item The state's lift $s$ is always a function of the level.
\item The pointwise lift of a realising displacement equals it wherever
  $D \ne 0$. There the \emph{slaving} holds, $\bu\cdot\nabla\Phi_0 =
  -(\phi + s)$, and each parcel rides the perturbed equipotentials
  shifted by $s$; the normal displacement is $u_\nu = -(\phi + s)/g$.
\item Where $D = 0$, the pointwise lift is $s$ plus any field of zero
  level mean. That remainder changes no Eulerian field, since it enters
  $\rho_1$ only through $D\hat s$, and $p_1$ not at all. It is the extra
  relabelling freedom of a neutral layer: fluid of one substance may be
  exchanged across levels as long as the mass below each level is
  unchanged.
\end{itemize}
The displacement along the levels, $\bu_\parallel$, is then fixed on
each level surface by its surface divergence, up to divergence-free
fields along the level (the strict class of \S\ref{sec:A4}). Its
existence turns on how the levels meet the walls, which is the subject
of the next two subsections.

\paragraph{The status of a realising displacement.} It is worth being
precise about what kind of object this $\bu$ is, since it was
introduced as a device. Away from a stratified level wall it is the
real displacement up to gauge. The relations above determine it
exactly modulo the strict relabelling class, and that class is
precisely the Eulerian-unobservable freedom: $\rho$ is constant on
levels, so a relabelling permutes indistinguishable fluid and changes
no observable field. The realisation is therefore pinned exactly to
the extent that it is observable. It also agrees with the dissipative
physics. In any dissipative completion of the static problem
(\S\ref{sec:dissipative}), the actual particle displacement at $t \to
\infty$ satisfies, in the interior, the same momentum balance, mass
conservation and constitutive law as the limiting Eulerian state, and
the same algebra then forces the same $s$, $u_\nu$ and $\Div\bu$: the
real field lies in the realising class. Which representative the
physics lands on --- which particle went where along the levels ---
depends on the mechanism and the loading history, and is exactly the
information the secular state discards. At a stratified level wall the
regular class has no welded member (\S\ref{sec:levelwalls}); the
realising object there is the limit of welded fields of
Proposition~\ref{prop:layer}, the regular field $\bu^0$ plus a
tangential transport concentrated on the wall.

\subsection{Transverse walls}
\label{sec:transverse}

Where a wall meets the level $L_c$ at angle $\theta > 0$, write
\begin{equation*}
  \bn = \cos\theta\,\nuh + \sin\theta\,\btau ,
\end{equation*}
with $\btau$ the unit vector tangent to the level, normal to the edge
curve $L_c\cap\Sigma$, and pointing out of the fluid. The normal trace
of a displacement is $\bu\cdot\bn = u_\nu\cos\theta +
(\bu_\parallel\cdot\btau)\sin\theta$, where $u_\nu := \bu\cdot\nuh =
-(\phi + \hat s)/g$. Welding is therefore a condition on the
along-level displacement at the edge of each level:
\begin{equation}
  \bu_\parallel\cdot\btau = \frac{\bu_s\cdot\bn - u_\nu\cos\theta}{\sin\theta}
  \qquad\text{on } L_c\cap\Sigma .
  \label{eq:edge}
\end{equation}
For a field tangent to the levels, $\Div\bu_\parallel =
\Div_L\bu_\parallel + \bu_\parallel\cdot\nabla_L\ln g$, with $\Div_L$
the surface divergence on $L_c$, so on each level the weighted field
$g\,\bu_\parallel$ has a prescribed surface divergence, namely
$g\bigl(\Div\bu - \Div(u_\nu\nuh)\bigr)$, and a prescribed outward flux
through the edge. Such a field exists exactly when the divergence
theorem on $L_c$ holds for it, and is unique up to divergence-free
tangent fields with no edge flux. That compatibility condition is,
once the coarea weights are tracked, the $c$-derivative of the volume
identity of \S\ref{sec:realise}, which the state satisfies by
construction (argued). So on a transverse wall, away from level
points, a regular welded displacement realises the state, slaving and
all. There is no mismatch, no sheet and no transport: the along-level
displacement takes up, pointwise, whatever the slaved normal
displacement leaves, and the no-penetration condition of the
displacement formulations constrains a component that the slaving
leaves free.

At level points $\sin\theta \to 0$, and \eqref{eq:edge} grows like
$1/\sin\theta$. What happens there depends on the kind of level point;
the following is argued from local analysis.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{At the top of the fluid}, the levels are small discs whose
  whole edge lies on the wall. The divergence theorem on such a disc
  forces the edge mean of the numerator of \eqref{eq:edge} to vanish at
  the order needed. The remainder varies round the edge by $O(r)$ at
  radius $r$, while $\sin\theta = O(r)$, so $\bu_\parallel$ stays
  bounded.
\item \emph{Where a level first meets the wall, or at a saddle}, the
  small edge curve is only part of the boundary of a large level. The
  mean mismatch on it need not vanish and must be supplied from the
  rest of the level. Locally this is a point source in the level
  surface: $\bu_\parallel$ grows like the inverse of the distance from
  the level point. That is singular, but square-integrable in three
  dimensions.
\end{itemize}
The along-level displacement is never constructed by the
closure, carries no energy beyond its divergence, and enters no
Eulerian field beyond it, so none of this touches the reduced problem.

A transverse wall also constrains the solid, not the fluid
(\S\ref{sec:solid-asks}): with a density contrast it needs deviatoric
pre-stress in the solid.

\subsection{Level walls: the reference surface, the sheet, and the transport}
\label{sec:levelwalls}
\label{sec:wall}

On a top level wall the situation is different in kind: no level below
$c_w$ meets the wall at all. The along-level displacement can never
reach the wall, and the normal trace of a realising displacement is
its slaved component alone.

\paragraph{The reference surface.} For every $c < c_w$, the fluid of
mass $M_0(c)$ lies below the perturbed equipotential at value $c -
s(c)$. Extrapolate to the top, with $s_w := s(c_w)$, and define the
equipotential
\begin{equation*}
  \Gamma := \{\Phi_0 + \phi = c_w - s_w\} .
\end{equation*}
This is where the top of the fluid would be if the fluid ended on an
equipotential. Measured along $\bn = \nuh$ from $\Sigma$, $\Gamma$
stands at height $-(\phi + s_w)/g$, which is the slaved normal trace
$u_\nu$ at the wall, while the deformed wall stands at height
$\bu_s\cdot\bn$. Their signed separation and the corresponding mass
per unit area are
\begin{equation}
  a := \bu_s\cdot\bn + \frac{\phi + s_w}{g} = \bu_s\cdot\bn - u_\nu,
  \qquad
  \sigma := \rho_F\,a .
  \label{eq:sigma}
\end{equation}
Using \eqref{eq:dV} and \eqref{eq:Vdef} at $c_w$, $V(c_w) =
-\bar\Phi(c_w) - s_wA(c_w)$, so
\begin{equation}
  \oint_\Sigma\sigma\dS
  = \rho_F\bigl(S[\bu_s] + \bar\Phi(c_w) + s_wA(c_w)\bigr)
  = \rho_F\bigl(S[\bu_s] - V(c_w)\bigr) = 0
  \label{eq:wall}
\end{equation}
by the fill condition. On a level wall the fill condition says that
$\Gamma$ encloses the right volume. In other words,
\eqref{eq:wall} is the \emph{level mean of the normal matching}: the
wall and $\Gamma$ need not match pointwise, but their integrals over
the wall must.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1.9cm,y=1.3cm]
  \fill[gray!8] (0,-1.15) rectangle (6.283,1.25);
  \fill[white] plot[domain=0:6.283,samples=80,smooth] (\x,{0.45*sin(\x r)+0.12*sin(2*\x r)}) -- (6.283,1.25) -- (0,1.25) -- cycle;
  \begin{scope}
    \clip (0,0) rectangle (6.283,1.2);
    \fill[blue!25] plot[domain=0:6.283,samples=80,smooth] (\x,{0.45*sin(\x r)+0.12*sin(2*\x r)}) -- (6.283,0) -- (0,0) -- cycle;
  \end{scope}
  \begin{scope}
    \clip (0,-1.2) rectangle (6.283,0);
    \fill[pattern=north east lines,pattern color=red!60] plot[domain=0:6.283,samples=80,smooth] (\x,{0.45*sin(\x r)+0.12*sin(2*\x r)}) -- (6.283,0) -- (0,0) -- cycle;
  \end{scope}
  \draw[very thick] plot[domain=0:6.283,samples=80,smooth] (\x,{0.45*sin(\x r)+0.12*sin(2*\x r)});
  \draw[dashed,thick] (0,0) -- (6.283,0);
  \node[anchor=west] at (6.33,0) {$\Gamma$};
  \node at (3.15,1.0) {solid};
  \node at (3.15,-0.85) {fluid};
  \node at (1.25,0.22) {\small $\sigma>0$};
  \node[fill=white,inner sep=1pt] at (4.65,-0.24) {\small $\sigma<0$};
  \node[anchor=south] at (1.6,0.58) {\small deformed wall};
  \draw[-{Stealth}] (5.9,-0.65) -- (5.9,-0.15) node[midway,right] {\small $\bn=\nuh$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{A level wall after deformation. The reference surface
$\Gamma$ (dashed) is the equipotential on which a slaved displacement
would end the fluid. The fluid actually ends at the deformed wall
(thick). Shaded: fluid above $\Gamma$, counted positive in $\sigma$.
Hatched: the part of the region below $\Gamma$ that is not fluid,
counted negative. The two balance exactly when the fill condition
holds. The vertical scale is greatly exaggerated: the band has
thickness $O(\varepsilon)$.}
\label{fig:gamma}
\end{figure}

Figure~\ref{fig:gamma} shows what $\sigma$ is. It is the signed mass
between the true upper boundary of the fluid, the wall, and the
equipotential $\Gamma$ on which a fluid ending on an equipotential
would stop. It is \emph{not} a property of the state, which by
\S\ref{sec:feels} has a regular density and no surface mass beyond the
ordinary moving-boundary term $F3$. At finite amplitude it is the band
of \S\ref{sec:fluidatwall}, measured against $\Gamma$.

\paragraph{No regular displacement, unless the wall is neutral.} The
normal trace of a realising displacement is $\bu\cdot\bn = -(\phi +
\hat s)/g$. Welding therefore requires $\hat s - s_w = -g\,a$ on
$\Sigma$.
\begin{itemize}
\item If $D(c_w) \ne 0$, then \eqref{eq:Dss} forces $\hat s = s_w$ on
  $\Sigma$, and welding is possible only if $a \equiv 0$. Generically no
  regular displacement realises the state: its normal trace is that of
  $\Gamma$, not of the wall.
\item If $D = 0$ at and near $c_w$, the pointwise lift on the wall
  level is free subject to \eqref{eq:mean}. The choice $\hat s = s_w -
  g\,a$ has level mean $s_w$ exactly when $\oint_\Sigma a\dS = 0$, which
  is the fill condition. A neutral level wall is welded, and needs no
  sheet.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{The tangential transport.} When $D(c_w) \ne 0$, what fails
is only the regular trace. Let $\bu^0$ be a realising field away from
the wall, with $\bu^0\cdot\bn = \bu_s\cdot\bn - a$ on $\Sigma$. Write
