$\gradS$, $\divS$ and $\Delta_\Sigma$ for the surface gradient, surface
divergence and Laplace--Beltrami operator on $\Sigma$.

\begin{lemma}
\label{lem:q}
There is a tangent field $\bq$ on $\Sigma$ with $\divS\bq = -a$ if and
only if $\oint_\Sigma a\dS = 0$. The field $\bq$ is unique up to a
divergence-free tangent field, and its gradient representative is $\bq
= \gradS\psi$ with $\Delta_\Sigma\psi = -a$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Necessity is the divergence theorem on the closed surface $\Sigma$. For
sufficiency: the Laplace--Beltrami operator on a closed connected
surface has the constants as its kernel, so by the Fredholm
alternative its range is the mean-zero functions.
\end{proof}

$\bq$ is a volume flux per unit length of wall. On a sphere of radius
$r$, with $a$ of degree $\ell \ge 1$, $\bq = r^2\gradS a/\ell(\ell +
1)$. Use collar coordinates $\bx = \mathbf y - \eta\,\bn(\mathbf y)$,
with $\mathbf y \in \Sigma$ and $\eta$ the distance into the fluid. Take
$\chi$ smooth on $[0, \infty)$ with $\chi(0) = 1$ and $\chi = 0$ on
$[1, \infty)$, and set $\chi_\delta(\eta) := \chi(\eta/\delta)$. Define
\begin{equation}
  \bxi^\delta := \bu^0 + a\,\chi_\delta(\eta)\,\bn - \chi_\delta'(\eta)\,\bq ,
  \label{eq:xidelta}
\end{equation}
with $a$, $\bn$ and $\bq$ extended constantly along the normals.

\begin{proposition}
\label{prop:layer}
Assume $\oint_\Sigma a\dS = 0$. Then, as $\delta \to 0$:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item $\bxi^\delta$ is smooth and welded, and $\bxi^\delta = \bu^0$ for
  $\eta \ge \delta$.
\item $\Div\bxi^\delta - \Div\bu^0$ and $\bxi^\delta\cdot\nuh -
  \bu^0\cdot\nuh$ are bounded uniformly in $\delta$ and supported in the
  layer $\eta < \delta$. Hence the Eulerian density and label fields of
  $\bxi^\delta$ converge in $L^2$ to those of the state.
\item $\bxi^\delta\dV \rightharpoonup \bu^0\dV + \bq\dS_\Sigma$
  weakly-$*$ as vector measures.
\item $\|\bxi^\delta - \bu^0\|_{L^2}^2 \sim
  \delta^{-1}\|\chi'\|_{L^2}^2\,\|\bq\|_{L^2(\Sigma)}^2 \to \infty$,
  unless $\bq \equiv 0$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}[Proof (key steps)]
\emph{(i)} At $\eta = 0$ the second term adds $a$ to the normal trace,
and the third is tangential.
\emph{(ii)} The normal term has divergence $-a\chi_\delta' + O(1)$. The
tangential term has divergence $-\chi_\delta'\bigl(\divS\bq +
O(\eta)\bigr) = a\chi_\delta' + O(\eta\chi_\delta')$, the $O(\eta)$
coming from the metric of the parallel surfaces. The $O(\delta^{-1})$
terms cancel, and $|\eta\chi_\delta'| \le \sup|\chi'|$ on the layer.
Since $\nuh = \bn + O(\eta)$, the tangential term changes
$\bxi\cdot\nuh$ by $O(1)$.
\emph{(iii)} For a continuous test field $\bw$, $\int\bw\cdot
(-\chi_\delta'\bq)\dV \to \oint_\Sigma\bw\cdot\bq\dS$, because
$\int_0^\infty(-\chi_\delta')\,\mathrm d\eta = 1$ and the volume Jacobian
is $1 + O(\eta)$.
\emph{(iv)} Direct computation of the dominant tangential term.
\end{proof}

Every $\bxi^\delta$ is a smooth, welded field, so there is no gap and
no interpenetration at any stage. Passing to the limit in
$\int_F(\bxi\cdot\nabla\vartheta + \vartheta\Div\bxi)\dV =
\oint_\Sigma\vartheta\,\bxi\cdot\bn\dS$, for smooth test functions
$\vartheta$, shows that the limit satisfies the kinematic condition in
the form
\begin{equation*}
  \bu^0\cdot\bn - \divS\bq = \bu_s\cdot\bn\qquad\text{on } \Sigma .
\end{equation*}
\emph{The apparent normal jump $a$ belongs to the regular part alone,
and equals $-\divS\bq$.} In the language of Part~I the limit is a
current, $T = \bu^0\,\vol + \bq\dS_\Sigma$, with $\iota_T\varpi$ read
distributionally; the kinematic condition says that the mass form it
transports, $-\mathcal L_T\varpi = -\mathrm d(\iota_T\varpi)$, has the
boundary flux of a welded field, the surface divergence of $\rho_F\bq$
being the extra term that Cartan's formula produces when
$\iota_T\varpi$ has a part supported on $\Sigma$. The fill condition
$\oint\sigma = 0$ is exactly the solvability condition of
Lemma~\ref{lem:q}. The gauge freedom in $\bq$ is the wall's share of
the relabelling freedom, and only $\sigma = -\rho_F\divS\bq$ is
observable.

\begin{remark}[Energy]
\label{rem:energy}
By \S\ref{sec:eulerianform}, the second variation $E_2$ depends on a
fluid displacement only through $\Div\bxi$, $\xi_\nu$ and its wall
trace, and it is continuous in $L^2$ in the first two. By (ii) those
converge, so $E_2[\bxi^\delta]$ converges to the energy of the state.
The state differs from $\bu^0$ only in the wall trace, $\bu_s\cdot\bn$
in place of $\bu^0\cdot\bn$, so the limit is
\begin{equation*}
  E_2[\bu^0] + \oint_\Sigma\rho_F\,\phi\,(\bu_s\cdot\bn - \bu^0\cdot\bn)\dS
  = E_2[\bu^0] + \oint_\Sigma\sigma\phi\dS ,
\end{equation*}
at rate $O(\delta)$. The extra term is the coupling of the concentrated
transport to the potential, $\rho_F\oint\bq\cdot\gradS\phi\dS$, and it
is exactly what $F3$ adds to the slaved field. (Continuity in the
change of state $(\delta\rho, \delta S)$ itself would fail near neutral
levels, where recovering $\xi_\nu$ from $S_1$ divides by $\hat S_0'$;
that is why the statement is made in $(\Div\bxi, \xi_\nu)$.) For $N^2 <
0$ the form is indefinite, and ``minimising sequence'' should be read
as ``sequence along which the energy converges''.
\end{remark}

The level wall and the transverse wall are two ends of one picture. On
a transverse wall the along-level displacement meets the wall, and
only isolated level points produce point sources. On a level wall
every point is a level point. The point sources fill the wall with
strength proportional to $a$, and their superposition is the surface
transport $\bq$, with $\divS\bq = -a$. At finite amplitude this is the
along-level displacement within the band of top levels of
\S\ref{sec:fluidatwall}, which the deformed wall cuts transversally. As
$\varepsilon \to 0$ the band collapses, and that displacement
concentrates onto $\Sigma$ (argued).

\paragraph{Where a displacement is regular.} Collecting the three
cases:
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{A transverse patch at $D \ne 0$.} A regular welded
  displacement realises the state away from the level points that a
  closed transverse wall cannot avoid (\S\ref{sec:transverse}).
\item \emph{A level wall at $D \ne 0$.} No regular welded displacement
  realises the state. The welded fields of Proposition~\ref{prop:layer}
  realise it in the limit, with a tangential transport $\bq$
  concentrating on the wall; the normal trace is continuous at every
  stage, and the apparent jump $a$ between the wall and $\Gamma$ belongs
  to the regular part.
\item \emph{$D = 0$.} The lift is free and the matching is imposed per
  point; the wall is welded, and the problem is the welded one, well
  posed modulo gauge (\S\ref{sec:counting}).
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Why the displacement is not small}
\label{sec:notsmall}

Let the mismatch $a$ on a stratified level wall have amplitude
$\varepsilon$ and lateral scale $L$, so that $L = r/\ell$ on the
sphere and $\bq = O(\varepsilon L)$. Any displacement realising the
state near the wall must carry a volume $O(\varepsilon L)$ per unit
length along the wall. Within a layer of thickness $\delta$, its
tangential part is of size $\varepsilon L/\delta$ and its gradient of
size $\varepsilon L/\delta^2$. A linear displacement description needs
that gradient to be small, which requires
\begin{equation*}
  \delta \gg (\varepsilon L)^{1/2} .
\end{equation*}
The state is an $O(\varepsilon)$ perturbation everywhere. The
displacement that produces it is not uniformly small near a stratified
level wall. This is the precise sense in which linear displacement
theory is the wrong tool there, while the state description is
untouched. The concentrated transport $\bq\,\delta_\Sigma$ is the limit
$\varepsilon \to 0$ followed by $\delta \to 0$. It records, at first
order, a lateral rearrangement that the exact theory never needs to
describe. This is the remark of \S\ref{sec:allconserved} made concrete:
the rearrangement is within the class, but not by a small smooth map.

\subsection{The wall layer in the dissipative completions}
\label{sec:dissipative}

The transport has a direct reading in the dissipative completions of
the static problem (\code{doc/static\_fluid\_core.tex}, its
regularisation section): fluid viscosity, or the Maxwell core of the
production method. In each, the evolution under a step load is well
posed, and the displacement is welded at every finite time; no mass is
ever added or removed anywhere. Where the wall recedes from $\Gamma$ a
gap cannot open: fluid flows in to fill it. At $N^2 \ne 0$ that fluid
cannot come from the interior, whose parcels the stratification holds
to their equipotentials; it comes \emph{laterally}, creeping along the
wall from the parts where the wall advances, driven by the along-level
pressure imbalance that the mismatch sustains and resisted by the
dissipative mechanism. With viscosity the accommodation occupies a wall
layer of width $\ell(t)$ that thins as $t$ grows: the layer field
$\bxi^\delta$ of \eqref{eq:xidelta} with $\delta \sim \ell(t)$, and in
the $t \to \infty$ limit the measure $\bu^0 + \bq\,\delta_\Sigma$ of
Proposition~\ref{prop:layer}. Two small lengths must be kept apart.
The dynamical layer width $\ell(t)$ is the region over which the
displacement adjusts from the slaved interior to the wall; it is this
that thins with $t$. The fluid the transport delivers sits between
$\Gamma$ and the wall, a region of thickness $|a|$ --- first-order small,
fixed by the mismatch, independent of $t$ --- and at the ambient density
$\rho_F$ (Figure~\ref{fig:sheet}). No density grows: by
Proposition~\ref{prop:layer}(ii) the Eulerian density of the layer
fields converges in $L^2$ to the state's, and what concentrates is the
displacement, not the mass. Once $\ell(t)$ falls below $(\varepsilon
L)^{1/2}$, the linear displacement description of the layer fails
(\S\ref{sec:notsmall}) while the state does not. The secular closure
books the end state of the transport without computing the transport;
that is the sense in which it is \emph{secular}: it answers after the
lateral rearrangement has completed, however slow the mechanism.

Diffusion of the stratifying quantity is a different completion. Wunsch
(1975), through his Lamb argument, has the end state reached by
diffusive label change at the wall, with no lateral transport. Whether
that route reaches the same state depends on whether label diffusion
changes the inventory at first order near the wall, and that is open
(\S\ref{sec:standing}).

\begin{figure}[htbp]

interior field ends on the equipotential $\Gamma$ (dashed). The fluid
fills to the wall: its displacement, welded throughout, adjusts from
the slaved interior across a layer whose width $\ell(t)$, set by the
dissipation, thins as $t$ grows (dotted edge), and the region between
$\Gamma$ and the wall is fed laterally, by creep along the wall from
where the wall advances (arrows); at $N^2 \ne 0$ the interior cannot
supply it, since the stratification holds parcels to their level
surfaces (dashed grey). The transported fluid occupies a region of

%=====================================================================
\section{Why displacement formulations fail, and what they compute instead}
\label{sec:fail}
\label{sec:why}

\status{derived from \S\S\ref{sec:realise}--\ref{sec:levelwalls}; the
numerical facts are measured (the record is
\code{doc/static\_fluid\_core.tex}).}

The natural reading of ``static'' is to set the time derivatives to
zero and keep the fluid as an elastic material with vanishing shear
modulus, welded to the wall or slipping along it. Part~III says exactly
when that works.

\paragraph{The counting.} Momentum balance in the fluid leaves free one
scalar per level, the function $f$ (Remark~\ref{rem:eulerian-route}).
At $D \ne 0$ the label fixes each parcel's level, so the normal trace
the fluid presents at a level wall is $-(\phi + s)/g$ with $s$ a level
function: one number per level to match a whole function
$\bu_s\cdot\bn$ on the wall, kinematic matching being one condition per
wall \emph{point}. No tangential freedom reaches a level wall, because
no level below $c_w$ meets it. The elastic-static problem is therefore
overdetermined at a stratified level wall, and has no solution unless
the mismatch $a$ vanishes identically. The freedom in $f$ can absorb at
most the level-integral part of the mismatch --- exactly the scalar the
fill condition determines --- and the conflict lives in the components
orthogonal to it, which $f$ cannot reach. On a sphere at degree $\ell
\ge 1$ the closure gives $s = 0$, so $a = \bu_s\cdot\hat{\mathbf r} +
\phi/g$, which is nonzero for every load; at degree $0$ the freedom
survives, and with it the well-posed scalar of the closure. On a
transverse wall the free edge values of $\bu_\parallel$ meet the
pointwise condition instead (\S\ref{sec:transverse}), and only near the
level points does the conflict return. The counting is Longman's
(1963) and Jeffreys and Vicente's (1966), \S\ref{sec:literature}.

\subsection{The neutral case}
\label{sec:counting}

At $D = 0$ the counting changes sides, and the case deserves to be seen
whole. The relation \eqref{eq:Ds} loses its left side. It no longer
determines anything about the lift, and becomes instead a condition on
the free function,
\begin{equation}
  f' + \frac{\rho f}{\kappa} = 0
  \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
  f(c) = f_0\,\exp\Bigl(-\int_{c_0}^{c}\frac{\rho}{\kappa}\,\mathrm dc'\Bigr),
  \label{eq:neutral-f}
\end{equation}
with one constant $f_0$ per connected component of the fluid in place
of one scalar per level. On the self-compressed profile a lifted parcel
arrives with exactly the ambient density, so a lift produces no
Eulerian density perturbation and costs no energy: the buoyancy term
of \eqref{eq:E2xi} vanishes identically. The Eulerian state of the
fluid is then fixed by the potential and by $f_0$ alone, and the
pointwise lift is free (\S\ref{sec:realise}), and with it the fluid's
normal trace at the wall. The counting closes in the right order.
\begin{itemize}
\item Kinematic matching $\bu\cdot\bn = \bu_s\cdot\bn$ is
  \emph{imposed} on a free trace, rather than checked against a
  determined one: one condition per wall point against one free value
  per wall point.
\item The constant $f_0$ is set by the component's fill condition.
\item The freedom left over once both are imposed is exactly the
  mass-preserving relabelling group, class~K of \S\ref{sec:A4}.
\end{itemize}
The welded static problem at $D = 0$ is therefore well posed
\emph{modulo gauge}. That is why the gauge-penalty treatment is clean
precisely at neutral stratification and nowhere else
(\code{doc/static\_fluid\_core.tex}, the hierarchy). Neutral
stratification is the one case in which the natural reading of
``static'' survives. The closure itself needs no special case at $D =
0$ (\S\ref{sec:uniqueness}), and the verification's neutral-core line
is the measured form of this agreement (\S\ref{sec:verification}).

\subsection{The band, measured}

When $D \ne 0$, the discrete displacement problems are a minimising
sequence for the static energy whose infimum is not attained. Under
refinement the computed fluid displacement develops ever finer
structure at the wall and does not converge in the displacement space.
Meanwhile the solid observables wander within a \emph{band}: a spread
of values whose width is set by the stratification and which no
resolution removes. \emph{Measured} on the two-dimensional
\code{fluid\_core} disc at degree 2, the band is a relative spread of
$\sim 10^{-3}$--$10^{-2}$ in gauge-invariant solid observables across
the welded, slipping and regularised solves; the welded and slipping
answers wander under $p$-refinement at the $3\times10^{-3}$ level
rather than converging; and the band shrinks roughly in proportion to
$N^2$ (a factor $\sim 14$ under $N^2/30$). On the mass-matched neutral
twin all formulations agree to $10^{-10}$.

\subsection{What the displacement formulations compute}

\paragraph{What a discretisation does.} A finite-element space of
displacements contains welded fields, and among them, by
Proposition~\ref{prop:layer}, fields whose state is as close as one
likes to the secular state, at the cost of a tangential layer of
mesh-scale thickness. The minimising sequence is the layer field
$\bxi^\delta$ with $\delta \sim h$. Its limit, $\bu^0 +
\bq\,\delta_\Sigma$, lies in no $L^2$-based space, so nothing converges
in the displacement. The robust signature is near-wall growth
$\|\bu_h\|_{L^2} \sim h^{-1/2}\|\bq\|$, with the across-wall integral of
the tangential displacement tending to $\bq$ (\emph{untested},
\S\ref{sec:verification}). A conforming discrete field can keep
$\Div\bu$ bounded in the layer only through a cancellation between
$\partial_\eta(\bu\cdot\bn)$ and $\divS\bu_\parallel$ that low-order
elements do not reproduce, which is a locking mechanism; neither
convergence of the solid observables nor a clean rate is guaranteed.
The band above is exactly this. At $N^2 = 0$ the welded compressible
solve converges and agrees with the closure, because the neutral wall
is welded (\S\ref{sec:levelwalls}): in the one-dimensional prototype the
two agree to $10^{-11}$ at degree 0 (Table~\ref{tab:prototype}).

\paragraph{Dissipation selects the secular state.} Dissipation of any
kind gives a well-posed evolution: viscosity, a Maxwell rheology of the
core, or diffusion of the stratifying quantity. For the advective
completions the long-time limit is the state in which the fluid has
rearranged to hydrostatic equilibrium in the perturbed potential at
fixed inventory, and that secular state is what a static fluid method
must compute; whether the diffusive completion reaches the same state
is open (\S\ref{sec:dissipative}). The limit must be read with care. It is a
statement about the Eulerian state (the density, the pressure, the
potential, the tractions on the solid), not about the displacement
that carries the fluid to it. The displacement the evolution
accumulates tends to $\bu^0 + \bq\,\delta_\Sigma$ at a stratified level
wall, with a relabelling-scale component in the interior that the
dissipative path, not any static principle, selects. The welded space
does not contain that limit. The secular state cannot therefore be
expected from a direct static computation in a displacement
description, and the static object that is actually well posed carries
no fluid displacement: that is what Dahlen's elimination computes, and
the closure keeps this structure on any geometry. (On a rotating body
the dissipation must also destroy the geostrophic support,
\S\ref{sec:rotating-limit}.)

\paragraph{The classes, one by one.}
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Welded.} As above.
\item \emph{Slipping.} Matching only the normal component adds
  tangential jumps $[\bu_t]$ across $\Sigma$. A jump across the wall
  carries no flux along it, so the slipping class contains the wrong
  singular object and fails in the same way. Its one advantage is on
  transverse walls, where \S\ref{sec:transverse} shows that a slipping
  displacement exists away from level points; the band should shrink as
  the wall becomes more transverse (\emph{untested}).
\item \emph{Gauged.} A small penalty selects one representative of the
  relabelling freedom. That freedom is real (\S\ref{sec:realise}), but
  the wall conflict is not a gauge; the penalty removes a null space,
  not the overdetermination.
\item \emph{Maxwell relaxation.} Giving the fluid a Maxwell viscosity
  and integrating in time is a dissipative completion. At every finite
  time the problem is well posed and the displacement is welded; the
  wall layer thins as $t$ grows, and the long-time limit is the measure
  $\bu^0 + \bq\,\delta_\Sigma$ of \S\ref{sec:levelwalls}. This route
  computes the right state, at the price of a time integration whose
  layer must be resolved until it no longer matters.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{What the secular state is not.} Two things are sometimes
conflated with it. One is the inviscid rotating state in which the
density surfaces are held off the geopotentials by thermal-wind
balance; the closure computes the relaxed state, which is the right one
only when dissipation destroys the geostrophic support within the
loading time (\S\ref{sec:rotating-limit}). The other is the overturned
family of (A4), which the closure does not compute either; it
linearises about the background's own arrangement.

%=====================================================================
\part{Special cases and extensions}

\section{The sphere: Dahlen's reduction, and degree zero}
\label{sec:dahlen}
\label{sec:sphere}

\status{derived (the reduction and the degree-0 closure); measured (its
properties).}

Let the background be radial, so that the level surfaces are spheres
(circles in two dimensions), $\nabla\Phi_0 = g\hat{\mathbf r}$,
$\partial_r\rho = \rho'\,g$, the wall is a level wall, and every
perturbation separates into spherical harmonics (circular harmonics in
two dimensions) of degree $\ell$. The level integral of any field of
degree $\ell \ge 1$ vanishes.

\paragraph{Degree $\ell \ge 1$: the interior relations.} Then
$\bar\Phi = 0$, $\delta\Vol = 0$ below $c_w$ and $S[\bu_s] = 0$, so the
closure with its end conditions gives $V \equiv 0$
(Remark~\ref{rem:sphere}), hence $s = 0$ and $f = 0$. Momentum balance
alone gives the same. The tangential part of \eqref{eq:momentum} has no
$\rho_1$ term, and $\rho$ is constant on each sphere, so
\begin{equation}
  \nabla_{\!\parallel}\bigl(p_1 + \rho\phi\bigr) = \mathbf 0,
  \qquad
  p_1 + \rho\,\phi = f(r) .
  \label{eq:tangential}
\end{equation}
A function of $r$ alone has no component along a harmonic of degree
$\ell \ge 1$, so at every such degree
\begin{equation}
  p_1 = -\rho\,\phi \qquad (\ell \ge 1).
  \label{eq:p-slaved}
\end{equation}
The radial part of \eqref{eq:momentum} is $-\partial_rp_1 -
\rho\,\partial_r\phi - g\rho_1 = 0$. By \eqref{eq:p-slaved},
$\partial_rp_1 = -\rho' g\,\phi - \rho\,\partial_r\phi$, and so
\begin{equation}
  \rho_1 = \rho'\,\phi \qquad (\ell \ge 1).
  \label{eq:rho-slaved}
\end{equation}
These are Dahlen's relations. They follow from momentum balance alone,
and so hold for any static description of the fluid. Dahlen's
reduction keeps them, discards the constitutive law and the
kinematics, and never introduces a fluid displacement.

\paragraph{The mixed weak form.} The solid is described in the mixed
organisation. Its weak form is the symmetric bilinear form $\mathcal
A(\bu_s, \phi\,|\,\bv, \chi)$ on displacement--potential pairs and
their test functions $(\bv, \chi)$, displayed in full as $\mathcal
A_{\mathrm{mix}}$ in \S\ref{sec:seam} (``The two solid-side forms, as
assembled''); it is the form (mixedA) of
\code{doc/quasi\_static\_models.tex}. Its sign convention is that the
potential enters through $+(4\pi G)^{-1}\int\nabla\phi\cdot\nabla\chi$,
so a density perturbation $\rho_1$ enters the potential row as
$+\int\rho_1\chi$. Here $\nuhs$ is the solid's outward normal on
$\Sigma$ (the code comments' $\mathbf m$, the SubMesh's
\code{CalcOrtho} normal). The fluid contributes three terms, named
F1--F3 in this library (\code{mixed\_problem.hpp};
\code{doc/quasi\_static\_models.tex}; \code{doc/benchmarks.tex}, ``The
CMB treatments''):
\begin{align}
  \text{(F1)}&\quad \int_F\rho'\,\phi\,\chi\dV ,
  \nonumber\\
  \text{(F2)}&\quad -\int_\Sigma\rho_F\,(\nuhs\cdot\nabla\Phi_0)
    (\nuhs\cdot\bu_s)(\nuhs\cdot\bv)\dS ,
  \label{eq:F123}\\
  \text{(F3)}&\quad -\int_\Sigma\rho_F\,\bigl[\phi\,(\nuhs\cdot\bv)
    + \chi\,(\nuhs\cdot\bu_s)\bigr]\dS .
  \nonumber
\end{align}
$F1$ is the fluid's Eulerian density perturbation
\eqref{eq:rho-slaved} in Poisson's equation. $F2$ is the buoyancy of the
displaced wall: the term $\bu_s\cdot\nabla p_0 =
-\rho_F\,\bu_s\cdot\nabla\Phi_0$ of the wall pressure \eqref{eq:wallp},
acting on the solid; on a level wall $\nuhs\cdot\nabla\Phi_0 = \mp g$.
$F3$ has two halves: the $\bv$-half is the pressure $-\rho_F\phi$ of
\eqref{eq:p-slaved} acting on the solid, and the $\chi$-half is the
fluid half $\rho_F\,\bu_s\cdot\bn$ of the moving-interface term of
\S\ref{sec:feels}. All three are the wall terms of \S\ref{sec:feels}
and the $F1$ of \S\ref{sec:eulerianform}, specialised. With the coupling
form
\begin{equation*}
  \mathcal C(\phi, \bv) = \int_{\text{solid}}\rho\,\nabla\phi\cdot\bv\dV
  - \int_\Sigma\rho_F\,\phi\,(\nuhs\cdot\bv)\dS ,
\end{equation*}
written $c(\phi, \bv)$ in \code{doc/quasi\_static\_models.tex}, whose
interface part is $F3$, the class solves two rows. The displacement row
is the solid's elastic and hydrostatic-gravity terms, plus $F2$, plus
$\mathcal C(\phi, \bv)$, equal to the load $\ell_u(\bv)$. The potential
row is $(4\pi G)^{-1}[\int\nabla\phi\cdot\nabla\chi + \mathrm{DtN}(\phi,
\chi)]$, plus $F1$, plus $\mathcal C(\chi, \bu_s)$, equal to the load
$\ell_\phi(\chi)$. Here $\mathrm{DtN}$ is the exterior
(Dirichlet-to-Neumann) condition --- the boundary form on a sphere
enclosing the body that imposes the potential's harmonic decay at
infinity, so that no exterior mesh is carried --- and $\ell_u$,
$\ell_\phi$ are the load functionals of a surface load or a tidal
potential. We write $L_D(\bu_s, \phi)$, or $\Ltot_D$, for the quadratic
functional whose variations are these rows without their loads: one
half of $\mathcal A$, with F1--F3 added, evaluated on $(\bu_s, \phi)$
twice. The fluid's displacement and its bulk modulus appear nowhere. At
$\ell \ge 1$ the border rows of \S\ref{sec:weak} are homogeneous and
inert, so the closure is Dahlen's reduction unchanged.

\paragraph{What the reduction implies but never writes.} It is the
fluid displacement. By \S\ref{sec:realise} the only candidate is the
slaved field $u_r = -\phi/g$, with $\Div\bu = 0$ (the two
determinations of $\rho_1$, from mass conservation and from
\eqref{eq:rho-slaved}, differ by $D(\phi + g u_r)$, which forces the
slaving wherever $D \ne 0$). Its trace does not match
$\bu_s\cdot\hat{\mathbf r}$. By \S\ref{sec:levelwalls} the difference is
the sheet $\sigma = \rho_F(\bu_s\cdot\hat{\mathbf r} + \phi/g)$, which
measures the wall against the equipotential $\Gamma$, of zero mean at
$\ell \ge 1$ automatically, and realised in displacement language by
the transport $\bq = r^2\gradS a/\ell(\ell + 1)$. The surface term $F3$
books the fluid filling to the wall: together with the solid's volume
coupling it places the density-jump term $(\rho_F - \rho_S)\,
\bu_s\cdot\bn$ on the wall (\S\ref{sec:feels}), exactly what a
displacement continuous across the wall would produce, and its energy
is the $\oint\sigma\phi$ of Remark~\ref{rem:energy}. It needs no
limiting argument, being the first variation of the exact statement
that the fluid fills its container.

\paragraph{Properties (measured).} These are measured on the
two-dimensional disc models of \code{doc/static\_fluid\_core.tex} (its
numerical-evidence section).
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] At $\ell \ge 1$ the construction never consults $\kappa$.
  Dahlen's answer is independent of the fluid's bulk modulus:
  unchanged to $10^{-11}$ under a thirty-fold scaling of $\kappa$. The
  reason is that $\kappa$ enters the fluid only through the adiabatic
  part of $D$ and through the dilatation. At $\ell \ge 1$ neither
  reaches an Eulerian field: \eqref{eq:p-slaved}--\eqref{eq:rho-slaved}
  contain $\rho'$ but not $\kappa$.
\item[(ii)] At $N^2 = 0$ ($D = 0$, the adiabatic profile) the reduction
  coincides with the full elastic static fluid, which is then well
  posed. So Dahlen, welded and slipping agree there: $\sim 10^{-10}$ at
  every degree tested above zero on the neutral core.
\item[(iii)] Where $N^2 \ne 0$, Dahlen converges under refinement (to
  machine precision in the one-dimensional reference), while the
  displacement formulations wander within their band
  (\S\ref{sec:why}).
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{The degree-0 hole.} At $\ell = 0$ the argument for
\eqref{eq:p-slaved} fails at its first line. Every degree-0 field is a
function of $r$ alone, the tangential balance is empty, and $f(r)$ in
\eqref{eq:tangential} is a free function. The reduction then leaves one
function undetermined. A discretisation of F1--F3 alone does not detect
this. $F1$ builds in $\rho_1 = \rho'\phi$ and $F3$ builds in the wall
pressure $-\rho_F\phi$, so the discretisation silently computes the
member $f \equiv 0$ of the family, which nothing physical selects.
Because F1--F3 contain no $\kappa$, that member is the same whatever
the stratification. The physical degree-0 response depends on $\kappa$
(through the adiabatic part of $D$ and the compression), but the
computed one is blind to it.

The error is large. The \code{fluid\_core} model has a uniform-density,
compressible fluid core under a solid mantle. Its in-situ gradient
vanishes while its adiabatic one does not, so $D = \rho^2/\kappa > 0$
and $N^2 < 0$. Its degree-0 load Love numbers, the surface radial
displacement $h'_0$ and potential perturbation $k'_0$ under a degree-0
surface load in the library's normalisation, come out of the mixed
class as $h'_0 = -1.457$ and $k'_0 = -5.35$. The spectral reference
(\code{pyslfp}) gives $-0.1321$ and $0$; mass conservation fixes $k'_0
= 0$. On the two-dimensional disc the reduction alone gives a surface
radial displacement $u_r(a) = +3.29$ against $-0.0991$.

Spectral codes avoid the hole by solving $\ell = 0$ with the
displacement description, which computes the fluid's degree-0
compression directly; the harmonics decouple, so this costs nothing at
other degrees. A three-dimensional discretisation has no such sector.
Closing the hole within the three-dimensional problem is one of the two
things the generalisation does. The other is to do without the sphere.

\paragraph{Degree $0$: the closure.} At $\ell = 0$ the level integrals
do not vanish and the border is active. With $V = 4\pi r^2u_r$, $A =
4\pi r^2/g$ and $\mathrm d/\mathrm dc = g^{-1}\partial_r$, one has $V'/A
= \Div\bu$ for a radial field, and
\begin{equation*}
  \Bigl(\frac1A\Bigr)' = \frac1g\,\partial_r\Bigl(\frac{g}{4\pi r^2}\Bigr)
  = \frac{1}{4\pi r^2g}\Bigl(\partial_rg - \frac{2g}{r}\Bigr) .
\end{equation*}
The closure \eqref{eq:closure} with $\delta\Vol = -\bar\Phi = -4\pi
r^2\phi_0/g$, where $\phi_0$ is the degree-0 part of $\phi$, so that
$\delta\Vol/A = -\phi_0$, becomes
\begin{equation}
  \partial_r\bigl(\kappa\Div\bu\bigr)
  + \rho\Bigl(\frac{2g}{r} - \partial_rg\Bigr)u_r
  - \rho\,\partial_r\phi_0 = 0 ,
  \label{eq:deg0}
\end{equation}
the degree-0 radial momentum equation of a compressible fluid, with $f
= p_1 + \rho\phi_0$ the degree-0 pressure.\footnote{Multiplying
\eqref{eq:closure} by $-g$, with $\rho(\delta\Vol/A)' =
-\rho g^{-1}\partial_r\phi_0$, gives the sign shown, and the same sign
follows directly from the radial component of \eqref{eq:momentum} with
$p_1 = -\kappa\Div\bu + \rho g u_r$ and $\rho_1 = -\Div(\rho\bu)$.} Its
dead-gravity limit $\phi_0 = 0$ (background gravity kept, its
perturbation dropped) is $\partial_r(\kappa\Div\bu) + \rho u_r(2g/r -
\partial_rg) = 0$, the degree-0 radial equation of the zero-rigidity
fluid. At the wall $\sigma$ is uniform at $\ell = 0$, so the fill
condition $\oint\sigma = 0$ is $\sigma = 0$: continuity of $u_r$, the
degree-0 matching.

\paragraph{Bookkeeping at degree 0.} Two cancellations occur in the
bordered functional \eqref{eq:L}.
\begin{itemize}
\item For a degree-0 potential, $\int_F\rho'\phi^2\dV =
  \int\rho'\bar\Phi^2/A\dc$, so the term
  $-\tfrac12\int\rho'\bar\Phi^2/A$ cancels the degree-0 part of $F1$
  exactly.
\item On the level wall $F2$ is $\int\rho_Fg(\bu_s\cdot\bn)(\bv\cdot\bn)
  \dS$. Its degree-0 part is $\rho_FgS[\bu_s]S[\bv]/|\Sigma| =
  \rho_FS[\bu_s]S[\bv]/A_w$, because $A_w = |\Sigma|/g$, and the rank-one
  term $-(\rho_F/2A_w)S^2$ --- the null-Lagrangian boundary term of
  \eqref{eq:nulllag} --- cancels it.
\end{itemize}
Both cancelled terms are consequences of the slaving $u_r = -\phi/g$,
which does not hold at degree $0$. $F3$ stays, being the boundary term
that the welded coupling $\int_F\rho\nabla\phi\cdot\bu$ produces under
integration by parts. So at $\ell = 0$ the bordered system is the
welded compressible fluid in a different assembly, which is exact
physics there: a degree-0 field has no along-level component, hence no
relabelling freedom and no wall conflict. This is the compressional
degree-0 problem that spectral codes solve separately with the
displacement kept, now supplied inside the three-dimensional problem.
Off the sphere the level integrals of a general load do not vanish, and
the border is active for every load.

%=====================================================================
\section{A fluid shell: the inner core}
\label{sec:shell}

\status{derived for the two-wall budget; argued and cited for the rigid
modes and the foliation; measured (\S\ref{sec:verification}).}

In the Earth-like case a solid inner core is enclosed by the fluid, so
the fluid region is a shell between an inner wall $\Sigma_i$ (the ICB)
at level $c_i = c_{\min}$ and an outer wall $\Sigma_o$ (the CMB) at
level $c_o = c_{\mathrm{top}}$. Everything above applies; this section
collects what the second wall changes.

\paragraph{Two walls, one body of fluid.} On the ICB $\bn$ points out
of the fluid, into the inner core, and $\nuh = -\bn$. For every level
above the ICB the wall patch below it contains the whole ICB, so
$S_c[\bu_s] = S_i[\bu_s] := \oint_{\Sigma_i}\bu_s\cdot\bn\dS$ for $c_i <
c < c_o$, the volume the inner core vacates. The end conditions
\eqref{eq:bcs} read
\begin{equation*}
  V(c_i) = 0,\qquad V(c_o) = S_i[\bu_s] + S_o[\bu_s] :
\end{equation*}
the body of fluid below the lowest level is empty, and the whole
fluid's volume change is the container's, both walls counted. The flux
of fluid through a level, $U := V - S_i$, is $-S_i$ just above the ICB
(the fluid fills what the inner core vacates, or yields what it
occupies) and $S_o$ at the CMB. In between, \eqref{eq:V-ode-general}
holds with $U$ in the gravity term. Their total, $\int_F\rho_1\dV +
\oint_{\Sigma_i\cup\Sigma_o}\rho_F\,\bu_s\cdot\bn\dS = 0$, is the
conservation of the whole shell's mass in its displaced container.

In the built form \eqref{eq:L-built} each end is a wall constraint,
$U(c_j) = \oint_{\Sigma_j}\bu_s\cdot\nuh\dS$; on the ICB $\nuh = -\bn$,
so the right-hand side there is $-S_i$. Each constraint carries its
multiplier $\lambda_j = \pm f(c_j)$, the two wall pressures: plus at
the CMB and minus at the ICB, the sign recording only the orientation
of $\nuh$ relative to $\bn$. Each constraint and its multiplier are one
piece of bookkeeping: an end of the body of fluid, and the pressure
with which the fluid holds that wall.

\paragraph{The ICB is a level wall.} In a hydrostatic state the ICB is
an equipotential, by the argument of \S\ref{sec:solid-asks}: a density
jump across a transverse wall is incompatible with a hydrostatic solid,
and the ICB carries one. A hydrostatic inner core therefore meets the
fluid on a level wall, and the closure applies unchanged. A non-level
ICB belongs to an aspherical, pre-stressed inner core and is the
transverse-wall case, handled by the referential machinery of
\S\ref{sec:solid} with its admissibility condition \eqref{eq:traction}.

\paragraph{Rigid modes of a floating solid.} The inner core is a solid
component disconnected from the mantle, coupled to the rest of the body
only through gravity and the wall pressure.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Rotations.} A rigid rotation $\mathbf b\times\bx$ of the
  inner core alone transmits nothing through an inviscid fluid. On a
  spherically symmetric model it is an exact null mode of the closed
  problem: it strains nothing, it has $\bu_s\cdot\nabla\Phi_0 = 0$ and
  $\nuhs\cdot\bu_s = 0$ on the ICB, and it moves no mass, since
  $(\mathbf b\times\bx)\cdot\nabla\rho = 0$. On an aspherical model it
  is near-null, with a restoring torque of the order of the asphericity
  (\code{doc/quasi\_static\_models.tex}, ``Enclosed solid regions''). The
  implementation projects these modes on request: the mixed class's
  \code{AddRegionRotations} exists for exactly this.
\item \emph{Translation.} A rigid translation $\mathbf a$ of the inner
  core is not null. Gravity restores it, through the density contrast at
  the ICB. It is the genuinely soft degree-1 mode, the static limit of
  the Slichter problem, and it must never be projected.
\end{itemize}
Neither motion enters the border. A rigid motion sweeps no net volume
through a closed wall, $\oint_{\Sigma_i}\mathbf a\cdot\bn\dS =
\oint_{\Sigma_i}(\mathbf b\times\bx)\cdot\bn\dS = 0$, by the divergence
theorem over the inner core, so both wall constraints are blind to the
inner core's rigid motions on any geometry. The closure neither
stiffens nor softens the Slichter mode, and its conditioning is the
mixed class's. Discretisation error shows first as a spurious
inner-core translation, and the remedy is refinement, not projection.
The inner core's degree-0 breathing, by contrast, does sweep volume
through the ICB, and it couples to the border through the inner wall's
constraint.

\paragraph{The foliation in a shell.} This is where (A2) bites
hardest. A full core in a convex region has convexity theorems for its
levels; a shell between two level walls has none, and the
counterexamples of Hamel, Nadirashvili and Sire (2016) are exactly
convex rings with non-convex level sets (\S\ref{sec:foliation}). For an
Earth-like inner core the assurance is near-sphericity, and an
implementation should check the foliation rather than assume it; the
built checks are (A2a) and, on a shell, the band non-overlap that
follows (\S\ref{sec:implementation}). In two dimensions an annulus also
admits, in principle, a loop in its Reeb graph; it cannot occur when the
inner and outer walls' bands of levels do not overlap, which is the
natural setup assertion for a shell (built: the setup
refuses a shell whose walls' bands overlap). The three-layer
verification case of \S\ref{sec:verification} is a shell of this kind.
%=====================================================================
\section{The referential solid, and the wall seam}
\label{sec:solid}
\label{sec:prestress}

\emph{Status: derived for what the fluid asks of the solid; argued for
the organisation. The wall seam (\S\ref{sec:seam}) is derived and
cross-checked at the identity equilibrium mapping on a level wall, and
the three pointwise identities of Proposition~\ref{prop:seam} were
verified symbolically on random polynomial fields; its implementation is built and gated at
that state (\S\ref{sec:implementation}, gate 1). A general pre-stressed
solid and transverse walls are argued; the mapped variant is not
derived.}

Nothing in Parts~I--III consults the solid's constitution. The fluid relations are Eulerian and local to
$F$, and the solid enters them through only two quantities. One is the
total background potential $\Phi_0$, sourced by every mass including a
pre-stressed solid. The other is the wall's normal displacement. The
closure therefore couples unchanged to the general referential
equations in $(\hat C, \Se, \varphi_e)$
(\code{doc/gravitating\_elasticity.md} \S3.1), of which the mixed
description is the special value $\Se = -p_0 I$, $\varphi_e =
\mathrm{id}$.

The organisation is hybrid: the referential potential $\zeta$ on the
solid and in the vacuum, and the Eulerian potential $\phi$ in the
fluid. It is also the natural organisation for secular motions on an
aspherical reference. The referential solid carries no bare
displacement in its gravity coupling, the advection of the background
potential being absorbed into $\zeta$ (next paragraph). The
equipotential-riding fluid carries no displacement at all. Three
things need care: the potential across the wall, the pull-back of the
fluid terms, and the terms that the hybrid organisation must add on
the wall, the \emph{wall seam} of \S\ref{sec:seam}.

\paragraph{The potential across the wall.} In the solid, the
referential potential perturbation is the Lagrangian one pulled back
to the reference body,
\begin{equation*}
  \zeta = \phi\circ\varphi_e + (\bu\cdot\nabla\Phi_0)\circ\varphi_e .
\end{equation*}
In the fluid there is no displacement, and the same formula gives
$\phi\circ\varphi_e$: $\zeta$ would jump across $\Sigma$ by
$(\bu_s\cdot\nabla\Phi_0)\circ\varphi_e$. Two realisations exist.
\begin{itemize}
\item The displacement can be extended into the fluid by the
  prescribed radial extension $E$ of the slipping classes. This is a
  fixed linear map that carries the wall's displacement into the
  fluid region along radial lines; the slipping formulation uses it
  to keep one potential space. Every fluid term is then written in
  $\phi = \zeta - (E\bu)\cdot\nabla\Phi_0$. This is an exact change of
  variables with no gauge content, since $E\bu$ is never treated as a
  fluid displacement.
\item Alternatively, $\zeta$ can be broken at $\Sigma$, with its jump
  prescribed as $\bu_s\cdot\nabla\Phi_0$.
\end{itemize}
The first keeps one potential space. \S\ref{sec:implementation}
realises it discretely, by conjugating the fluid region's Eulerian
forms, its Laplacian included, with the change of variables.

\paragraph{The fluid terms, pulled back.} The volume source and the
border integrals are assembled on the reference fluid mesh. The
border's basis functions are composed with $\Phi_0\circ\varphi_e$, and
the integrals carry the volume Jacobian $J_e = \det\nabla\varphi_e$.
The wall integrals carry the surface Jacobian. The fill
constraint \eqref{eq:bcs} reads the referential flux of $\bu_s$ through the
cofactor (Nanson-transported) normal. At $\varphi_e = \mathrm{id}$ all
of this is the mixed assembly.

\subsection{What the fluid asks of the solid}
\label{sec:solid-asks}

\emph{Derived.} Traction continuity along a wall requires the solid's
and the fluid's background pressures to agree there. On a transverse
wall that identity, $p_{0S}(c) = p_{0F}(c)$, holds over the whole range
of levels the wall cuts; differentiating along the wall, with
hydrostatic balance \eqref{eq:hydro} on the fluid side, gives $\mathrm
dp_{0S}/\mathrm dc = -\rho_F$. A hydrostatic solid would also have
$\mathrm dp_{0S}/\mathrm dc = -\rho_S$, so $\rho_S = \rho_F$: a
transverse wall with a density contrast requires deviatoric pre-stress
in the solid. This is a constraint on the solid, not on the fluid. It
belongs to the referential description, not to the mixed one, and it is
why the ICB of a hydrostatic model is level (\S\ref{sec:shell}).

\subsection{The wall seam}
\label{sec:seam}

\emph{Status: derived and cross-checked, at the identity equilibrium
mapping and a hydrostatic natural state (continuum; the identity
\eqref{eq:seam-identity} for any wall shape, the kernel list for a
level wall). The extension to a general pre-stressed solid is argued.
The implementation is complete and the cross-check gates below pass at
the two solid assemblies' common discretisation level; the spectral
degree-0 gate passes at $0.47\,\%$, matching the mixed class
(\S\ref{sec:implementation}, gate 1). The derivation is given in full below,
through the ledger of Proposition~\ref{prop:seam}.}

The hybrid organisation puts a referential solid against an Eulerian
fluid. At a hydrostatic natural state ($\Se = -p_0 I$, $\varphi_e =
\mathrm{id}$) the referential and mixed descriptions describe the same
physics, so the hybrid must then reproduce the mixed class exactly.
This subsection derives what has to be added on the wall for that to
hold. The answer is a one-sided form on the solid side of the wall, the
\emph{seam}. It has two parts of comparable size: the \emph{follower}
of the fluid's pressure, and a \emph{gravity seam}, which is the
referential solid's gravitational traction, a traction that an
Eulerian fluid never balances. Neither part contains a density.

\paragraph{Conventions.} In this subsection $\bu$ and $\bv$ are the
solid's displacement and its test function, and $\chi$ is the
potential test function.
\begin{itemize}
\item $\nuhs = -\bn$ is the solid's outward unit normal on
  $\Sigma$ (\S\ref{sec:setting}), the normal of the solid's boundary
  elements. It points into the fluid, towards the centre at a CMB.
  Proposition~\ref{prop:seam} and its ledger hold on any region
  $\Omega$. There the outward normal of $\Omega$ is written $\nuho$,
  and the surface notation below is taken with respect to it. On the
  wall, for $\Omega = M_S$, $\nuho = \nuhs$.
\item $\mathbf g_0 := \nabla\Phi_0$ is the background gravity vector
  (the $\mathbf g_0 = \nabla\zeta^0$ of the referential notes, since
  $\zeta^0 = \Phi_0$ at $\varphi_e = \mathrm{id}$), so $|\mathbf g_0| =
  g$. On $\Sigma$, $\gamma := \nuhs\cdot\mathbf g_0$, which is $-g$
  at a CMB and $+g$ at an ICB.
\item $\mathbf H := \nabla\nabla\Phi_0$; $q := g^2/8\pi G$ and $\pi_\pm
  := p_0 \pm q$.
\item Surface calculus on $\Sigma$: $\mathbf P = I - \nuhs\otimes\nuhs$; $u_\perp = \nuhs\cdot\bu$, the normal
  component, and $\bu_T =
  \mathbf P\bu$; $\nabla_\Sigma f = \mathbf P\nabla f$; $\Div_\Sigma\bv
  = \operatorname{tr}(\mathbf P\,\mathrm D\bv)$, the full surface
  divergence of the trace, normal part included, with $(\mathrm
  D\bv)_{ij} = \partial_jv_i$; and $\partial_{\bw}\mathbf a :=
  (\mathrm D\mathbf a)(\mathbf P\bw)$, the derivative of a field
  $\mathbf a$ along the tangential part of $\bw$.
\item $\mathbf L := \nabla_\Sigma\nuhs$ is the Weingarten map,
  symmetric on the tangent plane. On a sphere or circle of radius
  $r_\Sigma$, $\mathbf L = -\mathbf P/r_\Sigma$ and $\operatorname{tr}
  \mathbf L = -(d-1)/r_\Sigma$.
\end{itemize}
Forms are written $\mathcal A(\bu, \phi \,|\, \bv, \chi)$. They are
symmetric bilinear, with the assembled block operator as their matrix.

\paragraph{The two solid-side forms, as assembled.} The mixed class
(\code{src/mixed\_problem.cpp}) assembles on the solid $M_S$, with
$C^{\mathrm{eff}}$ the effective (seismological, PREM-type) elastic
tensor:
\begin{equation*}
\begin{aligned}
  \mathcal A_{\mathrm{mix}} ={}&
  \int_{M_S} C^{\mathrm{eff}}\varepsilon(\bu):\varepsilon(\bv)
  + \tfrac12\int_{M_S}\rho\bigl[\bv\cdot\nabla(\bu\cdot\mathbf g_0)
     + \bu\cdot\nabla(\bv\cdot\mathbf g_0)\bigr] \\
  &- \tfrac12\int_{M_S}\rho\bigl[(\bv\cdot\mathbf g_0)\Div\bu
     + (\bu\cdot\mathbf g_0)\Div\bv\bigr]
  + \int_{M_S}\rho\,(\nabla\phi\cdot\bv + \nabla\chi\cdot\bu) \\
  &
  + \frac{1}{4\pi G}\Bigl[\int_B\nabla\phi\cdot\nabla\chi
     + \mathrm{DtN}(\phi, \chi)\Bigr]
  + \text{F1} + \text{F2} + \text{F3} .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
Its second and third terms are the symmetrised advective gravity
terms. Its loads, for a surface mass load $\sigma_L$ on the outer
surface $\partial M$, are $-\oint_{\partial M}\sigma_L\,(\mathbf
g_0\cdot\bv) - \oint_{\partial M}\sigma_L\chi$. This is the
$\mathcal A$ of \S\ref{sec:dahlen}.

The referential class (\code{src/referential\_problem.cpp}) works at
$\varphi_e = \mathrm{id}$. It assembles on $M_S$ and, through the
prescribed vacuum extension $\bar\bu = E_{\mathrm{vac}}\bu$ (which
tapers to zero at the DtN sphere), on the vacuum buffer $V_{\mathrm
b}$:
\begin{equation*}
\begin{aligned}
  \mathcal A_{\mathrm{ref}} ={}&
  \int_{M_S}\bigl[\hat C\varepsilon(\bu):\varepsilon(\bv)
     - p_0\,\mathrm D\bu:\mathrm D\bv\bigr]
  + \frac{1}{8\pi G}\int_{M_S\cup V_{\mathrm b}}
     \langle a''(\bar\bu, \bar\bv)\mathbf g_0, \mathbf g_0\rangle \\
  &+ \frac{1}{4\pi G}\int_{M_S\cup V_{\mathrm b}}
     \bigl[\langle a'(\bar\bv)\mathbf g_0, \nabla\zeta\rangle
     + \langle a'(\bar\bu)\mathbf g_0, \nabla\chi\rangle\bigr]
  + \frac{1}{4\pi G}\Bigl[\int_B\nabla\zeta\cdot\nabla\chi
     + \mathrm{DtN}(\zeta, \chi)\Bigr] .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
Its load is $-\oint_{\partial M}\sigma_L\chi$, in the potential row
only. The ingredients are:
\begin{itemize}
\item $a'$ and $a''$, the first and second variations of $a(F) =
  JF^{-1}F^{-\mathsf T}$ at the identity
  (\code{doc/gravitating\_elasticity.md} \S3.1), in the convention
  $a(I + \mathrm D\bu) = I + a'(\bu) + \tfrac12 a''(\bu, \bu) +
  O(\bu^3)$. Expanding $J = \det F$ and $F^{-1}$ to second order,
  \begin{align*}
    a'(\bu) ={}& (\Div\bu)\,I - \mathrm D\bu - \mathrm D\bu^{\mathsf T},\\
    a''(\bu, \bv) ={}& \mathcal N(\bu, \bv)\,I
    - \Div\bu\,\bigl(\mathrm D\bv + \mathrm D\bv^{\mathsf T}\bigr)
    - \Div\bv\,\bigl(\mathrm D\bu + \mathrm D\bu^{\mathsf T}\bigr)\\
    &+ \mathrm D\bu\,\mathrm D\bv + \mathrm D\bv\,\mathrm D\bu
    + \mathrm D\bu^{\mathsf T}\mathrm D\bv^{\mathsf T}
    + \mathrm D\bv^{\mathsf T}\mathrm D\bu^{\mathsf T}
    + \mathrm D\bu\,\mathrm D\bv^{\mathsf T}
    + \mathrm D\bv\,\mathrm D\bu^{\mathsf T},
  \end{align*}
  with $\mathcal N(\bu, \bv) = \Div\bu\Div\bv -
  \operatorname{tr}(\mathrm D\bu\,\mathrm D\bv)$ the null Lagrangian
  of the ledger below. (A check: along a finite rigid rotation $a
  \equiv I$, so $a''$ must satisfy $a''(\bu, \bu) = -a'(\bw)$ for
  antisymmetric $\mathrm D\bu$ with $\mathrm D\bw = (\mathrm D\bu)^2$;
  it does.);
\item $\hat C = C^{\mathrm{eff}} - p_0(\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl} -
  \delta_{il}\delta_{jk} - \delta_{ik}\delta_{jl})$, the bare tensor
  of that note's dictionary.
\end{itemize}
The origin of the referential form is the exact energy of a
gravitating elastic solid written referentially. If $\psi$ maps the
reference body to the current one, with $F = \nabla\psi$ and $J = \det
F$, and $\zeta := \Phi\circ\psi$ is the potential composed with the
map, then $(\nabla\Phi)\circ\psi = F^{-\mathsf T}\nabla\zeta$ and the
gravitational self-energy becomes $-\tfrac{1}{8\pi G}\int\langle
a(F)\nabla\zeta, \nabla\zeta\rangle\,\mathrm dV_{\mathrm{ref}}$, while
Poisson's equation pulls back to $\Div\bigl(a(F)\nabla\zeta\bigr) =
4\pi G\rho_{\mathrm{ref}}$, the reference density being a fixed field.
Geometrically, $a(F) = \sqrt{\det\mathsf G}\,\mathsf G^{-1}$ with
$\mathsf G = F^{\mathsf T}F$ the pulled-back metric: the self-energy is
$-\tfrac{1}{8\pi G}\int\mathrm d\zeta\wedge\star_{\mathsf G}\mathrm
d\zeta$ with the Hodge star of the pulled-back metric, the mass form
pulls back to $\psi^*\varpi = \rho_{\mathrm{ref}}\,\vol_{\mathrm{ref}}$,
and the referential description is the Eulerian one transported to the
reference body together with its metric. The gravity terms of
$\mathcal A_{\mathrm{ref}}$ are the polarisation of the second-order
expansion of $\tfrac{1}{8\pi G}\int\langle a(I + \mathrm
D\bu)\nabla(\Phi_0 + \zeta), \nabla(\Phi_0 + \zeta)\rangle$, in the
mixed sign convention in which the potential's own term enters with a
plus and its stationarity is Poisson's equation. Linearising at
$\varphi_e = \mathrm{id}$ gives $\zeta = \phi + \bu\cdot\mathbf g_0$,
the Lagrangian perturbation of the potential.

The question is what the two forms differ by once $\zeta$ is expressed
through $\phi$.

\paragraph{The change of variables, region by region.} At $\varphi_e
= \mathrm{id}$ the referential potential is $\zeta = \phi +
\bu\cdot\mathbf g_0$. The comparison is made one region at a time.

\begin{proposition}[The change of variables on one region; derived]
\label{prop:seam}
Let $\Omega$ be a region on which $\nabla^2\Phi_0 = 4\pi G\rho$ and,
if $\Omega$ carries elasticity, $\nabla p_0 = -\rho\,\mathbf g_0$ with
the bare tensor $\hat C$. Write $\mathcal A^\Omega_{\mathrm{ref}}$ and
$\mathcal A^\Omega_{\mathrm{mix}}$ for the parts of the two forms
supported in $\Omega$; for the mixed form these are the
$C^{\mathrm{eff}}$, advective, $\rho\nabla\phi$ and Laplacian terms.
Then for all smooth fields
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal A^\Omega_{\mathrm{ref}}(\bu, \phi + \bu\cdot\mathbf g_0
  \,|\, \bv, \chi + \bv\cdot\mathbf g_0)
  = \mathcal A^\Omega_{\mathrm{mix}}(\bu, \phi \,|\, \bv, \chi)
  + R_{\partial\Omega}(\bu, \phi \,|\, \bv, \chi),
  \label{eq:seam-identity}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation*}
  R_{\partial\Omega} = \oint_{\partial\Omega}\Bigl\{
  -p_0\,\nuho\cdot\mathbf X^{\mathrm s}(\bu, \bv)
  + \frac{1}{4\pi G}\,\nuho\cdot\mathbf Z^{\mathrm s}(\bu, \bv)
  + \frac{1}{4\pi G}\bigl[\mathbf t_{\bv}\cdot\nabla_\Sigma\phi
  + \mathbf t_{\bu}\cdot\nabla_\Sigma\chi\bigr]\Bigr\}\dS ,
\end{equation*}
with $\nuho$ the outward normal of $\Omega$ (on the wall, for
$\Omega = M_S$, $\nuho = \nuhs$), superscript
$\mathrm s$ the symmetrisation in $(\bu, \bv)$, and
\begin{align*}
  \mathbf X(\bu, \bv) &= \bu\Div\bv - (\bu\cdot\nabla)\bv,
  &\nuho\cdot\mathbf X &= u_\perp\Div_\Sigma\bv
     - \nuho\cdot\partial_{\bu}\bv,\\
  \mathbf Z(\bu, \bv) &= \tfrac12 g^2\,\mathbf X(\bu, \bv)
     + \bu\,(\mathbf g_0\cdot\mathbf H\bv)
     - \mathbf g_0\,(\bu\cdot\mathbf H\bv),
  &\nuho\cdot\mathbf Z &= \tfrac12 g^2\,\nuho\cdot\mathbf X
     + \mathbf t_{\bu}\cdot\mathbf H\bv,\\
  \mathbf t_{\bu} &= (\nuho\cdot\bu)\,\mathbf g_0
     - (\nuho\cdot\mathbf g_0)\,\bu
     = \nuho\times(\mathbf g_0\times\bu) ,
\end{align*}
where $\mathbf t_{\bu}$ is tangent to $\partial\Omega$.
\end{proposition}

\paragraph{The integration-by-parts ledger.} The proof is three
integrations by parts. Each uses one volume identity, and no other
property of the region.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{The elastic part uses hydrostatics, not Poisson.} By the
  dictionary, $\hat C\varepsilon:\varepsilon - p_0\,\mathrm D\bu:
  \mathrm D\bv = C^{\mathrm{eff}}\varepsilon:\varepsilon -
  p_0\mathcal N(\bu, \bv)$, where $\mathcal N(\bu, \bv) =
  \Div\bu\Div\bv - \operatorname{tr}(\mathrm D\bu\,\mathrm D\bv) =
  \Div\mathbf X(\bu, \bv)$ is the classical null Lagrangian. Integrate
  by parts and use $\nabla p_0 = -\rho\,\mathbf g_0$, symmetrised:
  \begin{equation*}
  \begin{aligned}
    -\int p_0\mathcal N
    ={}& -\oint p_0\,\nuho\cdot\mathbf X^{\mathrm s}
      - \tfrac12\int\rho\bigl[(\mathbf g_0\cdot\bu)\Div\bv
         + (\mathbf g_0\cdot\bv)\Div\bu\bigr] \\
      &+ \tfrac12\int\rho\bigl[\mathbf g_0\cdot(\bu\cdot\nabla)\bv
         + \mathbf g_0\cdot(\bv\cdot\nabla)\bu\bigr] .
  \end{aligned}
  \end{equation*}
  The second term is the mixed class's divergence-advective pair
  exactly. Since $\bv\cdot\nabla(\bu\cdot\mathbf g_0) = \mathbf
  g_0\cdot(\bv\cdot\nabla)\bu + \bu^{\mathsf T}\mathbf H\bv$, the third
  is its gradient-advective pair minus $\int\rho\,\bu^{\mathsf T}
  \mathbf H\bv$. This is the \emph{$C^{\mathrm{eff}}$ rearrangement}.
  The volume part of $-p_0\Div\mathbf X$ becomes the advective gravity
  through hydrostatics; the boundary part is minus the follower.
\item \emph{The gravity--gravity part uses Poisson, not hydrostatics.}
  After the substitution, the $\bu$--$\bu$ terms of the referential
  form are
  \begin{equation*}
  \begin{aligned}
    \Gamma(\bu, \bv) ={}& \frac{1}{8\pi G}\int\langle a''(\bu, \bv)
    \mathbf g_0, \mathbf g_0\rangle \\
    &+ \frac{1}{4\pi G}\int\bigl[\langle a'(\bv)\mathbf g_0,
    \nabla(\bu\cdot\mathbf g_0)\rangle + \langle a'(\bu)\mathbf g_0,
    \nabla(\bv\cdot\mathbf g_0)\rangle + \nabla(\bu\cdot\mathbf
    g_0)\cdot\nabla(\bv\cdot\mathbf g_0)\bigr] .
  \end{aligned}
  \end{equation*}
  Every term quadratic in $\mathbf g_0$ and first order in both
  gradients cancels except $\tfrac12 g^2\mathcal N$. Then
  $\operatorname{tr}\mathbf H = 4\pi G\rho$ and the full symmetry of
  $\nabla\nabla\nabla\Phi_0$ give $\Gamma(\bu, \bv) = \int\rho\,
  \bu^{\mathsf T}\mathbf H\bv + (4\pi G)^{-1}\oint\nuho\cdot\mathbf
  Z(\bu, \bv)$. Its volume term cancels the one the elastic part left.
\item \emph{The coupling uses Poisson.} $(4\pi G)^{-1}[\langle
  a'(\bv)\mathbf g_0, \nabla\phi\rangle + \nabla\phi\cdot\nabla(\bv
  \cdot\mathbf g_0)] = \rho\,\bv\cdot\nabla\phi + (4\pi G)^{-1}
  \Div[\bv(\mathbf g_0\cdot\nabla\phi) - \mathbf g_0(\bv\cdot
  \nabla\phi)]$. The volume part is the mixed coupling $\int\rho\,
  \nabla\phi\cdot\bv$, and the boundary flux is $\nuho\cdot[\bv
  (\mathbf g_0\cdot\nabla\phi) - \mathbf g_0(\bv\cdot\nabla\phi)] =
  \mathbf t_{\bv}\cdot\nabla_\Sigma\phi$. The two pieces taken
  separately each carry $\partial_{\nuho}\phi$ on $\partial\Omega$; only
  their sum is free of it.
\end{itemize}
Table~\ref{tab:seam-ledger} collects the result.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\small
\caption{The integration-by-parts ledger of
Proposition~\ref{prop:seam}: each group of referential terms on a
region $\Omega$, after the substitution $\zeta = \phi + \bu\cdot\mathbf
g_0$, splits into a mixed-class volume term and a boundary term on
$\partial\Omega$, using one volume identity.}
\label{tab:seam-ledger}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.27\textwidth}p{0.27\textwidth}p{0.22\textwidth}l@{}}
\toprule
referential terms & volume part (mixed term) & boundary part & identity \\
\midrule
$\int[\hat C\varepsilon:\varepsilon - p_0\,\mathrm D\bu:\mathrm D\bv]$
  & $\int C^{\mathrm{eff}}\varepsilon:\varepsilon$, both advective pairs, $-\int\rho\,\bu^{\mathsf T}\mathbf H\bv$
  & $-\oint p_0\,\nuho\cdot\mathbf X^{\mathrm s}$ & $\nabla p_0 = -\rho\,\mathbf g_0$ \\
$a''$, both $a'$ terms with $\bu\cdot\mathbf g_0$, Laplacian of $\bu\cdot\mathbf g_0$
  & $+\int\rho\,\bu^{\mathsf T}\mathbf H\bv$
  & $+(4\pi G)^{-1}\oint\nuho\cdot\mathbf Z^{\mathrm s}$ & $\nabla^2\Phi_0 = 4\pi G\rho$ \\
$a'(\bv)$ against $\nabla\phi$, Laplacian cross term
  & $\int\rho\,\nabla\phi\cdot\bv$
  & $+(4\pi G)^{-1}\oint\mathbf t_{\bv}\cdot\nabla_\Sigma\phi$ & $\nabla^2\Phi_0 = 4\pi G\rho$ \\
transpose & $\int\rho\,\nabla\chi\cdot\bu$
  & $+(4\pi G)^{-1}\oint\mathbf t_{\bu}\cdot\nabla_\Sigma\chi$ & $\nabla^2\Phi_0 = 4\pi G\rho$ \\
$\int\nabla\phi\cdot\nabla\chi$ & the same & $0$ & --- \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

Three structural facts come out of the steps.
\begin{enumerate}
\item The normal derivatives cancel \emph{pointwise} inside $\nuho\cdot\mathbf X$, $\nuho\cdot\mathbf Z$ and $\mathbf t\cdot
  \nabla\phi$. Every boundary term is therefore a trace, or a
  tangential derivative of a trace.
\item $\mathbf H$ enters only as $\mathbf H\mathbf t$ with $\mathbf t$
  tangent. That is the tangential derivative of $\mathbf g_0$ along
  $\partial\Omega$, which is continuous across $\partial\Omega$.
\item No density appears on $\partial\Omega$. $\rho$ enters only
  through the two volume identities: hydrostatics in the elastic part,
  Poisson in the gravity parts.
\end{enumerate}

An independent check by domain variation reproduces $\Gamma(\bu, \bu)$
term by term. The check expands $\int_\Omega\langle a(\nabla\psi)
\nabla(\Phi_0\circ\psi), \nabla(\Phi_0\circ\psi)\rangle =
\int_{\psi(\Omega)}|\nabla\Phi_0|^2$ to second order in $\psi =
\mathrm{id} + \epsilon\bu$. It also gives $R$ a plain reading. $R$ is
the domain variation of the solid region's field energy, plus the
background flux against the second-order part of the referential
potential. Both are artefacts of describing gravity referentially on a
sub-region only.

\paragraph{Where the boundary terms land.} Apply
Proposition~\ref{prop:seam} to two regions: $\Omega = M_S$ (elastic,
$\rho = \rho_S$), and $\Omega = V_{\mathrm b}$ ($\rho = 0$, no
elasticity, $\bar\bu = E_{\mathrm{vac}}\bu$).
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{The outer surface $\partial M$.} $p_0 = 0$ there, which
  kills the elastic term. The gravity terms are traces and tangential
  derivatives only, $\bar\bu = \bu$ there, and the solid and vacuum
  sides carry opposite normals. So they cancel identically, for any
  surface shape. Under $\chi_\zeta = \chi + \bv\cdot\mathbf g_0$ the
  referential load becomes $-\oint\sigma_L\chi_\zeta = -\oint\sigma_L
  \chi - \oint\sigma_L(\bv\cdot\mathbf g_0)$, exactly the mixed
  class's two loads. The free-surface equivalence needs nothing.
\item \emph{The DtN sphere.} The extension and its gradient vanish
  there, so $\zeta = \phi$ and every term vanishes.
\item \emph{The wall $\Sigma$.} Only the solid side is referential.
  The fluid side carries the mixed class's own Eulerian forms,
  conjugated exactly (\S\ref{sec:implementation}), and nothing there
  cancels $R_\Sigma$.
\end{itemize}
The hybrid as assembled without a seam is therefore $\mathcal
A_{\mathrm{mix}} + R^{M_S}_\Sigma$, and the seam to be added is
\begin{equation*}
  \mathcal W_\Sigma := -R^{M_S}_\Sigma .
\end{equation*}
The fluid side is already right. F1, the fluid Laplacian and F3,
conjugated, are the mixed class's forms by construction. In $(\bu,
\zeta)$ on a level wall the conjugated F3 is $-\oint\rho_F(\zeta v_\perp +
\chi u_\perp) + 2\oint\rho_F\gamma\,u_\perp v_\perp$. With F2 $=
-\oint\rho_F\gamma\,u_\perp v_\perp$, the net fluid-side wall form is
$-\oint\rho_F(\zeta v_\perp + \chi u_\perp) + \oint\rho_F\gamma\,u_\perp v_\perp$. All
the fluid-side density is there.

\paragraph{The seam in hybrid variables.} On $\Sigma$, substitute
$\phi = \zeta - \bu\cdot\mathbf g_0$ and $\chi_\phi = \chi -
\bv\cdot\mathbf g_0$. The trace $\bu\cdot\mathbf g_0$ is the same from
both sides, since $E\bu = \bu$ on $\Sigma$. Then use $\mathbf
t\cdot\mathbf H\bv = \mathbf t\cdot\nabla_\Sigma(\bv\cdot\mathbf g_0) -
\mathbf g_0\cdot\partial_{\mathbf t}\bv$ for tangent $\mathbf t$. The
result is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
  \mathcal W_\Sigma ={}& \oint_\Sigma\pi_-\,\nuhs\cdot\mathbf
  X^{\mathrm s}(\bu, \bv)\dS
  + \frac{1}{8\pi G}\oint_\Sigma\mathbf g_0\cdot\bigl(\partial_{\mathbf
  t_{\bu}}\bv + \partial_{\mathbf t_{\bv}}\bu\bigr)\dS \\
  &- \frac{1}{4\pi G}\oint_\Sigma\bigl[\mathbf t_{\bv}\cdot\nabla_\Sigma
  (\zeta - \tfrac12\bu\cdot\mathbf g_0) + \mathbf t_{\bu}\cdot
  \nabla_\Sigma(\chi - \tfrac12\bv\cdot\mathbf g_0)\bigr]\dS .
\end{aligned}
  \label{eq:seam}
\end{equation}
The potential enters against $\zeta - \tfrac12\bu\cdot\mathbf g_0 =
\tfrac12(\zeta + \phi)$, the mean of the referential and Eulerian
potentials on the wall.

On a level wall, $\mathbf g_0 = \gamma\nuhs$, $\mathbf t_{\bw} =
-\gamma\bw_T$ and $\bu\cdot\mathbf g_0 = \gamma u_\perp$, and the seam is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
  \mathcal W_\Sigma ={}& \oint\pi_-\,\nuhs\cdot\mathbf X^{\mathrm s}
  (\bu, \bv)
  - \oint q\,\bigl[\nuhs\cdot\partial_{\bv}\bu
     + \nuhs\cdot\partial_{\bu}\bv\bigr] \\
  &+ \frac{1}{4\pi G}\oint\gamma\bigl[\bv_T\cdot\nabla_\Sigma(\zeta -
  \tfrac12\gamma u_\perp) + \bu_T\cdot\nabla_\Sigma(\chi -
  \tfrac12\gamma v_\perp)\bigr] .
\end{aligned}
  \label{eq:seam-level}
\end{equation}
For interpretation and spectral checks only, a smooth, closed level
wall with constant $p_0$ and $g$ gives, after a surface integration by
parts,
\begin{equation*}
\begin{aligned}
  \mathcal W_\Sigma ={}& \oint\pi_+\bigl[\bu_T\cdot\mathbf L\bv_T -
  \bu_T\cdot\nabla_\Sigma v_\perp - \bv_T\cdot\nabla_\Sigma u_\perp\bigr]
  + \oint\pi_-\,(\operatorname{tr}\mathbf L)\,u_\perp v_\perp \\
  &+ \frac{1}{4\pi G}\oint\gamma\bigl[\bv_T\cdot\nabla_\Sigma\zeta +
  \bu_T\cdot\nabla_\Sigma\chi\bigr] .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation*}

\paragraph{The wall-term list.} Four kernels express the seam at the
identity map, on a level wall. They are one-sided, on the solid's
boundary elements, with normal $\nuhs$. For $\mathsf G_P$ and
$\mathsf G_A$ the first argument is the test slot and the second the
trial slot.
\begin{itemize}
\item $\mathsf G_P[\pi](\mathbf a, \bw) = \oint\pi\,\nuhs\cdot\partial_{\bw}\mathbf a$, the existing
  \code{SlipInterfacePressureIntegrator}$(\pi)$.
\item $\mathsf G_A(\mathbf a, \bw) = \oint(q\,\nuhs - (\gamma/4\pi
  G)\,\mathbf g_0)\cdot\partial_{\bw}\mathbf a$, the existing
  \code{SlipInterfaceGravityIntegrator}.
\item $\mathsf G_s(\bw, s) = (4\pi G)^{-1}\oint\gamma\,(\mathbf
  P\bw)\cdot\nabla_\Sigma s$, with a vector test $\bw$ and a scalar
  trial $s$: the existing\\ \code{SlipInterfaceGravityScalarIntegrator}.
\item $\mathsf N[\pi](\bu, \bv) = \tfrac12\oint\pi\,[u_\perp\Div_\Sigma
  \bv + v_\perp\Div_\Sigma\bu]$, a \emph{new} kernel, normal times surface
  divergence.
\end{itemize}
Since $\oint\pi\,\nuhs\cdot\mathbf X^{\mathrm s} = \mathsf N[\pi]
- \tfrac12(\mathsf G_P[\pi] + \mathsf G_P[\pi]^{\mathsf T})$, the seam
on a level wall at the identity map is
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
  \mathcal W_\Sigma ={}& \mathsf N[p_0 - q]
  - \tfrac12\bigl(\mathsf G_P[p_0] + \mathsf G_P[p_0]^{\mathsf T}\bigr)
  + \tfrac12\bigl(\mathsf G_A + \mathsf G_A^{\mathsf T}\bigr) \\
  &+ \mathsf G_s(\bv, \zeta - \tfrac12\bu\cdot\mathbf g_0)
  + \mathsf G_s(\bu, \chi - \tfrac12\bv\cdot\mathbf g_0),
  \qquad q = \frac{|\nabla\zeta^0|^2}{8\pi G} .
\end{aligned}
  \label{eq:seam-kernels}
\end{equation}
In block terms:
\begin{itemize}
\item the $\mathsf N$ kernel and the $\mathsf G_P$ and $\mathsf G_A$
  pairs are displacement--displacement forms;
\item $\mathsf G_s(\bv, \zeta)$ is a mixed form $B_s$ from potential to
  displacement, added to the coupling block, with its transpose in the
  potential row;
\item the $-\tfrac12\bu\cdot\mathbf g_0$ parts are the
  displacement--displacement form $-\tfrac12(B_sS + S^{\mathsf
  T}B_s^{\mathsf T})$, with $S$ the glue matrix of
  \S\ref{sec:implementation}. Only the rows of $S$ at wall nodes enter,
  where $E\bu = \bu$, so the seam is independent of the extension, as
  it must be.
\end{itemize}
On a level wall $\mathsf G_A = -\mathsf G_P[q]$. The two pressure
pairs therefore combine into one pressure kernel at $p_0 + q$, but the
$\mathsf G_A$ form is the one \eqref{eq:seam} produces off level walls.

For the new kernel, take scalar shapes $\eta_a$ with surface gradients
$\mathbf s_a = \nabla_\Sigma\eta_a$ and quadrature weight $w$, with
row $=$ test $\eta_a\mathbf e_i$ and column $=$ trial $\eta_b\mathbf
e_j$. Its element matrix is $w\,\pi\,\tfrac12[(\nuhs)_i\eta_a(\mathbf
s_b)_j + (\mathbf s_a)_i (\nuhs)_j\eta_b]$, which is symmetric. On a faceted
wall (order-1 geometry) the element-wise curvature is zero. The
intrinsic kernels capture the curvature through the surface gradients
of the finite-element fields. The curvature-explicit smooth form above
must not be assembled with facet geometry, because it would silently
lose $\mathbf L$.

\paragraph{What the seam is: the follower, and a gravity seam.}
\eqref{eq:seam} has two parts.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{The follower.} Define
  \begin{equation*}
    F_f(\bu, \bv) := \oint p_0\,\bigl[v_\perp\Div_\Sigma\bu - \nuhs\cdot\partial_{\bv}\bu\bigr] = \oint p_0\,\nuhs\cdot\mathbf
    X(\bv, \bu) .
  \end{equation*}
  Since $\delta^2|F| = -\oint\nuhs\cdot\mathbf X$, $F_f$ is the
  second variation of $-p_0|F|$: the pressure times the volume the wall
  encloses. When $p_0$ is constant on $\Sigma$, $F_f$ is symmetric and
  equals $\mathsf N[p_0] - \tfrac12(\mathsf G_P[p_0] + \mathsf
  G_P[p_0]^{\mathsf T})$. The slip pressure kernel is only its second
  half, and it is \emph{not} symmetric by itself, even at constant
  $p_0$:
  \begin{equation*}
    \mathsf G_P(\bu, \bv) - \mathsf G_P(\bv, \bu) = \oint p_0\bigl(
    \bv_T\cdot\nabla_\Sigma u_\perp - \bu_T\cdot\nabla_\Sigma v_\perp\bigr).
  \end{equation*}
  Only the full follower is symmetric.
\item \emph{The gravity seam.} The rest is
  \begin{equation*}
    -\mathsf N[q] + \tfrac12(\mathsf G_A + \mathsf G_A^{\mathsf T}) +
    \mathsf G_s(\bv, \zeta - \tfrac12\bu\cdot\mathbf g_0) + \mathsf
    G_s(\bu, \chi - \tfrac12\bv\cdot\mathbf g_0) .
  \end{equation*}
  It is the referential solid's gravitational traction. In the
  referential organisation gravity acts through a Piola stress, at the
  identity $T_g = (8\pi G)^{-1}[g^2 I - 2\mathbf g_0\otimes\mathbf
  g_0]$. Its traction on a level wall, $T_g\nuhs = -q\,\nuhs$,
  is the vector of the $\mathsf G_A$ kernel. A referential medium on
  the other side of the wall would carry the same traction and balance
  it. An Eulerian fluid carries none, so the seam must supply it on the
  solid side. It is of the same size as the follower. These sizes are
  computed from the model, not measured. On the library's
  two-dimensional test model, $q/p_0 \approx 0.26$ at the CMB. The
  seam's shear part, the wall traction $-(\gamma/4\pi
  G)\nabla_\Sigma(\zeta - \tfrac12\bu\cdot\mathbf g_0)$, is comparable
  at degree $2$ to F3's normal coupling.
\end{itemize}

The follower is therefore not the only wall term that the mixed
description hides. The gravity seam is a second, of the same size.
The mixed solid absorbs the follower through the $C^{\mathrm{eff}}$
rearrangement of the elastic part,
\begin{equation*}
  \hat C\varepsilon:\varepsilon - p_0\,\mathrm D\bu:\mathrm D\bv =
  C^{\mathrm{eff}}\varepsilon:\varepsilon - p_0\Div\mathbf X ,
\end{equation*}
whose volume part becomes the advective gravity through hydrostatics
and whose boundary part is $-F_f$. It does not absorb it through its
advective form. The follower involves the background pressure $p_0$;
it is unrelated to the multiplier $\lambda$ of \S\ref{sec:weak}, which
is the perturbation $f(c_w)$ of the wall pressure.

\paragraph{The fluid half of F3.} No density appears in the seam. The
solid's half of F3, $-\rho_S\,\bu_s\cdot\bn$, is inside the referential
potential row automatically, as it is inside the mixed coupling,
because $\rho$ is a fixed referential field. The fluid's half,
$+\rho_F\,\bu_s\cdot\bn$, is added explicitly, as in the mixed
description. There is no solid-side buoyancy term. The linearised
gravitational traction of the referential solid does contain $\rho_S$,
through $\nuhs\cdot\mathbf H\nuhs$ and the solid-side
$\partial_{\nuhs}\phi$. Those normal-derivative pieces cancel against the
potential row's boundary terms under the change of variables
(the coupling step of the ledger), and they do not survive into the
seam.

\paragraph{Cross-checks.} Five independent checks pin the seam, signs
and factors included.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{Rigid rotation is exactly null.} Take $\bu = \bomega\times
  \bx$ on a radial model. The mixed form gives zero ($\varepsilon = 0$,
  $\bu\cdot\mathbf g_0 = 0$, $u_\perp = 0$). The referential solid without
  a seam gives $(\pi_+/r_\Sigma)\oint_\Sigma|\bu|^2$, in any dimension:
  $\rho g = -\partial_rp_0$ and one radial integration by parts turn
  the volume terms into the $p_0$ part, and $\nuhs\cdot\mathbf Z$
  gives the $q$ part. The seam gives $-(\pi_+/r_\Sigma)\oint|\bu|^2$,
  so the rotation is null. The pressure pair $-\tfrac12(\mathsf G_P +
  \mathsf G_P^{\mathsf T})$ at $p_0$ alone removes only the $p_0$ part,
  leaving $(q/r_\Sigma)\oint|\bu_T|^2$.
\item \emph{Degree 0 is pressure times volume change.} Take $\bu =
  U\nuhs$ on a sphere. Only the $\mathsf N$ kernel acts, and
  $\mathcal W_\Sigma = \pi_-(\operatorname{tr}\mathbf L)\oint U^2$. Its
  pressure half, $p_0(\operatorname{tr}\mathbf L)\oint U^2 = -8\pi p_0
  r_\Sigma U^2$ in three dimensions, is the second derivative of
  $-p_0|F|$ with $|F| = \tfrac{4\pi}{3}(r_\Sigma - U)^3$: the
  pressure--volume term.
\item \emph{The slipping classes' forms are recovered.} Apply the
  Proposition to both sides of a slipping interface, both referential,
  the fluid's normal being $-\nuhs$. Write $\mathbf a =
  \tfrac12(\bu_s + \bu_f)$ for the mean trace and $\mathbf s = \bu_f -
  \bu_s$ for the tangential slip.
  \begin{itemize}
  \item The elastic terms give minus the interface pressure form
    $B_\Sigma$ of \code{doc/slip\_interface.tex} (Proposition ``The
    interface form''): in that note's Hessian normalisation, at the
    identity map,
    \begin{equation*}
      B_\Sigma(\bv, \bv)
      = -\oint_\Sigma \pi\,\boldsymbol\nu\cdot\partial_{\mathbf s}
        \bigl(\bv_{\mathrm s} + \bv_{\mathrm f}\bigr)\dS ,
      \qquad \mathbf s = \bv_{\mathrm s} - \bv_{\mathrm f} ,
    \end{equation*}
    used in its symmetrised polarisation, with $\pi$ the interface
    pressure (here $p_0$), $\boldsymbol\nu$ the interface normal in
    that note's orientation, and $\mathbf s$ minus the tangential slip.
    So $B_\Sigma$ is the two-sided version of the
    follower, and the mixed organisation needs no slip term on a level
    wall.
  \item The gravity terms give the broken-$\zeta$ form $G_\Sigma$ of
    the same note (Proposition ``The gravity interface form'') in its
    Eulerian intermediate form: at the identity map, where that note's
    $\mathbf b$ is $\mathbf g_0$ and $|\mathbf b|^2/8\pi G = q$,
    \begin{equation*}
      G_\Sigma = \oint_\Sigma q\,\Bigl[
        \boldsymbol\nu\cdot\partial_{\mathbf s}
          \bigl(\bv_{\mathrm s} + \bv_{\mathrm f}\bigr)
        + \partial_{\mathbf s}\bigl(\zeta^1_{\mathrm s}
          + \zeta^1_{\mathrm f}\bigr)
        - \mathbf g_0\cdot\partial_{\mathbf s}
          \bigl(\bv_{\mathrm s} + \bv_{\mathrm f}\bigr)
      \Bigr]\dS ,
    \end{equation*}
    with the same $\mathbf s$, used in its symmetrised polarisation
    over the product space. The agreement is term by term after one
    surface integration by parts, with the curvature terms cancelling.
    $G_\Sigma$ is finite-difference verified in the library
    (\code{BrokenZetaGravityIdentity} in \code{TestSlipInterface}), so
    this is an independent check of $\mathbf Z$ and of the coupling's
    boundary term.
  \end{itemize}
  The seam is the one-sided counterpart. The fluid side has no
  displacement, so both the jump and the mean trace are replaced by
  the solid trace. The half in $\zeta - \tfrac12\bu\cdot\mathbf g_0$ is
  the residue of that.
\item \emph{AC18, eq.~129} (Al-Attar, Crawford, Valentine and Trampert
  2018).
  \begin{itemize}
  \item With $P^1 = C^{\mathrm{eff}}\varepsilon - p_0(\Div\bu)I +
    p_0\,\mathrm D\bu^{\mathsf T}$, their continuous normal vector (with
$\varpi^0$ their symbol, unrelated to the mass form $\varpi$ of this
note),
    $\boldsymbol\tau = \mathbf t + \Div_\Sigma(\varpi^0\bu)\hat{\mathbf
    n} - \varpi^0(\nabla_\Sigma\bu)\cdot\hat{\mathbf n}$, is
    $C^{\mathrm{eff}}\varepsilon(\bu)\hat{\mathbf n}$ at constant
    $\varpi^0 = p_0$.
  \item Their interface condition is thus that the $C^{\mathrm{eff}}$
    traction be continuous and normal, which is the natural condition
    of the mixed class.
  \item The referential solid needs $\oint(\boldsymbol\tau - \mathbf
    t)\cdot\bv = \oint p_0[v_\perp\Div_\Sigma\bu - \nuhs\cdot
    \partial_{\bv}\bu]$, which is the follower.
  \end{itemize}
  Their eq.~92 makes $\varpi^0 = +p_0$, the pressure.
  \code{doc/gravitating\_elasticity.md} \S4 describes $\varpi^0$ as
  minus the equilibrium pressure, which looks like the opposite sign
  and is to be checked. Their gravity is the non-local Eulerian
  perturbation, with no interface terms, so this checks the follower
  only.
\item \emph{MA24} (Maitra and Al-Attar 2024, eqs.~139--144, 172--177,
  B9--B15).
  \begin{itemize}
  \item In the referential organisation gravity is a Piola stress,
    $T_g$ above at the identity, and their eq.~176 is the continuity of
    mechanical plus gravitational tractions.
  \item The gravitational traction is continuous automatically only
    when \emph{both} sides carry the referential gravity.
  \item Their remark after eq.~177 is that the stress terms can be
    converted to body forces and the tractions then removed. That is
    exactly what the gravity seam does on the solid side of an
    Eulerian fluid.
  \end{itemize}
  One caveat: their linearised fluid--solid integral (eqs.~B5--B6)
  drops the surface Jacobian. The omission is invisible at $\varphi_e =
  \mathrm{id}$, where the Jacobian is $1$ and the cofactor of $\nabla
  \varphi_e$ is $I$. It matters only for the mapped seam, which should
  carry the Nanson normal and the surface element explicitly, as the
  slip kernels do.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Beyond the identity map and the level wall.} Three cases
remain.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{A general pre-stressed solid (argued).} The gravity seam
  used only Poisson in the solid, never hydrostatics or the rheology.
  The pressure part is the fluid's follower $F_f$, a load whatever the
  solid's constitution. So for $(\hat C, \Se)$ at $\varphi_e =
  \mathrm{id}$ on a level wall with $\Se\nuhs = -p_0\nuhs$, the
  seam is $F_f$ plus the same gravity seam.
\item \emph{Transverse walls.} \eqref{eq:seam} holds as written. In the
  hydrostatic natural state a transverse wall is possible only where
  $\rho_S = \rho_F$ (\S\ref{sec:solid-asks}), so the mixed target
  exists only for level walls. With deviatoric pre-stress, $p_0$
  varies along $\Sigma$, the follower is no longer symmetric by itself,
  and the total is symmetric only when
  \begin{equation}
    \Se\,\bn = -p_0\,\bn \quad\text{on } \Sigma
    \label{eq:traction}
  \end{equation}
  (argued). That is, the solid's pre-stress must carry the fluid's
  normal traction and no shear traction on the wall. The
  equilibrium-stress generators of \code{background.hpp} take boundary
  tractions as data, so admissible $\Se$ for a non-level wall can be
  constructed, with the wall pressure as the datum. Relative to the
  level kernels, a transverse wall adds $(8\pi G)^{-1}\oint[v_\perp\,
  \mathbf g_0\cdot\partial_{\mathbf g_T}\bu + u_\perp\,\mathbf g_0\cdot
  \partial_{\mathbf g_T}\bv] - (4\pi G)^{-1}\oint[v_\perp\,\mathbf g_T\cdot
  \nabla_\Sigma(\zeta - \tfrac12\bu\cdot\mathbf g_0) + u_\perp\,\mathbf
  g_T\cdot\nabla_\Sigma(\chi - \tfrac12\bv\cdot\mathbf g_0)]$, with
  $\mathbf g_T = \mathbf P\mathbf g_0$. Its solid row also feels the
  pointwise wall pressure $-\oint_\Sigma f(\Phi_0(\bx))\,\bv\cdot\bn\dS$
  of the (S) row of \eqref{eq:Lv}.
\item \emph{The mapped variant}, $\varphi_e \ne \mathrm{id}$, is not
  derived. It needs the Nanson normal, $F_e^{-1}$ in the direction
  slot, the surface Jacobians, and the MA24 caveat above.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Consistency conditions}
\label{sec:solid-consistency}

Four conditions are required:
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] $\Phi_0$ is the total potential of the equilibrium
  configuration, the pre-stressed solid included;
\item[(ii)] the fluid is hydrostatic in it;
\item[(iii)] \eqref{eq:traction} holds on $\Sigma$;
\item[(iv)] the solid is otherwise arbitrary.
\end{enumerate}
Under (i)--(iii) the referential solid with the Eulerian fluid closure,
its border and the wall seam is a symmetric bordered system with the
variational structure of the conservative system of solid, fluid and
gravity: it is \eqref{eq:Lv} with $\Ltot_D$ replaced by the
referential functional plus the seam, in the hybrid variables. Two things are not yet written out term by term: the
follower's symmetry with a deviatoric $\Se$ on a transverse wall, and
the pull-back of the border and of the seam under $\varphi_e \ne
\mathrm{id}$.

%=====================================================================
\section{Rotation}
\label{sec:rotation}

\emph{Status: derived for the fluid; cited for the rotational feedback
and the generalised rotational theory; argued for their meeting with
the closure and for the inviscid limit (\S\ref{sec:rotating-limit});
measured on the mixed class (\S\ref{sec:implementation}).}

\paragraph{The background.} No fluid relation above used Poisson's
equation for $\Phi_0$. They used only \eqref{eq:hydro} and Poisson's
equation for the perturbation $\phi$. Take $\Phi_0$ to be the
\emph{geopotential}, gravitational plus centrifugal
($-\tfrac12|\bOmega\times\bx|^2$), with $g = |\nabla\Phi_0|$ the
effective gravity and $N^2$ and $D$ formed with it. Every step then
holds verbatim, and the fluid is foliated by the geopotential surfaces.
On a rotating hydrostatic figure the body is axisymmetric about
$\bOmega$, and the core--mantle boundary is a geopotential surface:
the level-wall case. The energy of \S\ref{sec:energy1d} is then the
energy of a body held at fixed angular velocity.

\paragraph{The perturbation.} In the library's rotational feedback, the
perturbation $\bomega$ of the rotation vector is an unknown,
determined by a secular angular-momentum balance
(\code{doc/generalised\_rotation.md}). Its equations are the
$\bomega$ rows below. A change $\bomega$ perturbs the centrifugal
potential by
\begin{equation*}
  \psi = -(\bOmega\times\bx)\cdot(\bomega\times\bx) = \sum_k\omega_k\psi_k ,
\end{equation*}
a known function once $\bomega$ is. $\psi$ joins $\phi$ everywhere in
the fluid:
\begin{itemize}
