\item $f = p_1 + \rho(\phi + \psi)$ and $\rho_1 = \rho'(\phi +
  \psi) - f'$;
\item the level geometry, $\delta\Vol = -\bar\Phi - \sum_k\omega_k\bar\Psi_k
  + S_c[\bu_s]$, and with it the lift $s = (\delta\Vol - V)/A$;
\item the level integrals become $\bar\Phi \to \bar\Phi +
  \sum_k\omega_k\bar\Psi_k$;
\item the wall pressure becomes $f - \rho_F(\phi + \psi +
  \bu_s\cdot\nabla\Phi_0)$.
\end{itemize}
Making that substitution in \eqref{eq:L} gives the border its
$\bomega$ columns:
\begin{itemize}
\item $-\int(\rho'\bar\Psi_kV/A + \rho\bar\Psi_kV'/A)\dc$ in the
  (V) row;
\item $-\int\rho'\bar\Phi\bar\Psi_k/A\dc$ in the (P) row;
\item an $\bomega$ block, $-\tfrac12\int\rho'\bar\Psi_j\bar\Psi_k/A\dc$.
\end{itemize}
By the symmetry of the functional, the $\bomega$ rows acquire the
transposes. This is how the border supplies the $\bomega$-row data of
the mixed description. That row is the fluid's inertia perturbation
$\int_F\rho_1(|\bx|^2\mathbf 1 - \bx\otimes\bx)\dV$, plus the
moving-wall term of $F3$, paired with $\bOmega$. The library computes it with
the machinery it uses for prescribed (tidal) potentials, treating each
$\psi_k$ as one. The border supplies the $-f'$ part of $\rho_1$ that
the reduction alone lacks.

\paragraph{A consequence for the reduction alone: length of day
(derived).} A change of rotation rate, $\bomega \parallel \bOmega$,
has $\psi = -\Omega\omega_3(x^2 + y^2)$, whose degree-0 part on the
sphere is $-\tfrac23\Omega\omega_3r^2$. The $\omega_3$ row of the
secular balance is $-C_3\omega_3 = \Omega I^{(1)}_{33}$, with $C_3$
the polar moment of inertia and $I^{(1)}_{33} = \int\rho_1(x^2 +
y^2)\dV$ the perturbation of the polar inertia. It reads the degree-0
part of $\rho_1$, and the feedback $\psi(\omega_3)$ loads degree $0$
in turn. With the reduction alone, the fluid's degree-0 response is
the member $f \equiv 0$ of \S\ref{sec:dahlen}. So the length-of-day
response of a fluid-core model, under a load with a degree-0 part, is
contaminated at the level of that member. Polar wander ($\bomega \perp
\bOmega$) is degree $2$ only and is clean. The closure removes the
contamination. This is checkable against the spectral reference with
no new theory (gate~2 of \S\ref{sec:verification}).

\paragraph{The generalised rotational theory.} The library implements
the rotational theory of Maitra and Al-Attar (2024) in a secular
reduction (\code{doc/generalised\_rotation.md}). That reduction keeps
three things:
\begin{itemize}
\item the inertia matrix of the rotational balance, built from the
  model's own equilibrium inertia tensor $I^{(0)}$;
\item the displacement pinned by the Tisserand gauge (zero relative
  angular momentum, which fixes the rigid rotation that the static
  problem leaves free);
\item $\bOmega$ along a principal axis of $I^{(0)}$.
\end{itemize}
With the closure all three survive unchanged. $I^{(0)}$ is whole-body,
the fluid's hydrostatic density included. The Tisserand gauge
restricted to the solid is forced rather than chosen: the eliminated
fluid has no displacement, and the slaved one's relative angular
momentum is relabelling gauge. $\bOmega$ along a principal axis is
automatic on an axisymmetric figure. The inertia perturbation is
Eulerian in the fluid, with the border and the moving-wall term of $F3$ added.

Two points want verification rather than argument.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{The gauge-shift certificate.}
  \code{doc/generalised\_rotation.md} certifies that $\bomega$ is
  invariant under a rigid re-gauge $\bu_s \to \bu_s + \mathbf b\times
  \bx$ of the displacement, the rotational row shifting by a
  closed-form commutator of the inertia tensor: under that re-gauge
  the reported $\bomega$ shifts by $\delta\bomega =
  D^{-1}[\mathrm{ad}_{\mathbf b}, I^{(0)}]\bOmega$, where
  $[\mathrm{ad}_{\mathbf b}, I^{(0)}]\bOmega = \mathbf
  b\times(I^{(0)}\bOmega) - I^{(0)}(\mathbf b\times\bOmega)$ and $D$
  is the matrix of the rotational row, so the shift vanishes for every
  $\mathbf b$ exactly when $I^{(0)} \propto \mathbf 1$. The
  certificate must be re-derived with the closure,
  because on an aspherical level wall the fluid is not inert under the
  re-gauge. Without rotation, the Eulerian fluid follows a rigid
  motion of the total mass exactly. $\rho_1 = -(\mathbf
  b\times\bx)\cdot\nabla\rho$ follows from $\rho'\phi$ with $\phi
  = -(\mathbf b\times\bx)\cdot\nabla\Phi_0$. On a level wall $u_\nu =
  -\phi/g = (\mathbf b\times\bx)\cdot\bn$, so the mismatch $a$ of \eqref{eq:sigma} vanishes and there is no
  sheet. A rigid rotation is therefore a null pair $(\bu_s, \phi)$ of
  the closure, a solution of the homogeneous system. With the
  centrifugal background it is a frame unknown instead, and the
  expectation is that the whole-body commutator formula survives.
\item \emph{The self-consistent option.} The library's self-consistent
  rotational option (\code{SetRotation(Omega)}, the ``self-consistent
  door'' of that note: model inertia plus Tisserand gauge) and the
  closure are made for each other. A rotating hydrostatic figure with
  a fluid core is where the model's own inertia is realistic. It is
  also where, at $N^2 \ne 0$, no static fluid method existed. The
  rapid-wobble regime, in which the core does not follow the mantle,
  remains outside the secular reduction as before.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{The inviscid rotating limit.} One hypothesis is tacit in
handing the closure a geopotential background. On a rotating body an
inviscid alternative to the rearranged hydrostatic end state exists,
with the fluid's density surfaces held off the geopotentials by
thermal-wind balance (Wunsch 1974). The closure computes the relaxed
state, which is the right one only when dissipation destroys the
geostrophic support within the loading time. The argument and the
timescales are in \S\ref{sec:rotating-limit}.

%=====================================================================
\part{Implementation, verification, standing}

\section{Implementation}
\label{sec:implementation}
\label{sec:impl}

\emph{Status: built and gated for the mixed class, in one and three
dimensions, for level walls (\S\ref{sec:verification}). The
referential variant is built through its level border, with the
cross-check and spectral gates of its first gate passed (the closing
paragraph); the mapped-state and pre-stressed gates remain. Transverse
walls, the tree closure and the connectedness check are specified, not
built.}

\paragraph{What a code assembles.} The bordered system \eqref{eq:Lv}
adds to the mixed solid--potential system one one-dimensional unknown
per connected fluid region, the function $V(c)$ on $[c_{\min},
c_{\mathrm{top}}]$, and one scalar per wall, the multiplier.
Everything the fluid contributes is assembled from four ingredients.
\begin{enumerate}
\item \emph{The level operator and its adjoint.} For a level function
  $q$ and a test function $\chi$ on $F$, $\int_Fq(\Phi_0(\bx))\chi(\bx)
  \dV = \int q\,\bar X\dc$. So if $V$ is expanded in basis functions
  $b_j(c)$ on the level interval, the coupling of the border to the
  potential is the matrix $\int_Fb_j(\Phi_0(\bx))\,\chi_i(\bx)\dV$, a
  volume integral over the fluid mesh with the basis composed with
  $\Phi_0$. No level surface is constructed. The same integral with
  $\chi \equiv 1$ gives $\int b_jA\dc$, which is how $A$ enters the
  mass-like terms; the stiffness $\int\kappa V'^2/A$ needs $A(c)$
  itself.
\item \emph{The constraint.} $S[\bu_s] = \oint_\Sigma\bu_s\cdot\bn\dS$
  is a surface integral on the wall; on a transverse wall $S_c[\bu_s] =
  \int_\Sigma H(c - \Phi_0)\,\bu_s\cdot\bn\dS$, again with no level
  curves. The constraint is one row and column; its multiplier is the
  wall pressure \eqref{eq:lambda-top}.
\item \emph{The border block.} The one-dimensional form of the (V) row
  on the chosen basis, piecewise smooth coefficients included; the end
  condition $V(c_{\min}) = 0$ is essential.
\item \emph{The wall terms.} $F2$ and $F3$ as in \S\ref{sec:dahlen}, on
  walls of either kind, with $\rho(\Phi_0(\bx))$ in place of $\rho_F$ on
  a transverse wall.
\end{enumerate}
\emph{Where the built discretisation differs.} The library's border
(\code{SetLevelBorder}, \code{level\_border.hpp}) realises these
ingredients with two differences of strategy, not of equation. First,
it carries the lift and the multiplier of the lift's definition as
unknowns, the built form \eqref{eq:L-built} below with unknowns
$(U, s, \nu)$ per region and one $\lambda$ per wall, rather than the
$(V, \lambda)$ form \eqref{eq:Lv}; eliminating $s$ and $\nu$
reproduces $J$ (\S\ref{sec:weak}). This is what makes the columns
local and the cost small. Second, the specification forms $A(c)$
by smoothed differentiation of the cumulative volume $\Vol(c) =
|\{\Phi_0 < c\}\cap F|$; the built border never forms $A(c)$
pointwise: it uses the histogram element volumes $\mathrm{Vol}_e$, the
element stiffness $\int_e\kappa\dV/\mathrm{Vol}_e^2 = \kappa_e/
\mathrm{Vol}_e$, and the $4/3$ centre factor in three dimensions, all
described below. \emph{The built discretisation is the
gated one} (\S\ref{sec:verification}); the smoothed-differentiation
route is specified, not built. Of the four ingredients, the
transverse-wall versions of 2 and 4 are not built.

\paragraph{The coordinate and the grid.} The border's unknowns are
functions of the level $c$ on $(c_{\min}, c_{\mathrm{top}})$. Their
natural coordinate is the volume fraction below a level,
\begin{equation*}
  \mathsf v = \frac{|\{\Phi_0 < c\}\cap F|}{|F|} = \frac{\Vol(c)}{|F|},
  \qquad
  \mathrm d\mathsf v = \frac{A\dc}{|F|},
  \qquad
  \int\frac{\kappa V'^2}{A}\dc = \frac{1}{|F|}\int\kappa\Bigl(\frac{\mathrm dV}{\mathrm d\mathsf v}\Bigr)^2\mathrm d\mathsf v .
\end{equation*}
In $\mathsf v$ the stiffness has no singularity at a centre, where $A
\to 0$, and $V(0) = 0$ is a plain essential condition. On the sphere
$\mathsf v = (r/r_\Sigma)^3$, and on the disc $(r/r_\Sigma)^2$, with
$r_\Sigma$ the radius of the wall. (This $\mathsf v$ is a coordinate
for the discretisation only; the coordinate in which the fluid's
arrangement is fixed is the mass coordinate $m$ of
\S\ref{sec:exactgraph}.)

As built (\code{SetLevelBorder}), the grid's nodes are uniform in the
level $c$, with hat functions linear in $c$. The volume fraction enters
through the element volumes. Each element's volume is the volume of
the fluid quadrature points whose $\Phi_0$ falls in it, so together the
element volumes are the cumulative histogram of $\Phi_0$ over the
fluid. The one-dimensional block uses element-mean coefficients
$\kappa_e$ and $\rho_e$ and the element volumes $\mathrm{Vol}_e$. With
nodal values $U_e$, $U_{e+1}$ at the ends of element $e$, the stiffness
$\int\kappa U'^2/A\dc$ becomes $\sum_e\kappa_e(U_{e+1} -
U_e)^2/\mathrm{Vol}_e$, with no $A$ anywhere. This is the $\mathsf v$-form
above with each element's $A$ replaced by its mean. The replacement is
exact except on a centre's first element in three dimensions, where $A
\propto \sqrt{c - c_{\min}}$ and the element mean of $1/A$ is $4/3$ of
$1/\langle A\rangle$; the stiffness there carries that factor. The grid
is coarse against the mesh's resolution of $\Phi_0$, so that every
one-dimensional element contains many quadrature points. An element
with none is refused.

\paragraph{The foliation and wall checks.} Assumption (A2a), (L1) of
\S\ref{sec:foliation}, is asserted at setup rather than relied on.
$|\nabla\Phi_0|$ must exceed $5\,\%$ of its maximum at every fluid
quadrature point outside the lowest $5\,\%$ of the level range, a range
that excludes a neighbourhood of a centre; otherwise the setup refuses.
(A2b), one connected component per level --- required only below the
band of a deformed level wall (\S\ref{sec:fluidatwall}) --- is
\emph{specified} but not yet checked. The specified check reads it off
the fluid element graph thresholded at a few values of $c$. Walls are
classified, per interface attribute, by the sign of the area integral
of $\nuhs\cdot\nabla\Phi_0$ as the region's outer (top) or inner wall,
and each is taken to be level at the area mean of $\Phi_0$ over it.
The setup also checks that a wall classified so
is level: the spread of $\Phi_0$ over each wall (its range over the
wall's quadrature points, reduced over the ranks) must not exceed a
fraction \code{wall\_level\_tol} of the region's level range, $5\,\%$
by default, and on a shell the inner wall's whole band of levels must
lie below the outer's, the band non-overlap of \S\ref{sec:foliation}
(the region's Reeb graph is then a single edge). Either failure refuses
the setup with the spreads printed, and the spreads are reported for
every region in any case (\code{LevelBorder::wall\_level\_min},
\code{wall\_level\_max}). Measured: the canned three-dimensional test
mesh, which is coarse, reports $3.9\,\%$ and $3.1\,\%$ of the level
range at its two walls; the canned two-dimensional annulus
$4\times10^{-5}$ and $1.4\times10^{-4}$; the $h = 0.3$ benchmark
meshes $0.4\,\%$. The tolerance is a guard against a misclassified or
genuinely transverse wall, not a resolution criterion. The built border therefore covers level
walls; the transverse-wall couplings --- the $S_c[\bu_s]$ data in $J$
and the distributed traction of the (S) row of \eqref{eq:Lv} --- and
the tree closure of \S\ref{sec:tree} are not assembled.

\paragraph{Every coupling is an ordinary integral.} By
\eqref{eq:coarea}, $\int\bar X(c)\,q(c)\dc = \int_F
q(\Phi_0(\bx))\,\chi(\bx)\dV$ for any one-dimensional $q$. The columns
of \eqref{eq:rows} are therefore of two kinds. Some are volume
integrals over the fluid elements, with the border's basis functions,
or their derivatives, composed with $\Phi_0$ as coefficients; these
are assembled where F1 is assembled. The others are wall integrals
with the same coefficients. The one-dimensional block is sparse.
The built border never needs $A(c)$ pointwise; where a computation
does, it is the same histogram differentiated.

\paragraph{Unknowns, as built (\code{SetLevelBorder}).} The mixed class
carries, per fluid region, the nodal unknowns $(U_k, s_k, \nu_k)$ on
the grid and one multiplier per level wall. They enter through the
Lagrangian
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
  L_{\mathrm{border}} ={}& \frac12\int\frac{\kappa U'^2}{A}\dc
  + \int\rho\,s\,U'\dc - \frac12\int\rho' A\,s^2\dc
  + \int\nu\,(sA + U + \bar\Phi)\dc \\
  &+ \sum_{\text{walls } j}\lambda_j\Bigl(U(c_j) - \oint_{\Sigma_j}\bu_s\cdot\nuh\dS\Bigr).
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:L-built}
\end{equation}
This is \eqref{eq:L} with the lift $s$ kept as an unknown, and with
$\nu = -f'$ as the multiplier of the lift's definition, $U =
-(\bar\Phi + sA)$ (\S\ref{sec:threelaws}). Its rows reproduce the closure, as
follows. The $\nu$-row restores the volume budget $sA + U + \bar\Phi
= 0$, that is \eqref{eq:Vdef}. The $s$-row is pointwise in $c$,
\begin{equation*}
  \rho\,U' - \rho' A\,s + \nu A = 0
  \qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
  \nu = \rho'\,s - \frac{\rho\,U'}{A} ,
\end{equation*}
which is $-f'$ by \eqref{eq:G-rho} with the dictionary density
\eqref{eq:V-dict}: the multiplier of the volume budget is minus the
derivative of the free function. The $U$-row, integrated by parts, is
$-(\kappa U'/A)' - (\rho s)' + \nu = 0$ in the interior; substituting
$\nu$ cancels the $\rho' s$ terms and leaves $(\kappa U'/A)' +
\rho\,s' + \rho\,U'/A = 0$, which is \eqref{eq:V-ode} once the
$\nu$-row's budget eliminates $s$, exactly as in \S\ref{sec:closure}.
The $U$-row's boundary terms give the wall multipliers directly: at
the outer wall, $\kappa U'(c_w)/A_w + \rho_F\,s(c_w) + \lambda = 0$,
that is $\lambda = -\kappa U'(c_w)/A_w - \rho_F\,s(c_w) = f(c_w)$ by
the compression law \eqref{eq:compression}, which is
\eqref{eq:lambda-top}. The Poisson row
receives $+\int\nu(\Phi_0)\,\chi\dV$, the extra density $-f'$. Each
wall's row receives the wall pressure $\lambda_j = \pm f(c_j)$, plus for
the outer wall and minus for an inner one. This multiplier is $f$
itself, not the combination of \eqref{eq:lambda}, because here the
rank-one term of \eqref{eq:L} is generated inside the border
(\S\ref{sec:weak}).

\emph{The discrete blocks (built).} On the grid of $n$ elements, with
hat functions $N_i(c)$, element width $h$, element volume
$\mathrm{Vol}_e$, element means $\kappa_e$, $\rho_e$, and $d_i = \pm1$
the sign of $N_i'$ on the element, the dense block $D_b$ of
\code{LevelBorder} (local ordering $U\,|\,s\,|\,\nu\,|\,\lambda$)
holds, element by element and summed:
$K_{UU} = (\kappa_e/\mathrm{Vol}_e)\,d_id_j$, times $4/3$ on a centre's
first element in three dimensions; $K_{Us} = \int\rho\,N_i'N_j\dc =
\rho_ed_i/2$; $K_{U\nu} = \int N_iN_j\dc$, the one-dimensional mass
matrix $(h/6)(2\,|\,1)$; $K_{ss} = -\int_F\rho'\,N_i(\Phi_0)N_j(\Phi_0)
\dV$; $K_{s\nu} = \int_F N_i(\Phi_0)N_j(\Phi_0)\dV$; and a unit entry
coupling each wall's $\lambda$ to $U$ at its wall node. At a centre
$U_0 = 0$ is removed. The coupling columns are the $\nu$ columns
$\int_FN_k(\Phi_0)\,\chi\dV$ on the potential's true dofs and one wall
column $-\oint(\nuhs\cdot\nabla\Phi_0/|\nabla\Phi_0|)(\nuhs\cdot\bv)\dS$
per wall on the displacement's, so that on a CMB the $\lambda$ row reads
$U(c_w) = S[\bu_s]$ and on an ICB $U(c_i) = -S_i[\bu_s]$, the end
conditions \eqref{eq:bcs} of \S\ref{sec:shell}. These are
\eqref{eq:L-built} term by term; eliminating $s$ and $\nu$ reproduces
$J$.

\emph{The energy in the lift (derived).} The first three terms of
\eqref{eq:L-built} have a direct physical reading. By the coarea
formula each one-dimensional integral is the volume integral of its
level-constant integrand, with $U' = A\Div\bu$:
\begin{equation*}
  \frac12\int\frac{\kappa U'^2}{A}\dc + \int\rho\,s\,U'\dc
  - \frac12\int\rho' A\,s^2\dc
  = \int_F\Bigl[\frac{\kappa(\Div\bu)^2}{2} + \rho\,s\Div\bu
     - \frac{\rho' s^2}{2}\Bigr]\dV .
\end{equation*}
Complete the square in $\Div\bu$, with $f = -(\kappa\Div\bu + \rho s)$
from dictionary step (b):
\begin{equation*}
  \frac{\kappa(\Div\bu)^2}{2} + \rho\,s\Div\bu - \frac{\rho' s^2}{2}
  = \frac{(\kappa\Div\bu + \rho s)^2}{2\kappa}
    - \Bigl(\frac{\rho^2}{\kappa} + \rho'\Bigr)\frac{s^2}{2}
  = \frac{f^2}{2\kappa} - \frac12 D\,s^2
  = \frac{f^2}{2\kappa}
     + \frac12\rho N^2\Bigl(\frac{s}{g}\Bigr)^2 .
\end{equation*}
The first part is the acoustic energy of the level pressure
perturbation $f$. The second is the buoyancy energy of lifting
material by $s/g$ across the stratification, against the
actual-minus-adiabatic gradient $D$ of \S\ref{sec:background}. The term
$-\tfrac12\int\rho' As^2$ is the in-situ part of that buoyancy
energy. The adiabatic part, $-\tfrac12(\rho^2/\kappa)s^2$, is completed
from the bulk and coupling terms. Integrated, this is the third term of
\eqref{eq:E2state}, the Eulerian form of the fluid's energy.

\paragraph{Why the cost is small.} The advantage of
\eqref{eq:L-built} over \eqref{eq:L} is locality. The elimination
engine (\code{bordered.hpp}) solves the bordered system by eliminating
the border. Each border unknown that couples to the three-dimensional
unknowns costs one solve with the three-dimensional operator, for its
column. Here the potential enters only through the $\nu$ constraint,
as the columns $\int N_k(\Phi_0)\chi\dV$. The solid enters only
through the wall columns $-\oint(\nuhs\cdot\nabla\Phi_0/
|\nabla\Phi_0|)(\nuhs\cdot\bv)\dS$. Every other border unknown has
a zero column, and the engine skips it. The cost is therefore one base
solve per $\nu_k$ and per wall, cached across solves at a frozen
operator.

The $\nu$ columns are assembled on the same quadrature points as the
one-dimensional block's volume integrals, so that the discrete mass
balance of the border is an identity. On a shell the inner wall
carries its own constraint and multiplier (\S\ref{sec:shell}). The
monolithic MINRES route (\code{SetMonolithicBorder}; the benchmark's
\code{-level-mono}) is available as an alternative to the elimination:
measured on the \code{fluid\_core} case it agrees to eight digits, its
one-off degree-0 solve is about $2.4\times$ cheaper than the
elimination's column setup ($2.9$\,s against $6.9$\,s at $h = 0.2$,
order 2, eight ranks), and every solve then costs $3$--$6\times$ the
naked iterations (the border scalars degrade the outer Krylov), so the
elimination wins from the second solve at a frozen operator: the
monolithic route is for one-off static problems. Refused:
\begin{itemize}
\item the Picard border route;
\item the KKT gauge;

\end{itemize}
The centrifugal coupling of \S\ref{sec:rotation} is wired on the mixed
class: in the built Lagrangian $\psi$ accompanies $\phi$, so each
$\nu$ row reads the level means of $\psi_k$ through the SAME pairing
as the potential --- the $\nu$ column against the $\psi_k$ interpolant,
the discrete $\int N_i(\Phi_0)\psi_k\dV$ --- and the $\bomega$ rows
gain the transposes by the symmetry of the functional: the
eliminated-$s$ columns and block of \S\ref{sec:rotation} reduce to
this one dense cross-block, cached with the rotation columns. Measured
on the canned fluid-core ball (order 1, Earth-like near-neutral
moments, uniform load): polar wander is border-inert to the discrete
foliation's $10^{-6}$, the two solve routes agree to $10^{-7}$ in the
fields and in $\bomega$, and the $\omega_3$ (length-of-day) response
shifts by $8.6\,\%$ when the border closes the degree-0 freedom ---
the contamination of \S\ref{sec:rotation}, measured; its adjudication
against a spectral reference is an open gate. The referential class
still refuses rotation with the glue: its $\bomega$ field columns are
not yet conjugated, and the generalised (Tisserand) door's composition
with the closure belongs to that step.

For a rotating figure the geopotential is passed as the background
potential.

\paragraph{The node count.} The grid resolves the profile, so too few
nodes under-resolve it. But each node's hat function is integrated
over the mesh's quadrature points, so a grid finer than the mesh's
resolution of $\Phi_0$ makes the columns noisy. On the
\code{fluid\_core} cases of \S\ref{sec:verification}, the answer drifts
with the count above about twenty nodes at $h = 0.3$ and $0.2$, and
twelve to sixteen nodes are right for those meshes. The default is
sixteen. A rule tied to the mesh (one node per few element-widths of
$\Phi_0$) is the natural refinement.

\paragraph{Cost.} The total is one symmetric solve plus the cached
column solves. There is no relaxation, no time-stepping, no fluid
displacement and no gauge penalty. The column solves are the whole
price: one base solve per level node (the $\nu$ column) and per level
wall. The operator, the preconditioner and the iteration counts of the
base solve are unchanged, and the condensed border system is dense but
of order the node count. Measured on the \code{fluid\_core} case of
\S\ref{sec:verify} at $h = 0.2$, order two, eight ranks:
\begin{itemize}
\item the degree-0 solve takes $0.54\,$s without the border, $7.5\,$s
  with sixteen nodes ($17$ columns, one per $\nu$ node and one for the wall: $18$ solves with the base solve), and $13.6\,$s with thirty-two;
\item the degree-1 and degree-2 solves that follow at the same operator
  take $0.5\,$s either way, the columns being cached.
\end{itemize}
In a Love-number sweep or an elastic time series the border is
therefore paid once. A stepper that changes the operator pays it once
per operator version. The engine keeps two versions, which covers a
stepper alternating between relaxed and unrelaxed operators. Should
the one-off cost matter, there are two levers: a
multiple-right-hand-side solve for the columns, which share the
operator, and the monolithic route.

\paragraph{Diagnostics (built).} \code{LevelBorderDiagnostics} reports,
per region, the border's $U$ at each wall against the wall flux, and the
discrete mass identity: the fluid's Eulerian mass perturbation
$\int_F(\rho'\phi + \nu)\dV$ against $-\rho_F$ times the outer flux
plus $\rho_F$ times the inner one, which mass conservation makes equal.
It does not report the multiplier identity or the energy identity of
\S\ref{sec:verification}. \code{LevelBorderFluidBlockMinEigenvalue}
(\code{LevelBorder::FluidBlockMinEigenvalue}) reports the smallest eigenvalue of the Schur complement of $D_b$
over $(s, \nu)$ at zero potential, restricted to the interior $U$ nodes
(the wall values being the solid's): the discrete $J[V]$ at
$\delta\Vol = 0$ with Dirichlet ends, the check of
\S\ref{sec:uniqueness}.

\paragraph{The referential variant (built at the natural state;
gate 1 passed).} In the referential class
(\S\ref{sec:solid}) the closure is realised by a \emph{discrete} change
of variables rather than by new integrators. The pieces, in order of
construction, are as follows.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] \emph{Eliminated fluid regions in the class.} The solid's
  SubMesh excludes them, and the fluid's density lives on the parent
  mesh, as in the mixed class. This step is built. The referential
  class takes the regions on the mixed class's pattern
  (\code{Treatment::Full} only, SubMesh mode) and validates them. It
  sources the background potential from the referential fluid
  densities on the parent. At a natural reference state its
  background problem reproduces the mixed class's to the two
  assemblies' common quadrature (\code{TestReferentialProblem},
  \code{FluidCoreBackgroundPotential}, serial and parallel).
  \code{Solve()} refuses a fluid problem until the terms of
  (ii)--(iv) are assembled.
\item[(ii)] \emph{The potential glue.} The fluid terms F1 and F3 and
  the fluid region's part of the Laplacian are assembled as the
  Eulerian forms, in the variables $(\bu, \phi)$, exactly as the mixed
  class assembles them. (F2, a displacement--displacement wall form,
  is unchanged by the conjugation below and belongs with the wall terms
  of step (iii).) They are then conjugated algebraically by
  \begin{equation*}
    T = \begin{bmatrix} I & 0\\ -S & I\end{bmatrix},
  \end{equation*}
  so that the referential system sees them in $(\bu, \zeta)$, with
  $\phi = \zeta - S\bu$ on the fluid region. Here $S$ is a sparse
  nodal glue. It maps the solid's displacement true dofs to the fluid
  region's potential dofs, and its image represents
  $(E\bu)\cdot\nabla\Phi_0$, with $E$ the prescribed radial fluid
  extension (\code{NewRadialFluidExtension}, at present one fluid core
  inside a solid shell). The potential unknown $\zeta$ stays
  continuous, and the Eulerian perturbation inside the fluid is $\phi
  = \zeta - S\bu$.

  The discrete $\nabla\Phi_0$ is discontinuous across element faces,
  so evaluating it at the nodes involves a choice. That choice is a
  gauge choice: it affects only the interpretation of $\zeta$ inside
  the fluid, not the physics, which sees $\phi$. This is the discrete
  counterpart of the change of variables of \S\ref{sec:solid}, and it
  has two advantages over writing that change of variables into
  continuous integrators. First, it needs no Hessian of the discrete
  $\Phi_0$. A continuous substitution would differentiate
  $(E\bu)\cdot\nabla\Phi_0$ inside the forms, and that Hessian is not
  defined across element faces; the algebraic conjugation uses only
  nodal values of $\nabla\Phi_0$. Second, it makes the
  fluid-plus-border physics digit-identical to the mixed class's. When
  the equilibrium mapping is not the identity, the Eulerian forms are
  pulled back with the Jacobians of \S\ref{sec:solid} before the
  conjugation (formulated, not built).
\item[(iii)] \emph{The wall terms: F2, the follower, and the gravity
  seam} (\S\ref{sec:seam}, \eqref{eq:seam-kernels}), all on the solid
  side of the wall:
  \begin{itemize}
  \item F2, the buoyancy of the wall;
  \item the follower, $\mathsf N[p_0] - \tfrac12(\mathsf G_P[p_0] +
    \mathsf G_P[p_0]^{\mathsf T})$;
  \item the gravity seam, $-\mathsf N[q] + \tfrac12(\mathsf G_A +
    \mathsf G_A^{\mathsf T}) + \mathsf G_s(\bv, \zeta -
    \tfrac12\bu\cdot\mathbf g_0) + \mathsf G_s(\bu, \chi -
    \tfrac12\bv\cdot\mathbf g_0)$.
  \end{itemize}
  Built. F2, the $\mathsf N$ kernel (a new
  integrator,
  \code{BoundaryNormal\-SurfaceDivergence\-Integrator}) and the
  pressure pair are solid-side boundary integrators. The gravity
  kernels are matrix-side folds in \code{SetFluidPotentialGlue}.
\item[(iv)] \emph{The level border}, unchanged in content. Built: the
  builder is shared by the two classes (\code{level\_border.hpp}), and
  the referential \code{SetLevelBorder} conjugates its $\nu$ columns by
  $T$ — a column $(b_u, b_\phi)$ becomes $(b_u - S^T b_\phi, b_\phi)$,
  the wall columns, already in $\bu_s$, unchanged — into the same
  bordered elimination.
\item[(v)] \emph{The consistency check} $\Se\bn = -p_0\bn$ on the
  wall, \eqref{eq:traction}.
\end{enumerate}
The gates, in order, are three.
\begin{enumerate}
\item The hydrostatic natural state, where the result must equal the
  mixed class's to solver tolerance. Since (ii) and (iv) reproduce the
  mixed class's fluid and border assembly exactly, this gate tests the
  glue $S$ and the solid-side terms alone: the referential solid and
  the wall terms of step (iii). In two dimensions it must use a
  zero-mean surface load, or else be run in three dimensions, where
  the DtN condition pins the potential constant. With an eliminated
  fluid the potential constant is not null, and the two classes
  project it out differently.

  \emph{Measured, passed at the common discretisation level} (the
  fluid and border assemblies are shared exactly, so "solver
  tolerance" holds for them by construction; the solid sides differ at
  quadrature). Without the border (\code{FluidCoreGlueCrossCheck2D},
  zero-mean degree-2 load): without the complete seam the displacement
  difference stagnates at $1.7\times10^{-1}$; with it, it converges
  from $3.5\times10^{-1}$ at order one to $2.0\times10^{-3}$ at order
  two. With the border (\code{FluidCoreLevelBorder3D}, uniform load,
  pure degree 0): $2.0\times10^{-2} \to 9.4\times10^{-4}$ in
  displacement and $1.2\times10^{-3} \to 6.6\times10^{-5}$ in the
  potential over orders one to two, serial and parallel. The spectral
  gate (\code{love\_benchmark -method referential -level-border 16},
  \code{fluid\_core} $h = 0.2$, order 2, against \code{pyslfp}):
  $h'_0 = -0.13148$ ($0.47\,\%$; the mixed class $0.45\,\%$),
  $h'_1$ to $0.06\,\%$, $k'_0 = -8\times10^{-5}$ against the naked
  $-5.2$. At degree 2 the wall kernels' facet geometry limits order 2
  to $0.94\,\%$, $h$-insensitively; at order 3 the error collapses to
  $0.007\,\%$ — the seam converges in order, not in $h$, on straight
  facets.
\item A relabelled sphere through the mapping, where the result must
  equal the unmapped one.
\item A pre-stressed aspherical solid.
\end{enumerate}
Status: the formulation is settled, and the wall seam is derived and
cross-checked (\S\ref{sec:seam}); steps (i)--(iv) are built and
tested, and gate 1 is passed, cross-checks and spectral reference
alike. What remains: the consistency check (v), gates 2 and 3, the
mapped pull-back of the seam and the border, and the referential
class's rotation columns (the conjugation noted above).

%=====================================================================
\section{Verification}
\label{sec:verify}
\label{sec:verification}

\emph{Status: measured where marked; the predictions of the theory
listed after the measured record are untested.}

Every number in this section is a measurement of this library. The
predictions of the theory are listed separately and marked
untested.

\paragraph{The one-dimensional prototype (measured).} The radial tool of
the two-dimensional disc family (\code{benchmarks/disc/disc\_radial.py},
method \code{dahlen} at degree $l = 0$) solves the degree-by-degree
problem of a disc with a fluid core. The load is a unit surface
traction, and the discretisation is spectral elements of order $p$.
The tool assembles \eqref{eq:rows} from the level-foliation equations:
\begin{itemize}
\item the border as $V = r\,u_f$ on the core nodes, per unit angle,
  $u_f$ being the fluid's degree-0 radial displacement;
\item the multiplier as a separate unknown;
\item F1--F3 kept, with their level means cancelled by the border's
  terms.
\end{itemize}
It compares the result with the welded compressible solve. At $l = 0$
the welded solve is exact physics: there is no along-level
displacement at degree $0$, hence no relabelling freedom and no
conflict at the wall (\S\ref{sec:why}). The comparison is made on three
cores:
\begin{itemize}
\item a uniform core ($N^2 < 0$, $D > 0$);
\item the neutral core ($N^2 = 0$, $D = 0$);
\item a stably stratified twin ($N^2 > 0$, $D < 0$): the neutral core
  with its bulk modulus doubled, which makes the adiabatic gradient
  shallower than the in-situ one.
\end{itemize}
Table~\ref{tab:prototype} gives the worst agreement over the three.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\caption{The one-dimensional prototype: the closure against the welded
compressible solve at degree $0$, worst case over the uniform, neutral
and stable cores. $p$ is the spectral-element order of the radial
tool; the last row is the change of the closure's answer as $p$ goes
from $8$ to $11$.}
\label{tab:prototype}
\begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule
quantity, closure against welded & worst of the three cores \\
\midrule
surface and CMB displacement & $10^{-11}$ \\
fluid degree-0 profile & $1.4\times10^{-11}$ \\
surface potential, through $\zeta = g\,u_r + \phi$ & $5\times10^{-12}$ \\
multiplier identity $\lambda = -\kappa\Div\bu + \rho_F\phi$ at the wall & $2\times10^{-12}$ \\
resolution drift of the closure, $p = 8 \to 11$ & $2\times10^{-12}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The reduction alone (F1--F3, no border) gives $u_r(a) = +3.29$ against
$-0.0991$ on the uniform core, and the same number on the neutral core
and its stable twin. That number is the member $f \equiv 0$ of
\S\ref{sec:dahlen}. It is blind to $\kappa$, and so to $D$, as
\eqref{eq:G-f} says it must be. These are the validation entries
``border l=0'' of the tool. The neutral-core line, the closure against
the welded compressible solve to $10^{-11}$, is the measured form of
the neutral-case agreement of \S\ref{sec:counting}. The multiplier the
prototype measures is that of the level-wall form \eqref{eq:rows}, in
which the rank-one term sits on the solid side, so its identity is
\eqref{eq:lambda}: $\lambda = f(c_w) - \rho_FS[\bu_s]/A_w$, which at
degree 0 in welded variables is $-\kappa\Div\bu + \rho_F\phi$ at the
wall. The pressure $f(c_w)$ itself, the multiplier of \eqref{eq:Lv} and
of the built border, is $-\kappa\Div\bu + \rho_F(\phi +
\bu\cdot\nabla\Phi_0)$ there.

\paragraph{The three-dimensional gate (measured).} The gate runs the
mixed class with \code{SetLevelBorder} on the \code{fluid\_core}
Love-number cases (\code{love\_benchmark -level-border}), at order 2.
The core is uniform, so $\rho' = 0$, F1 vanishes, and the closure's
mass balance is exact. The reference is \code{pyslfp}: $h'_0 =
-0.13210$, $k'_0 = 0$. \code{pyslfp} is a spectral code that solves
the radial equations degree by degree, with degree $0$ in the
displacement description, and it is the library's independent
reference. Table~\ref{tab:gate3d} gives the results.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\caption{The three-dimensional gate: the degree-0 load Love number
$h'_0$ of the \code{fluid\_core} model from the mixed class with the
level border, by number of level nodes and mesh size, at order 2,
against the \code{pyslfp} reference $h'_0 = -0.13210$. The last column
is the reduction alone (no border).}
\label{tab:gate3d}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccccc@{}}
\toprule
$h'_0$ by level nodes & 8 & 12 & 16 & 24 & 32 & reduction alone \\
\midrule
$h = 0.3$ (3733 elements) & $-0.1306$ & $-0.1309$ & $-0.1310$ & $-0.1326$ & $-0.1340$ & $-1.457$ \\
$h = 0.2$ (8183 elements) & $-0.1311$ & $-0.1314$ & $-0.1315$ & $-0.1328$ & $-0.1337$ & $-1.424$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

At sixteen nodes the error is $0.8\,\%$ at $h = 0.3$ and $0.45\,\%$ at
$h = 0.2$. That is the level at which degrees 1 and 2 themselves sit
on these meshes ($0.2$ to $0.7\,\%$), and it falls with $h$. $k'_0$ is
$-9\times10^{-7}$ and $-7\times10^{-7}$, against $-5.3$ and $-5.2$ for
the reduction alone: mass conservation to solver tolerance. The
degree-1 and degree-2 numbers with the border equal those without it
to $4\times10^{-5}$ at $h = 0.2$, the border being inert there. The
drift above twenty nodes is the column noise of
\S\ref{sec:implementation}, ``The node count''.

\paragraph{A fluid shell with two walls (measured).} The canned
three-layer test mesh has a fluid outer core between a solid mantle
and a solid inner core, so the fluid region is a shell with two level
walls (\S\ref{sec:shell}). Its density is stratified, and the fluid carries the
Adams--Williamson bulk modulus, so $N^2 = 0$ ($D = 0$) and the gauged
displacement formulation is exact there. Under a uniform load the
closure agrees with the gauged solution to $0.4\,\%$ in the potential
and $2.7\,\%$ in displacement at order 2, from $4.7\,\%$ and $13\,\%$ at
order 1 (\code{TestMixedProblemGauged}, the \code{LevelBorder}
cases). The reduction alone differs by more than $10^{-2}$ by
construction.

\paragraph{The fluid block's energy (measured).} The
probe \code{LevelBorderFluidBlockMinEigenvalue}
(\S\ref{sec:implementation}) tests the $D > 0$ statement of
\S\ref{sec:uniqueness} on the border's own grid
(\code{MixedProblemGauged.LevelBorderFluidBlockEnergy2D}: the canned
two-dimensional annulus with the Adams--Williamson core, order 2,
sixteen nodes, the bulk modulus scaled by $\kappa/\kappa_{\mathrm{AW}}$ so
that $D = (\rho^2/\kappa_{\mathrm{AW}})(\kappa_{\mathrm{AW}}/\kappa - 1) >
0$ below scale 1). At $D = 0$ the smallest eigenvalue is $0.52$ (the
largest $53.5$); it scales with $\kappa$ while the compression term
dominates ($0.051$ and $0.0048$ at scales $0.1$ and $0.01$), crosses
zero between $\kappa/\kappa_{\mathrm{AW}} = 10^{-3}$ and
$5\times10^{-4}$ ($3.3\times10^{-5}$ and $-3.4\times10^{-4}$), and the
negative part then grows with $D$ ($-7.3\times10^{-3}$ at $10^{-4}$):
coercive with a wide margin, and lost at small enough $\kappa$, as
\S\ref{sec:uniqueness} predicts. The gate is the sign at $D = 0$ and
the monotone fall; the numbers are the test's own printout (serial and
parallel suites).

\paragraph{Predicted (untested).} These follow from
the theory of Parts~I--III, and no computation has yet tested them.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item \emph{The energy identity.} At the solution, $\Ltot$ of
  \eqref{eq:Lv} equals half the work of the loads, and the multiplier
  equals $f(c_{\mathrm{top}}) = -\kappa V'/A - \rho_Fs$ at the top. In
  the built border the multiplier identity is the $U$ row at the wall
  node itself, and holds by construction; the non-trivial
  three-dimensional version is the solid's normal traction at the wall
  against the wall pressure \eqref{eq:wallp}, which is gate 1 below.
\item \emph{The near-wall signature of the welded solve.} At $N^2 \ne 0$
  and $\ell \ge 1$, the $L^2$ norm of the welded fluid displacement near
  the wall should grow like $h^{-1/2}$, and the across-wall integral of
  its tangential component should converge to $\bq =
  r^2\gradS a/\ell(\ell + 1)$ on the sphere. This turns the observed
  non-convergence of \S\ref{sec:why} into a quantitative check of
  \S\ref{sec:levelwalls}.
\item \emph{The neutral aspherical core.} On any geometry with $D \equiv
  0$ the welded compressible solve is exact (\S\ref{sec:levelwalls}), so
  it and the closure must agree off the sphere as they do on it. This is
  the cleanest aspherical test available, needing no spectral reference
  (gate 3 below).
\item \emph{A transverse wall.} The solid row must feel
  $-\int_\Sigma f(\Phi_0)\,\bv\cdot\bn\dS$ pointwise, the pressure
  profile must stay bounded at the dome top with $V(c_{\mathrm{top}}) =
  S[\bu_s]$ (Remark~\ref{rem:dome}), and the slipping class should
  converge away from the level points, with a band that shrinks as the
  wall becomes more transverse. This needs the transverse-wall couplings,
  which are not built.
\item \emph{Uniqueness at $D > 0$} on the uniform sphere: the minimum
  eigenvalue of the fluid block should be positive for Earth-like
  $\kappa$ and cross zero as $\kappa$ is lowered. The two-dimensional
  version is measured (above); the three-dimensional uniform
  \code{fluid\_core} sphere is untested.
\item \emph{Length of day.} The degree-0 contamination of the reduction
  alone, and its removal by the closure, against the spectral reference
  (\S\ref{sec:rotation}; gate 2 below). The $8.6\,\%$ shift is measured
  (\S\ref{sec:implementation}); its adjudication is not.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Gates.}
\begin{enumerate}
\item The slaved fluid trace against the solid's at the CMB on the
  sphere at $\ell = 0$, pointwise and not only in the level mean. And
  the $\ell \ge 1$ inertness at $10^{-10}$ rather than $10^{-5}$: the
  discrete level sets are spheres only to mesh accuracy.
\item The rotational measurement of \S\ref{sec:rotation}: the
  $\omega_3$ (length-of-day) response under a load with a degree-0
  part on a fluid-core model, with and without the border, against the
  spectral reference.
\item A neutral aspherical equilibrium figure, on which the closure
  must equal the welded and gauged answers (both exact at $N^2 = 0$).
\item A non-neutral figure: $h$- and $p$-convergence, and a
  first-order-in-flattening check against the spherical response.
\item The rotating figure with the border, against the input-inertia
  rotational option (\code{SetRotation(Omega, moments)} with the
  Tisserand gauge), with the sphere as control.
\end{enumerate}
The rotational gates (2 and 5) test the closure's internal consistency,
not the choice between the relaxed and the thermal-wind state of
\S\ref{sec:rotating-limit}.

%=====================================================================
\section{The method against the older literature}
\label{sec:literature}

\emph{Status: cited (the papers; collected in
\code{doc/FluidCorePapers}); argued (the comparisons, and
\S\ref{sec:rotating-limit}).}

The static deformation of a body with a fluid core has a literature of
its own, opened by Longman (1963) and essentially closed by Dahlen and
Fels (1978). (The pre-history is Takeuchi (1950), the first static
computation with a fluid core, made on an Adams--Williamson core,
where the conflict stays hidden: $D = 0$ identically, the one case in
which the welded description is sound (\S\ref{sec:counting}). Dahlen's
\S9 credits him with the equivalent of (62)--(63) there.) This section
places the method in it: paper by paper,
what each contains in this note's language, and where each stops or
errs. The verdict in one line: every \emph{interior} relation of
\S\ref{sec:lin}, and the whole wall picture of
\S\S\ref{sec:levelwalls}--\ref{sec:dissipative},
have spherical, degree-separated ancestors in these papers, while the
free function as a named per-level unknown, the per-level mass budget
and its differential equation, the bordered variational form,
everything off the sphere, and the nonlinear theory appear in none of
them. The literature also asks one further question,
developed in \S\ref{sec:rotating-limit}.
Table~\ref{tab:literature} is the summary.

\paragraph{Longman (1963).} The paradox, and the counting conflict,
are Longman's. For $n \ge 1$ his tangential balance is
\eqref{eq:p-slaved}; closing the welded description he observes that
zero dilatation forces ``$P = g_0U$'' --- the slaving --- ``and then
we find that the remaining degree of arbitrariness is insufficient to
satisfy our boundary conditions'': the counting of \S\ref{sec:why},
stated in 1963. He resolved it in the wrong direction. Reading the
conflict as a constraint on the Earth model rather than on the
description, he concluded that the Adams--Williamson condition ``is
really implicit in our assumption of elastic equilibrium'' and that a
uniform liquid core of finite compressibility is a physical
impossibility. By \S\ref{sec:why} the correct reading is that the
frozen-label welded description fails at $D \ne 0$ while the secular
state exists for any stratification; the \code{fluid\_core} model of
\S\ref{sec:verification} is exactly Longman's impossible core. Three
other things are his. The separate degree-0 solve in the displacement
description --- the spectral repair of \S\ref{sec:dahlen} ---
originates here. The particle indeterminacy is already stated (``the
precise motion of an individual particle is not determinate''). And he
records numerically that converting the Gutenberg core to
Adams--Williamson ``was found to have very little effect on the
results. For $n = 0$, however, it had a slight effect'': property (i)
of \S\ref{sec:dahlen}, and the degree-0 $\kappa$-sensitivity, measured
in 1963.

\paragraph{Jeffreys and Vicente (1966).} (Not held; recounted by
Dahlen 1974, \S9, and by Smylie and Mansinha.) They made the
overdetermination explicit degree by degree --- the counting of
\S\ref{sec:why} in spectral form --- and concluded that a non-neutral
core admits no static solution at all. Right about the welded
description; wrong that there is nothing to compute.

\paragraph{Smylie and Mansinha (1971).} The first correct $\ell \ge 1$
algorithm, and the origin of the sheet as a picture. (In the papers'
radial system $y_1$ is the radial displacement and $y_6$ the
gravity-flux scalar.) Facing Jeffreys
and Vicente's dilemma they drop the pointwise matching: ``we cannot
insist on continuity of $y_1$ at the core--mantle boundary. The mantle
may be allowed to project into the liquid core provided the prevailing
total hydrostatic pressure of the core is assumed at its base.'' That
is the broken normal trace (in the reading of \S\ref{sec:levelwalls}
an apparent jump, between the wall and the equipotential $\Gamma$, that
belongs to the regular part of the displacement), the fill-to-the-wall
traction, and --- as ``a redistribution of superficial core material
(but no net change in core volume) necessary to fit the deformed core
into the deformed shell'', drawn in their Fig.~3 --- the zero-net-mass
redistribution that $\sigma$ of \S\ref{sec:levelwalls} measures:
Figure~\ref{fig:gamma}, in 1971. All of it is asserted rather than
derived; Dahlen (1974) calls the procedure correct but ad hoc. Two
defects. Their $y_6$ is taken continuous, which is inconsistent with
the moving-wall term $F3$; Chinnery (1975) corrects it. And their
inertia perturbation is computed from a Lagrangian core field with the
tangential displacement set to zero, which is not an admissible
representative: the along-level surface divergence is fixed by
$\Div\bu$ and $u_\nu$ (\S\ref{sec:realise}), so the truncated field's
implied density is not \eqref{eq:G-rho}. Dahlen put the resulting
error below ten per cent. The border's $\bomega$ columns
(\S\ref{sec:rotation}) supply the same term consistently.

\paragraph{Pekeris and Accad (1972).} The dynamical ancestor of the
wall analysis. Their stability parameter $\beta$ is a positive
multiple of $D$, with the same trichotomy, and their opening sections
demolish Longman's necessity claim with the uniform liquid sphere. For
an unstable core ($\beta > 0$, which includes the uniform compressible
core) they show that as the forcing frequency $\sigma \to 0$ (the
papers' $\sigma$, a frequency, not the sheet density of
\S\ref{sec:wall}) the
dilatation and radial stress vanish through the core \emph{except in a
boundary layer below the CMB of thickness proportional to $\sigma$},
which ``has a finite dynamic effect even though its thickness
vanishes'': the limiting interior is the slaved field, the layer's
residue is the wall conditions --- F2 and F3 at $f = 0$ --- and the
stress and displacement become discontinuous at the wall. That is the
wall layer of \S\ref{sec:dissipative}, reached through the
\emph{inviscid} dynamics at finite frequency: a further completion,
independent of every dissipative mechanism, collapsing to the same
wall terms. In the reading of \S\ref{sec:levelwalls} the apparent
displacement discontinuity in the limit is the jump $a$ between the
wall and $\Gamma$, carried by the regular part, with a tangential
transport concentrated in the collapsed layer. Their Table~1 also shows quantitatively how wrong a naive
static assembly is --- the published ancestor of the degree-0 numbers
of \S\ref{sec:dahlen}. For a stable core their own asymptotics shows
the $\sigma \to 0$ limit does not exist pointwise: the undertone
resonances accumulate at zero frequency, their ``infrared
catastrophe''. Yet their summary claims the yielding determinate ``in
all cases''. The claim overreaches exactly where the band of
\S\ref{sec:why} lives: discarding the oscillatory part is legitimate
only once dissipation damps it, as they half-concede.

\paragraph{Wunsch (1974).} The cleanest statement of what kind of
problem this is. On plane half-space models he shows that the static
limit of the ideal stratified fluid is singular because $\sigma$
multiplies the highest derivative; that ``if one insists upon
examining the static limit, one is forced into a paradox''; and that
the resolution is ``treating a real, rather than idealized, fluid''
--- density diffusion, viscosity, or ``an arbitrarily small
rigidity''. The two limits, $\sigma \to 0$ and dissipation $\to 0$, do
not commute. \S\ref{sec:why} is this thesis made constructive, and the
gauge-class status of a realising displacement
(\S\ref{sec:realise}) answers the unease he records about the purely
Eulerian reading. His third case, the rotating fluid, is the question
of \S\ref{sec:rotating-limit}.

\paragraph{Wunsch (1975, part II).} The dissipative half of the
programme, on the same half-spaces (Boussinesq, no gravity in the
solid, as he flags himself). The central result: with viscosity and
density diffusion, for $\sigma$ small against the diffusive decay rate
the thermal wind ``has time to dissipate \ldots\ the fluid comes to
rest'', and ``the appropriate boundary conditions for the static
displacement of an elastic mantle overlying a fluid core are no
stress, no slip, and continuity of vertical displacement'' --- the
relaxed state. (The no-stress statement is the Boussinesq, gravity-free
version: the relaxed fluid exerts only the hydrostatic stress of the
rearranged state, which in the self-gravitating problem is the
Lagrangian wall traction of \eqref{eq:lambda}; nothing to port.) In
the opposite ordering the thermal wind survives, supports a normal
stress, and changes the mantle response at order one --- a factor of
two in his worked example --- and he places even the fortnightly tide
possibly on that side. Two further things. His Lamb argument: an
interface particle can never leave the interface, so the end state is
reached by \emph{diffusive label change at the wall}, with the normal
displacement continuous --- ``Any conclusion at the static limit
\ldots\ that depends upon a discontinuity in vertical displacement
should be viewed with scepticism, for no real fluid-solid system will
behave that way'' --- a second route to the end state, distinct from
the lateral creep of \S\ref{sec:dissipative}. Whether it reaches the
same Eulerian state depends on
whether label diffusion changes the inventory at first order near the
wall, and this note leaves that open (\S\ref{sec:standing}). His
caution itself is borne out by Proposition~\ref{prop:layer}: the
realising fields are welded at every stage, and no conclusion here
depends on a discontinuity in normal displacement.
And the magnetic taxonomy: a poloidal (wall-normal) field acts like
rotation, supporting the unrelaxed state through a magneto-thermal
wind that only Ohmic diffusion removes; a toroidal field acts like
stratification, making the ideal limit singular. His close is the
honest one: the real core needs ``a series of problems depending upon
the precise parameter ranges''.

\paragraph{Dahlen (1974).} The first derivation of the elimination,
checked here term by term: his eq.~(60) is \eqref{eq:p-slaved}, (61)
is \eqref{eq:rho-slaved}, his CMB conditions (63) carry exactly the
content of F1--F3, his (66) is the strict relabelling class, and his
(68) is the slaving, ``every particle must remain on the equipotential
surface on which it originates''. His \S8 contains the degree-0
physics in words --- the volume change, the particles leaving their
equipotentials --- and solves it after Longman, in the displacement
description; \S\ref{sec:dahlen}'s reading of the reduction and of the
repair is accurate against the paper. What is not there: any per-level
condition, any single formulation covering all degrees at once,
anything aspherical, and any selection argument for the static problem
itself, which came four years later with Fels. His \S9 remains the
best contemporary history of the controversy.

\paragraph{Chinnery (1975).} The Lagrangian counterpart, on the
sphere. His four families of surfaces --- equipotential,
equal-density, isobaric, material --- are forced to coincide for $n
\ge 1$ in a stratified core, giving zero dilatation and zero
Lagrangian pressure and density change: the slaving, derived
materially. At Adams--Williamson ``there is no way to define
unambiguously a `material surface' within a homogeneous core'': the
degeneration at $D = 0$ and the onset of class~K, seen in the one
variable his formalism carries. His CMB conditions correct Smylie and
Mansinha's $y_6$, and his jump constant $K_1$ enters the gravity-flux
condition as exactly the term that $F3$ adds to the slaved field, the
$\oint\sigma\phi$ of Remark~\ref{rem:energy}, as bookkeeping without
the physical reading. His degree-0 treatment --- a
material variable, continuous with $y_1$, closed by ``the conservation
of mass condition'' --- is the sphere's \eqref{eq:G-band}. Absent: the
free function (forced to zero before he starts at $n \ge 1$, changed
away from at $n = 0$), any sign of $D$, any variational structure, and
any argument that the static problem is the right one, which he leaves
explicitly to the fluid dynamicists.

\paragraph{Crossley and Gubbins (1975).} Four pages, and the closest
ancestor of the wall analysis. The interior is the reduction, derived
independently of Dahlen from the material argument that a
non-neutrally-stratified element in equilibrium must keep its pressure
surface. The wall is the real content: a static elastic transition
layer, rigidity $\epsilon\mu_s$ with $\epsilon \to 0$, inside which
the tangential displacement grows unboundedly, so that the pill-box
argument for continuity of $y_6$ fails --- the box acquires ``a finite
contribution from the walls'' --- and the $y_6$ jump, $F3$'s
booking in the potential row, is \emph{derived} rather than asserted:
the tangential transport of Proposition~\ref{prop:layer}, seen from
inside an elastic layer.
Their closing criticism of Dahlen's pointwise no-penetration, that the
linearisation ``is therefore invalid for particle displacements and
particles are not required to remain on the boundary'', is the
statement that \S\ref{sec:levelwalls} sharpens into the level mean of
the normal matching, \eqref{eq:wall}. One caution if cited: the $\epsilon \to 0$ limit is
formal. By \S\ref{sec:why} the welded problem at $N^2 \ne 0$ has no
solution, and their construction evades this only because the interior
is already the reduced hydrostatic fluid, the elastic material
surviving in the layer alone. It is a static completion selecting the
same booking, not a convergence proof for the welded limit.

\paragraph{Dahlen and Fels (1978).} The mechanism, and the paper
Figure~\ref{fig:sheet} descends from. On a stratified Boussinesq box
with Rayleigh friction and Newtonian cooling (a diffusion of the
stratifying quantity), they solve the step-load evolution in closed
form and show: the final static Eulerian state follows ``simply from
the condition that the final static state must be one of hydrostatic
equilibrium'', independently of the dissipation --- credited jointly
to Dahlen (1974) and Crossley and Gubbins (1975); the zero-dissipation
limit is a singular perturbation (the identification is Wunsch's), an
essential singularity whose origin is the accumulation of the
gravitational mode spectrum at zero frequency; the final displacement
has a wall layer of thickness $\delta \approx (lN\tau)^{-1}$ in which
the motion is predominantly tangential, $\xi/\zeta \sim N\tau$ --- the
lateral creep of \S\ref{sec:dissipative}, the transport of
Proposition~\ref{prop:layer}; the linearisation requires the
displacement amplitude to be small against $\delta$ --- the
two-lengths separation of Figure~\ref{fig:sheet}, and in the form
$\delta \gg (\varepsilon L)^{1/2}$ the condition of
\S\ref{sec:notsmall}; the end state is
reached by label change, not frozen-label statics; and for $N^2 < 0$
no final state exists, the modes being underdamped. They state the
thesis of \S\ref{sec:why} outright: every static problem is ``the long
time limit of the corresponding transient excitation problem, with
dissipation'', which is ``the only way a real static deformation can
ever arise in nature''. They also correct Dahlen (1974) on his own
indeterminacy: no transient mantle forcing excites the steady toroidal
flows, so the catalogue of them answers no physical question --- the
dissipative-path remark of \S\ref{sec:realise}, in their words. Two
things are not there: the clock at fixed viscosity (their layer is a
fixed-width feature of the $t \to \infty$ state at finite $\tau$,
collapsing only as $\tau \to \infty$; Figure~\ref{fig:sheet} also
thins it in $t$), and the Eulerian booking --- no moving-wall term in a
potential row, no zero-net-mass statement, their box having no
potential row for it to live in.

\centering\small

\label{tab:literature}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.19\textwidth}p{0.42\textwidth}p{0.3\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule

Longman 1963 & the counting conflict; the slaving; the degree-0

derived; $y_6$ wrong; inertia booked on an inadmissible representative \\
Pekeris--Accad 1972 & $\beta \propto D$ and its trichotomy; the slaved

& formal $\epsilon \to 0$; $n \ge 1$ only; ``hydrostatic after'' a

\paragraph{What is new here.} The comparison leaves the following as
this note's content, none of it in the papers above.
\begin{itemize}
\item The free function $f$ as a named per-level unknown covering
  degree 0 and the aspherical case at once, and the proof that no
  local relation can fix it (\S\ref{sec:general}).
\item The per-level mass budget $\Delta M(c) = 0$ and its reduction to
  the regular, self-adjoint equation \eqref{eq:V-ode} for $V$,
  dividing by neither $D$ nor $N^2$. The old debate treats
  Adams--Williamson as a case split --- mandatory (Longman),
  indeterminate (Chinnery), expansion invalid (Pekeris and Accad); the
  closure needs none.
\item The wall condition \eqref{eq:wall}, the multiplier-as-wall-pressure
  identity \eqref{eq:lambda}, and the symmetric bordered weak form
  \eqref{eq:L}: one assembly for all degrees, closing inside the
  three-dimensional problem the split that Longman opened and every
  spectral code since has kept.
\item Everything off the sphere. Every paper above is spherical and
  degree-separated (Wunsch's half-spaces aside); the claim of the
  opening paragraphs stands against this folder.
\item The sheet $\sigma$ as the measure of the wall against the
  reference equipotential $\Gamma$, with zero net mass by the fill
  condition, and its identification in displacement language as a
  tangential transport $\bq$ with $\divS\bq = -a$
  (Lemma~\ref{lem:q}, Proposition~\ref{prop:layer}): the derived
  content of Smylie and Mansinha's Fig.~3, Chinnery's $K_1$
  bookkeeping, Crossley and Gubbins's pill-box flux and Dahlen and
  Fels's tangential layer, with the state itself carrying only the
  moving-wall term $F3$.
\item The exact theory: the inventory, the Reeb-graph reduction to
  profiles in the mass coordinate, the arrangement and the quantile
  function, and the closure as the Euler--Lagrange equation of the
  fluid's energy, with the null-Lagrangian identity \eqref{eq:nulllag}
  as the reason it is $D$-free.
\item The quotient state space, and the admissibility of reconstructed
  displacements --- which diagnoses Smylie and Mansinha's inertia
  error in one line.
\item The variational structure, the uniqueness statements (a theorem
  for $D \le 0$, a check for $D > 0$), the saddle
  continuation at $N^2 < 0$ (Dahlen and Fels stop at ``physically
  meaningless''), and the nonlinear rearrangement theory of
  \S\ref{sec:nonlinear}.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Citation debts.} The body re-derives, in sharper form,
results it should cite at the point of use. The insertions owed are:
Longman (1963) and Jeffreys and Vicente (1966) at the counting
conflict of \S\ref{sec:why}; Wunsch (1974) and Dahlen and Fels (1978)
at the dissipation paragraphs of \S\ref{sec:why} and at
Figure~\ref{fig:sheet}; Smylie and Mansinha (1971), Chinnery (1975)
and Crossley and Gubbins (1975) at the sheet and its booking
(\S\ref{sec:dahlen}, \S\ref{sec:wall}); Pekeris and Accad (1972) at
the band and, with Longman's numerical remark, at property (i)'s
$\kappa$-blindness; and Crossley and Gubbins (1975) at the level-mean
reading of the normal matching. They are recorded here pending review.

\subsection{The rotating inviscid limit}
\label{sec:rotating-limit}

\emph{Status: argued. This is the one question the literature raises
beyond the method's own, and it is physical, not formal.}

\paragraph{Wunsch's third case.} For a plane stratified half-space
under a boundary undulation of wavenumber $k$ and frequency $\sigma$,
the interior response decays with depth on the scale
\begin{equation*}
  \frac{1}{|k|}\sqrt{\frac{f^2 - \sigma^2}{N^2 - \sigma^2}},
\end{equation*}
with $f$ the Coriolis parameter (his notation; in this subsection
only, $f$ is not the free function). Without rotation the scale vanishes
as $\sigma \to 0$: the collapsing wall layer of
\S\ref{sec:dissipative}, the singular limit. With rotation it tends to
$f/(|k|N)$, finite: ``the limit $\sigma \to 0$ is well behaved. The
lines of constant density can be \ldots\ maintained in a static
deformation following the deformed solid.'' The supporting mechanism
is thermal-wind balance: the density surfaces are held off the
equipotentials by the Coriolis force of a steady flow along them.
Rotation, which is not a dissipation and selects nothing by
relaxation, regularises the static limit all the same --- and the
state it preserves is \emph{not} the rearranged hydrostatic one.

\paragraph{Why the counting changes.} The conflict of \S\ref{sec:why}
rests on the static momentum balance \eqref{eq:momentum}, whose
along-level part forces $p_1 + \rho\phi$ to be constant on each level.
A rotating fluid at zero frequency need not be at rest. A steady
velocity $\bv$ costs no inertia but exerts the Coriolis force, and the
along-level balance becomes
\begin{equation*}
  \nabla_{\!\parallel}\bigl(p_1 + \rho\phi\bigr)
  = -2\rho\,(\bOmega\times\bv)_\parallel .
\end{equation*}
One steady vector field is far more freedom than the wall mismatch
consumes: the slaving chain of \S\ref{sec:general} breaks at its first
link, the free function is no longer a function of the level alone,
and the frozen-label state survives, supported indefinitely by the
circulation. The over-determination of \S\ref{sec:why} is an
\emph{irrotational} statement.

\paragraph{The orderings.} Three rates compete: the loading rate
$\sigma$, the rotation $\Omega$, and the rate at which dissipation
destroys geostrophically supported configurations.
\begin{itemize}
\item Dissipation fast against $1/\sigma$: the circulation decays, the
  fluid rearranges, and the end state is the secular state of
  \S\ref{sec:why} --- the closure's state.
\item $\sigma \ll \Omega$ with dissipation slow against $1/\sigma$:
  the thermal-wind state, which the closure does not compute.
\end{itemize}
For the core at GIA periods, $1/\sigma \sim 10^2$--$10^4\,$yr and
$\sigma \ll \Omega$ trivially, so everything turns on the decay time
of the supported configuration --- and the candidate mechanisms spread
over many orders of magnitude. Wunsch's part II criterion on the
half-space is direct diffusion on the wavelength scale, $\sigma \ll
\nu k^2$: at degree-2 core scale with molecular $\nu \approx
10^{-6}\,$m$^2\,$s$^{-1}$ that is $\sim 10^{11}\,$yr, which taken
alone would put GIA deep in the thermal-wind regime. A sphere has
faster routes the half-space lacks: Ekman pumping, $\tau_E \sim
L/\sqrt{\nu\Omega}$, gives $\sim 10^4\,$yr at molecular viscosity,
marginal against the loading time; Ohmic decay of core-scale fields
acts on $\sim 10^5$--$10^6\,$yr; any eddy viscosity shortens all of
these. The magnetic field cuts both ways: Wunsch's poloidal case is a
field \emph{supporting} the unrelaxed state through a magneto-thermal
wind, relaxed only by its own Ohmic decay, not a relaxing agent. The
stake is order one --- in his worked example the geostrophically
supported mantle response differs from the relaxed one by a factor of
two, and he places even the fortnightly tide possibly on the supported
side. The adjudication is core physics --- the same physics that sets
the actual damping of the undertones --- not numerics, and it is not
settled here; Wunsch's own close is that the real core needs ``a
series of problems depending upon the precise parameter ranges''.

\paragraph{What this means for the closure.} Three statements.
\begin{itemize}
\item The secular-state claim of \S\ref{sec:why} carries, on a
  rotating body, a tacit hypothesis: the dissipative completion must
  also destroy the geostrophic support. ``Dissipation of any kind''
  remains right; that rotation is not one of the kinds needs saying,
  and the hypothesis should be stated where \S\ref{sec:rotation} hands
  the closure its geopotential.
\item The rotational gates (2 and 5 of \S\ref{sec:verification}) test
  the closure's internal consistency, not this choice: a spectral
  reference built on the same hydrostatic-rearrangement hypothesis
  cannot adjudicate between the relaxed and the thermal-wind state.
\item Computing the thermal-wind end state would be a different
  problem --- a steady rotating-stratified flow coupled to the elastic
  mantle --- and nothing in the library computes it. The rapid-wobble
  exclusion of \S\ref{sec:rotation} fenced the fast end of the secular
  reduction; this is the fence at the slow end. Wunsch's part II
  settles the half-space version in both orderings; what remains open
  is the sphere's timescales.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{A third completion: small rigidity (programme).} Wunsch's
list of regularisations includes ``an arbitrarily small rigidity'',
and Crossley and Gubbins's transition layer is its wall-local form.
Whether the zero-rigidity static limit of the welded problem agrees
with the dissipative secular state is examined nowhere: the band
measurements of \S\ref{sec:why} are at zero rigidity exactly. On the
sphere at $\ell \ge 1$ Crossley and Gubbins's layer says the two agree
in the jumps. Off the sphere and at degree 0 the question is open, and
the library can test it cheaply: the welded class with a small uniform
rigidity in the core, swept down, against the closure.


%=====================================================================
\section{Standing of the method, assumptions, and open items}
\label{sec:standing}
\label{sec:open}

\paragraph{Standing.} The theory of Parts~I--II is derived under
(A1)--(A5), with the exceptions marked in the status lines. The closure
reduces to Dahlen's reduction at $\ell \ge 1$ and to the compressible
degree-0 problem at $\ell = 0$ on a sphere, both derived here and both
measured (\S\ref{sec:verification}). Off the sphere it is, to our
knowledge, the only static method for a fluid region whose
stratification is not neutral; its aspherical verification beyond the
neutral case rests on the gates of \S\ref{sec:verification}. For static
problems with hydrostatic fluid regions the closure, once those gates
are passed, replaces the Maxwell relaxation of
\code{doc/static\_fluid\_core.tex}. It is one symmetric solve for the
secular state on any geometry, any stratification and any number of
regions. It consults the fluid's bulk modulus exactly where the physics
does, in the level-mean sector, and nowhere else.

Two limits stand. First, the closure does not touch $\ell \ge 1$ on the
sphere, where it is Dahlen's method. Whether Dahlen's degree-1 answer
or the welded one is the secular state on a stably stratified core is
decided by the adjudication recorded in
\code{doc/static\_fluid\_core.tex} --- Dahlen against the welded cluster,
with the Maxwell route's escalated limit joining Dahlen at
$1.2\times10^{-3}$ on the stratified disc --- not here. Second, the
closure is static. The relaxation remains the only route to the
transient, and the dynamic check of the closure's answer. The
gauge-penalty and slipping formulations are instruments, kept for
comparison and testing, and their standing is unchanged.

\paragraph{Standing assumptions.} Collected from the text, the
hypotheses under which the method is claimed:
\begin{itemize}
\item (A1) one conserved label with $\partial_S\rho$ of one sign and
  $\kappa > 0$ (\S\ref{sec:setting});
\item (A2a) no critical points of $\Phi_0$ in the fluid except a centre,
  checked at setup; (A2b) connected levels, required only below the band
  of a deformed level wall, and otherwise replaced by the tree
  (\S\S\ref{sec:foliation}, \ref{sec:tree}, \ref{sec:fluidatwall});
\item (A3) walls level or transverse with isolated nondegenerate level
  points; the built border covers level walls only, and checks at setup
  that each wall is level to a tolerance and, on a shell, that the
  walls' bands of levels are disjoint (\S\ref{sec:implementation});
\item (A4) the perturbed equilibrium keeps the background's arrangement
  in the mass coordinate (\S\ref{sec:A4}); on a tree the background must
  also supply the division of the inventory among pockets
  (\S\ref{sec:arrangement});
\item (A5) regularity;
\item the inventory is fixed over the loading time: the secular state is
  the end state of advective relaxation (\S\ref{sec:dissipative});
\item on a rotating body, dissipation destroys the geostrophic support
  within the loading time, so that the relaxed state, not the
  thermal-wind state, is the one computed (\S\ref{sec:rotating-limit});
\item for $D > 0$ somewhere, the fluid block has no zero eigenvalue
  (\S\ref{sec:uniqueness}), and the answer is the continuation of the
  stable family (Remark~\ref{rem:attainability});
\item for the referential solid, the consistency conditions of
  \S\ref{sec:solid-consistency}, in particular \eqref{eq:traction}.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Open items.}
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{The finite-amplitude problem on the tree}, where the
  arrangement among pockets changes only at combinatorial events (a
  pocket draining, a saddle rising past a top), and the foliation
  question at finite amplitude generally.
\item \emph{Walls with level patches.} A patch carries no fill-type
  condition of its own, since transport can leave it through its
  transverse edge, but the coupling is not written out.
\item \emph{Two independent labels} (\S\ref{sec:inventory}), which break
  the reduction to profiles.
\item \emph{Label diffusion at the wall.} Whether label diffusion
  changes the inventory at first order near the wall, on which the
  equivalence of the diffusive completion with the advective one
  depends (\S\ref{sec:dissipative}).
\item \emph{Proofs of the wall analysis}: the level-point behaviour on
  transverse walls and the compatibility condition for the along-level
  field there (\S\ref{sec:transverse}), and the finite-amplitude band's
  linear limit (\S\ref{sec:levelwalls}), which are argued.
\item \emph{Uniqueness of the exact equilibrium} for a given geometry and
  arrangement, which the functional \eqref{eq:Ev} does not settle
  (\S\ref{sec:NL-variational}); the compressible energy-minimisation
  statement selecting the stable arrangement (\S\ref{sec:arrangement});
  and the existence of the secular state for a stratified fluid in a
  deformable container, where the whole-fluid and solid-only cases exist
  in the literature but their coupling does not.
\item \emph{The parameter-dependence step} in the fibred Moser
  converse, Proposition~\ref{prop:moser}.
\item \emph{Coercivity of the coupled bordered form off the sphere},
  argued by continuity, not proved and not measured directly
  (\S\ref{sec:uniqueness}).
\item \emph{The rotational theory with the closure}: the gauge-shift
  certificate with the Eulerian fluid on an aspherical level wall, and
  the self-consistent rotational option (\S\ref{sec:rotation}); the
  referential class's rotation columns are not wired.
\item \emph{The mapped wall seam}, $\varphi_e \ne \mathrm{id}$ (the
  Nanson normal, the surface Jacobians, the MA24 caveat), the pull-back
  of the border under $\varphi_e \ne \mathrm{id}$, and the follower's
  symmetry with a deviatoric $\Se$ on a transverse wall
  (\S\ref{sec:seam}).
\item \emph{The rotating inviscid limit}: the regime adjudication of
  \S\ref{sec:rotating-limit}. Wunsch's part II settles the half-space in
  both orderings; the sphere's timescales (Ekman pumping, Ohmic decay,
  eddy viscosity) are the open physics.
\item \emph{The small-rigidity completion}: whether the welded problem's
  zero-rigidity limit is the secular state (\S\ref{sec:rotating-limit},
  closing remark).
\end{itemize}

  equation $\nabla^2 u = -1$ in a convex region, Math. Notes 9 (1971).
\item A.~U. Kennington, Power concavity and boundary value problems,
  Indiana Univ. Math. J. 34 (1985) 687--704; Theorem 4.2 as restated
  in K. Ishige and P. Salani, Parabolic power concavity and parabolic
  boundary value problems, Math. Ann. 358 (2014), Theorem 1.1.
\item L. Caffarelli and J. Spruck, Convexity properties of solutions
  to some classical variational problems, Comm. PDE 7 (1982)
  1337--1379 (the convex-ring theorem; also R. Gabriel 1957 and
  J. Lewis 1977 for the harmonic and $p$-harmonic cases).
\item F. Hamel, N. Nadirashvili and Y. Sire, Convexity of level sets
  for elliptic problems in convex domains or convex rings: two
  counterexamples, Amer. J. Math. 138 (2016) 499--527.
\item B. Gidas, W.-M. Ni and L. Nirenberg, Symmetry and related
  properties via the maximum principle, Comm. Math. Phys. 68 (1979).
\item B. Kawohl, Rearrangements and convexity of level sets in PDE,
  Lecture Notes in Mathematics 1150, Springer (1985).
\end{itemize}

\section*{References cited for the nonlinear theory}

\begin{itemize}\small
\item G.~R. Burton, Rearrangements of functions, maximization of
  convex functionals, and vortex rings, Math. Ann. 276 (1987)
  225--253, and the steady-flow papers that followed.
\item J.~F.~G. Auchmuty and R. Beals, Variational solutions of some
  nonlinear free boundary problems, Arch. Rational Mech. Anal. 43
  (1971) 255--271 (rotating self-gravitating fluids as energy
  minimisers).
\item E.~H. Lieb and H.-T. Yau, The Chandrasekhar theory of stellar
  collapse as the limit of quantum mechanics, Comm. Math. Phys. 112
  (1987) 147--174 (the non-rotating minimisation, in its classical
  part).
\item T.~B. Benjamin, The alliance of practical and analytical
  insights into the nonlinear problems of fluid mechanics, Lecture
  Notes in Mathematics 503 (1976) 8--29 (steady flows as extremisers
  over rearrangements).
\end{itemize}


\section*{References cited for the level structure and the exact theory}

\begin{itemize}\small
\item Sard (1942); Milnor, \emph{Morse Theory} (1963); Borodzik, N\'emethi
  and Ranicki, \emph{Morse theory for manifolds with boundary} (Algebr.\
  Geom.\ Topol., 2016); Reeb (1946); Carr, Snoeyink and Axen,
  \emph{Computing contour trees in all dimensions} (2003);
  Cole-McLaughlin et al., \emph{Loops in Reeb graphs of 2-manifolds}
  (2003); Moser, \emph{On the volume elements on a manifold} (1965);
  Lorenz (1955) and Winters et al.\ (1995) for the sorted reference
  state; Hardy, Littlewood and P\'olya, \emph{Inequalities}, for the
  rearrangement inequality. Full bibliographic details are not yet
  checked.
\end{itemize}
